<meta>This page begins the commentary of John of Saxony on the "Introduction to the Art of Judgments of the Stars" by the 10th-century astronomer Alchabitius. While the previous page concluded technical instructions for mundane astrology (predicting market prices), this page serves as a philosophical preface, justifying the practice of astrology by arguing that a wise person can use its predictions to mitigate fate.</meta>

# Commentary of John of Saxony on the text of Alchabitius.

<image-desc>A large woodcut decorative initial letter 'V' featuring scrolling foliate and floral patterns in a square border.</image-desc>

**"A wise man shall rule over the stars."** <note>original: "Vir sapiens dominabitur astris." This was one of the most famous proverbs in medieval astrology, suggesting that while the stars incline the will, they do not compel it.</note> Ptolemy says this in the *Aphorisms* of the Almagest. <note>The *Almagest* is Ptolemy's great 2nd-century work on astronomy, though this specific quote is traditionally associated with the *Centiloquium*, a collection of 100 astrological sayings attributed to him.</note> And it can be explained in this way: He shall rule the stars who is able to hinder or prevent the effects proceeding from those very stars; but a wise man is able to do this; therefore [the conclusion follows]. The **major premise** is obvious: for he rules another who can say to him "do this" and he does it, or "leave this" and he leaves it. The **minor premise** is proven by the authority of Ptolemy in the fifth proposition of the *Centiloquium* <note>A "centiloquy" is a collection of 100 sayings; this specific work was a cornerstone of medieval astrological education.</note> where he says: "The best astrologer can prevent much evil that is to come according to the stars when he has foreknown its nature. For in this way he will forewarn the person to whom the evil is to come, so that when it arrives, they may be able to endure it." And it is confirmed by the authority of the same author in the eighth proposition of that same work, where he says: "A wise soul will assist the work of the stars just as a sower assists natural strengths."

But we must consider the manner through which a wise man can hinder or assist the work of the stars. It is certain that we cannot simply [absolutely] stop celestial influence any more than we can stop a fire from burning; however, we can prepare the **subject** <note>In this context, the "subject" or "patient" is the person or thing receiving the astrological influence.</note> to receive that celestial influence in one way or another. For we see that the same heat of the sun dissolves ice yet hardens mud. **Haly** <note>Ali ibn Ridwan, an 11th-century Egyptian physician and astrologer whose commentary on Ptolemy was standard reading in medieval universities.</note> provides a familiar example of this in his commentary on the previously mentioned proposition, "The best astrologer, etc.," saying: "If we know that a hot and dry sickness <note>This refers to the theory of the four humors; a sickness caused by Mars was believed to be excessively 'hot' and 'dry,' like a fever.</note> of the nature of Mars is about to happen to someone, we can change that person—before the arrival of that influence—to the opposite state: namely, to coldness and moisture. Thus, the influence which should have made him sick will instead restore him to a balanced temperament."

It is also clear to anyone that we can hinder the action of fire so that it does not burn a specific combustible thing—namely, by removing the object from the fire or by throwing water upon it. Since, therefore, the sciences are divided according to the division of the things they study, and any person is called "wise" in their own science—taking "wisdom" broadly to mean "knowledge," as **Albumazar** <note>Abu Ma'shar, a 9th-century Persian scholar and one of the most influential astrologers in the Middle Ages.</note> does in many places in his *Introductory*—let us see which "wise men" these words refer to. We can distinguish between different types of wise men in a universal way.

Among the number of the wise, some are truly wise, while others are wise only in appearance. **Aristotle** touches upon this distinction in the first book of his *Sophistical Refutations* <note>original: "elenchorum." This is Aristotle's treatise on logical fallacies.</note> where he says: "Some are beautiful because of their actual beauty, while others fake it by dressing themselves up." And these—those wise in appearance—are split into two groups. Some of them appear wise only according to the estimation of the common people, like the rich of this world; others appear wise to the learned, such as the **sophists**. <note>A sophist is someone who uses clever but false arguments.</note> The first are those possessing the riches of this world, of whom Albumazar speaks in his *Great Introductory* when he discusses the sects that oppose astronomy. These are those of the "new sect." Albumazar says there: "He who has more substance [wealth] is more worthy in the eyes of the general public; and so it happens because the value of wealth in their eyes is greater than the value of knowledge." And they say that when...

<summary>This page introduces John of Saxony’s commentary on Alchabitius, using the famous "wise man shall rule the stars" proverb to argue that astrology allows for free will and preventative medicine. It establishes the philosophical legitimacy of the craft by citing authorities like Ptolemy, Aristotle, and Albumazar.</summary>
<keywords>John of Saxony, Alchabitius, Ptolemy, astrology vs free will, humoral theory, Mars, Albumazar, Aristotle, scholasticism, medical astrology</keywords>

--- Page 57 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical preface by John of Saxony. Having established the central proverb "A wise man shall rule over the stars," he now discusses the specific intellectual and moral qualities required of a true astronomer, distinguishing them from "sophists" and those who merely study the movements of the stars without understanding their influence on the world.</meta>

If a man has wealth, it does not hinder him if he is unskilled in the science of the stars, and of medicine, and of other sciences. This is a most false proof: when they judge knowledge through wealth. And this proof of error is a most false proof: because a thing should be proven through its own kind—that is, through what is similar. For example, wisdom is proven through wisdom, and substance <note>In this context, "substance" refers to material wealth or social standing.</note> through substance; and they are not proven through what is not similar to them. Therefore, wisdom is not proven by wealth. Others appear to be knowing or wise among those who are somewhat advanced, such as the sophists, of whom Aristotle says in the first book of the *Sophistical Refutations*: "for some it is more worthwhile to seem wise and not to be, than to be wise and not to seem so." <note>original: "magis opere pretium est videri et non existere quam existere et non videri"</note> And Master Alan of Lille, speaking of them, says:

> Poor lawyers visit his schools;
> they stir up true disputes; they become combatants.
> This one proves, that one disproves; this one shuts down that one.
> And two hundred sophists shout with one voice.

Those who are truly knowledgeable differ according to more or less certainty, as the Commentator <note>The "Commentator" refers to Averroes, the 12th-century Islamic philosopher whose commentaries on Aristotle were foundational to medieval European universities.</note> says in the second book of the *Metaphysics*. The mathematical sciences are in the first degree of certainty. The natural sciences follow them. Those who are less certain—namely, the logicians and natural philosophers who do not move on to the other sciences—possess nothing of philosophy but words. Of these also, Master Alan says:

> This one always cries out and argues,
> while he probes the Aristotelian secrets.
> But if you ask how or upon what his mind feasts,
> he lives by study, but he philosophizes in the old way.

The mathematicians are more certain. And they are divided into two groups: for some of them study only the movements of the stars and do not care for judgments <note>term: judgments - the practice of judicial astrology, or predicting how the positions of the stars will affect earthly events and human lives.</note>, or they even deny them. These are the ones of whom Albumasar <note>Albumasar (787–886 AD) was a leading Persian astronomer whose works on astrology were highly influential in the Latin West.</note> speaks in the fourth and fifth sects of those who speak against judgments, as will be seen later. The second group, however, studies both movements and judgments; of these Alan says, speaking of Albumasar himself:

> There Albumasar consults the stars, the poles,
> the heaven, and the seven planets, and brings back
> their counsel to the lands. Arming the lands
> and strengthening what is fleeting against the celestial rages
> and the fury of the gods above.

Of these, Ptolemy says the words previously proposed: **"A wise man shall rule over the stars."** <note>original: "Vir sapiens dominabitur astris."</note> In these words, Ptolemy touches upon three properties or conditions which a true philosopher, and especially an astronomer, ought to have, and by which three kinds of men are repelled from astronomy. The **first condition** is stability of intention. The **second** is suitability of disposition. The **third** is the renunciation of earthly possessions. He touches upon the first when he says "man" [*vir*]; the second when he says "wise" [*sapiens*]; the third when he says "shall rule over the stars" [*dominabitur astris*].

Regarding the **first condition**, Ptolemy says in the first proposition of the *Centiloquium*: "The science of the stars is from you and from them." <note>This implies that astrological knowledge requires both the data from the stars and the intellectual effort of the observer.</note> And Haly, in his commentary on the same, says: "He who desires to know future things must proceed by two paths. One, that he accepts the movement of the stars and the effects that happen in things according to their movement. And he should look at the books which the ancients wrote concerning the significance of their movements; and join to these whatever he has proven in his own time." Since, therefore, there is great labor in observing the movements of the stars, an astronomer cannot be effeminate <note>In the medieval sense, "effeminate" (effeminatus) implies being soft, fickle, or lacking the "masculine" vigor required for hard mental labor.</note> or lazy. And since much time is required to study the books of the ancients, he cannot be a wanderer. It is necessary, therefore, that an astronomer be firm and stable in meditation. And thus the weak and the lazy are repelled from astronomy.

Regarding the **second condition**, Ptolemy says in the seventh proposition of the *Centiloquium*: "No one can give certain judgments according to the temperament of the stars, except a man who has well understood the power of the soul and the natural temperament." He intends, therefore, that a man must be naturally disposed toward this science if he is to profit in it. In my opinion, this science holds this requirement above all other sciences. For I have seen good scholars in logic and in natural philosophy who could in no way grasp anything of astronomy—indeed, not even basic arithmetic. <note>term: algorismum - arithmetic or calculations using Arabic numerals, which was still a specialized skill in the late Middle Ages.</note> It is clear, therefore, that those who are naturally undisposed are repelled from astronomy; of whom Albumasar describes a sect, as will be seen immediately.

Regarding the **third condition**, Haly says in the commentary on the first proposition of the *Centiloquium*—

<summary>John of Saxony outlines the intellectual and moral requirements for an astronomer, arguing that true astrology requires a stable mind, natural aptitude, and a focus beyond material wealth. He critiques "sophists" and those who only study celestial mathematics without understanding their earthly effects.</summary>
<keywords>astrology vs astronomy, Albumasar, Ptolemy, Centiloquium, Alan of Lille, scholasticism, qualifications of a philosopher, Averroes, mathematical certainty</keywords>

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the necessary conditions for a student of astronomy. Having previously addressed wealth and social status, the author now examines the seventh condition: the detachment from worldly concerns and the relative impact of errors in this field compared to other sciences like medicine and navigation.</meta>

Seventh. Because we must be anxious about worldly things: it takes away from us the knowledge of future things; but if we were separated from all worldly things, we would be able to foreknow the future. This is just as we see in the case of hermits, many of whom predict future things. And certain epileptics, while they are tormented by epilepsy, predict the future; for then they do not use the senses of the body but only the power of the soul. Therefore, a father of philosophers <note>Referring to the ideal seeker of wisdom</note> should not be wealthy, according to the Philosopher <note>The standard medieval title for Aristotle.</note> in the seventh book of the *Politics*. It is not necessary for the philosopher to be the master of land and sea: but it suffices that he has a servant providing him with vegetables. And thus, those possessing the riches of this world are driven away from astronomy. Observing this, Socrates threw his gold into the sea, as Valerius <note>Valerius Maximus, a 1st-century Roman author known for his collection of historical anecdotes.</note> recounts. This condition does not please many; nor does it even please me.

Regarding these conditions, Albumasar <note>Abu Ma'shar (787–886 AD), a Persian astrologer whose *Introduction to Astronomy* was a fundamental text for medieval European scholars.</note> consoles us in his *Introductory*: and especially regarding the last condition, because he changes it to its opposite. He says thus:

> And perhaps some are not sufficient for the wisdom of this mastery while they look to arrive at the truth and comprehension of certain things from them, so that they might be able to repel error from themselves through the brevity of their practice in the knowledge of all things; the knowledge of which is necessary for them in that same intention: regarding the first condition.

And with a few things interposed, he says:

> And perhaps many subtle men of profound wisdom are not sufficient for those who use them to understand them, since the science of this mastery and of other masteries—which are necessary in the presence of things through their depth which we say is far from the senses—regarding the second condition.

Then he consoles us and says:

> That which can be reached through its science should not be nullified because of that which cannot be reached.

And with a few things interposed, he says that a little bit of wisdom yields much progress. And in this, he changes the third condition to the opposite. And he proves that there is greater utility in astronomy than in any other science. And if error happens to occur in it, it is more tolerable and does less harm than in other sciences. And he says thus:

> For we see doctors err in the foreknowledge of infirmities and diseases while they predict things that happen to the sick person from the gravity of the disease and its lightness, and from the speed of health and its slowness: also regarding his health or death; but that small amount of error by doctors does not hinder them from the desire for their mastery and from its arrangement and practice: nor does the general reproach or doubt of men toward them—who have seen from a small error in their mastery—prevent them from seeking utility and progress through their cure and from acquiescing to their medicines. Similarly, the directors of ships do not abandon their mastery, nor do men abandon the course of the sea because of a small error of the sailors. Similarly, the authors of other wisdoms and masteries are scarcely able to escape the accidents of error or the arrival of some impediment that happens to them; but because of this, their mastery is not nullified. And of all these things we have mentioned, the error of the impediment is more than the error of the astrologers: for when a doctor has erred in the foreknowledge of infirmities, or in the cure, or in the medicines, perhaps this will be the cause of the loss of souls. <note>In this context, "souls" likely refers to "lives" or "vital spirits."</note> And when sailors have erred, perhaps this will be the cause of the loss of ships and their shipwreck and the death of those who are in them. And if the shepherds and authors of the perception of animals <note>Those who study animal husbandry or veterinary medicine.</note> have erred, perhaps this will be a provocation of the loss of that same kind of animal. But indeed, if astrologers have erred, this is attributed more to the ignorance of the astrologer in the foreknowledge of the matter: for the inquirer omits to take precautions against horrible things before they come; and because...

<summary>This page argues that while worldly distractions hinder prophetic insight, the study of astronomy remains vital despite the possibility of human error. It compares the risks of astrology favorably against the fatal consequences of errors in medicine or navigation.</summary>
<keywords>astronomy vs. astrology, Albumasar, Socrates, Aristotle's Politics, medical error, navigation, ethics of science, detachment</keywords>

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues the defense of the "mastery of the stars" (astrology) by comparing it to other disciplines. It shifts from the personal requirements of the practitioner to a formal academic defense against critics, drawing heavily on the authority of Ptolemy, Albumasar, and Aristotle.</meta>

<sig>d ij</sig>

Perhaps this will be for them <note>The practitioners of other arts or the critics previously mentioned.</note> a dismissal of caution, a provocation of those same horrible things arriving, and perhaps there will be loss in them, and perhaps not. In other masteries <note>Latin: *magisteriis*; refers to specific fields of study or professional arts.</note>, however, it is not so: because the greatest part of the error of those authors is loss and hindrance without a doubt. And in this also what we said concerning the dignity and the honors of the science of the stars: if therefore the mastery of the stars is worthier and nobler than other masteries—and the path of its mastery in error at certain hours is less significant than the path of other masteries, being both sounder and less of a hindrance—then their truth is of greater profit. Wherefore it is fair, as much as becomes all intellectual and wise men, to receive astrologers, to employ them, and to acquiesce to their truth in those things which they say.

But this science, especially that part which concerns judgments <note>term: <term>Judgments</term> (Latin: *iudicijs*); in this context, it refers to judicial astrology, the practice of making specific predictions about future events or human affairs based on planetary positions.</note>, has many rivals and adversaries. Noticing this, Ptolemy in his *Quadripartitum* <note>original: "quadripartito"; also known as the *Tetrabiblos*, the foundational 2nd-century text on astrology.</note> confirms the judgments of the stars, as does Albumasar in his *Great Introduction* <note>Albumasar (787–886 AD) was a Persian astrologer whose works were central to the medieval European understanding of the cosmos.</note>. Both agree in their manner of confirmation, except that Albumasar posits more points than Ptolemy and employs a greater number of reasons. 

Both, in confirming the validity of judgments, begin with the work of the sun. For Ptolemy says in the thirteenth proposition of the first part: "Since the sun works with the air in all things existing on the earth." And Haly <note>Likely Haly Abenragel or Haly ibn Ridwan, famous commentators on Ptolemy.</note> in his commentary, explaining the words of Ptolemy, says that Ptolemy wants to show us that the sphere of fire and air, which is changed by the celestial bodies, changes all things that are on the lands. And Haly, in the commentary on the second proposition of the same work, says that the roots of this science are so manifest that even common people, knowing nothing of theory, know and understand them simply by looking at and experiencing them. 

And Ptolemy in his 21st proposition says that even ignorant commoners know things before they happen. And what is more, he himself says that many animals know things before they happen <note>A reference to natural instincts or "weather wisdom" in animals as a form of natural astrology.</note>. Albumasar also confirms the science of judgments through many reasons, all of which would be very long to narrate now. Specifically, however, Albumasar disputes against those who deny the judgments of the stars, and he divides them into ten sects <note>Groups or schools of thought.</note>. I shall briefly touch upon two or three words regarding each sect.

¶ The **First Sect** says that the planets have no significance over things that occur in this world, which is "below the circle of the moon" <note>The "sublunary" world; the Earthly realm where things are subject to change and decay, unlike the eternal heavens.</note>. To these people, Albumasar says that all philosophers have agreed that every substance which is moved by a natural motion effects natural conversions in the essences of things joined to it by nature; and he declares this through the action of fire. But since the celestial bodies are moved by natural motion, they therefore effect natural conversions in the four elements <note>Earth, Air, Fire, and Water.</note> joined to them by nature. Albumasar implies this reasoning with many words. One might also adopt against that sect the saying of Aristotle in the first book of the *Meteorology*: "It is necessary for the lower world to be contiguous with the superior motions, so that its whole power may be governed from there." <note>original: "Necesse est mundũ inferiorem contiguũ esse lationibꝰ superioũbꝰ: vt tota eius virtus inde guber netur."</note>

¶ The **Second Sect** is composed of those who said that the planets only have significance over universal things, such as the four elements, or genera and species <note>For example, they might influence "the weather" or "mankind" generally, but not a specific person.</note>. Against these, Albumasar posits, among other things, this reason: The whole is not the whole except through its parts; therefore, if they signify over the whole—namely over the species—it is necessary that they signify over the individuals which are the parts of that species.

¶ The **Third Sect** consists of certain disputants who said that the planets have no significance over what is "possible," but only over what is "necessary" or "impossible." <note>Meaning astrology could only predict things that must happen by law of nature, not things that might or might not happen through chance or will.</note> Albumasar says that the Philosopher <note>Aristotle.</note> dissolves their arguments and affirmed the existence of the "possible" through many reasons. Among others, he posits that which Aristotle posits in the first book of *On Interpretation* <note>original: "periarmenias" (Greek: *Peri Hermeneias*).</note>...

<summary>This page defends astrology against critics by citing authorities like Ptolemy, Albumasar, and Aristotle, arguing that celestial bodies naturally influence the sublunary world. It begins refuting ten "sects" of skeptics, specifically those who deny planetary influence on individuals or on "possible" future events.</summary>

<keywords>astrology, Ptolemy, Albumasar, Aristotle, Haly, sublunary world, four elements, Quadripartitum, judicial astrology, natural philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the defense of astrology against specific "sects" or schools of skeptical thought. The previous page ended while discussing the third sect; this page concludes that thought and addresses the fourth, fifth, and sixth sects, primarily using the arguments of Albumasar (Abu Ma'shar).</meta>

...blames, namely, that one ought not to give counsel or aid <note>The text concludes a point from the previous page regarding the moral or practical objections of a third group of critics.</note>. ¶ **The fourth sect** consists of those who have looked into the "science of the whole" <note>original: *scientia totius*; this refers to the study of the celestial spheres and the universal laws of the heavens.</note>, which is the science of the celestial circles and their existence. These people said that the planets have no significance over the things that are made and happen in this world regarding individual animals, seeds, and metals. They claim that their significance is only over the corruption or the change of the seasons. And Albumasar says that these people were unable to deny this much: namely, that the change of seasons arises from the strength of the planets' work, because this is found and appears through their operation, and to reject it would be a disgrace. He then argues against them based on what they have already conceded.

¶ **The fifth sect** is composed of those who looked into the science of the whole but nullified the "science of judgments" <term>Judgments (Latin: *iudicia*): The branch of astrology focused on predicting specific events or effects on Earth.</term> on the grounds of experimental evidence. They said that the judgments of astronomy are not true because things are perceived through experiments; and the minimum requirement for the truth of a thing to be perceived through experiments is when it is found to be the same twice. They argue this is impossible regarding the planets: for when a planet is in a certain place among the signs, and the other planets are looking at it <note>This refers to "aspects," or specific geometric angles between planets.</note> or are joined to it, they do not return to that same state among the signs except after many thousands of years. A single human life does not reach such a great quantity of years; how then is it possible for a human to find the planets twice in the same state so that it might be proven by their return to those locations what they signify? To these people, Albumasar says that the ancients knew the natures of the planets and their significations from many and diverse things, some of which are "particular and apparent," while others are "universal." 

Particular apparent things are like what is found from the operation of the Sun in heating and the Moon in moisture and decay, and from the operations of the planets in the corruption and change of the air every day and night. Universal things, however, are like what is found from their significance in the "revolution of the years of the world" <note>A technique for predicting the events of a coming year based on the moment the Sun enters Aries.</note> and in nativities <note>Individual birth charts.</note>. In short, the essence of his words is that a human can, in a short time, experience those things which occur commonly; but as for other things which happen rarely—such as "great conjunctions" <note>Rare alignments of Jupiter and Saturn that medieval thinkers believed signaled major historical shifts.</note>—the sayings of our predecessors ought to suffice for us. 

¶ **The sixth sect** is that of certain arithmeticians who were too lazy to look at the number of the stars and their equations <note>The mathematical corrections required to find a planet's true position.</note> from the book in which the science of the whole is most certain: namely, the *Almagest* which Ptolemy produced. Instead, they sought the locations of the planets through various tables, and they found that the tables differed regarding the locations of the planets. Therefore, they said that the judgments are not true since they do not have the true locations of the planets. To them, Albumasar says that when an astronomer wishes to judge any revolution, he ought to consider this: that he looks at the natures of the planets and their properties, the lord of each sign and its "exaltation," and the "lords of its triplicity" <note>Technical terms for different types of planetary dignity or strength in a specific zodiac sign.</note>. Also, he considers its place in the "angle" and the "succeeding" houses and its "falling" from the angle <note>Refers to the twelve astrological houses: Angles are the strongest (1st, 4th, 7th, 10th), Succedents follow them, and Cadents ("falling") are the weakest.</note>, and its presence in the "house of substance" (2nd house), the "house of brothers" (3rd house), and the other houses of the circle, and only then should he judge. 

Indeed, there is a particular significance to the degrees of the signs which astrologers use in certain specific matters. However, if there is an error in the location of a planet by a few minutes or a single degree, this does not hinder the "mastery of judgments" <note>The overall art of interpretation.</note>. It is necessary that he who practices the mastery of judgments—when the "calculator" <note>term: <term>Calculator</term> (Latin: *calculator*); a person or professional role dedicated purely to the mathematical computation of planetary positions, distinct from the astrologer who interprets them.</note> has given him the locations of the planets among the signs for certain times—judges through those locations whether the planets signify this or that. Truly, the certainty of the degree and the exact place they are in among their signs, according to the truth, shall rest upon the calculators and be demanded from them. 

He provides an example concerning physicians, saying: a physician is bound to judge the nature of each medicine, what thing it is useful for, and which infirmities it may profit. However, the seeking of the species in various regions and the grinding of them is not to be demanded of physicians, but from the "authors of spices" <note>Apothecaries or suppliers.</note>. So it is, he says, concerning the judgments of the stars. To that man who performs the judgments pertai-

<summary>This page addresses three skeptical "sects": those who limit planetary influence to the seasons, those who claim astrology cannot be proven by experience due to the long cycles of the planets, and those who dismiss it because mathematical tables disagree. Albumasar defends the field by distinguishing the role of the interpretive astrologer from the mathematical calculator, comparing it to the relationship between a doctor and an apothecary.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the defense of astrology against specific schools of thought ("sects"). It concludes the discussion of the sixth sect and proceeds through the seventh, eighth, ninth, and tenth sects, drawing heavily on the authority of Albumasar (the Persian astrologer Abu Ma'shar) and Hippocrates.</meta>

<sig>d iij</sig>

...not only to defend that which the planets signify over the things of this world, and what they signify; it pertains to this [science] to know their properties and natures. To the calculator <term>Calculator (Latin: *calculator*): In this period, a "calculator" was someone who performed the mathematical computations of planetary positions, whereas the "astrologer" interpreted their meaning.</term> it belongs to verify their degrees.

¶ **The seventh sect** is of those who rejected this science because they were overwhelmed by it <note>original: *aspersissent in ea*; literally "splattered in it," implying they became lost or frustrated by its complexity.</note> and it was impossible for them to arrive at what they desired. These are the envious followers of this mastery, [who envy] the knowledge of others within it; and they reject the science of the mastery of judgments out of envy. These are not to be contradicted, as Albumasar says, since their denial belongs to this mastery. For a denier ought not to be constrained, except by that which overcomes him and compels him to come to the evident truth.

¶ **The eighth sect** consists of certain people who boast of medical science so that they may increase their gain through it; these are not, however, honest and wise doctors in the mastery of medicine who have already read the books of the ancients in the science of medicine and known the roots of their mastery and the diversity of the elements or seasons and natures, as well as their corruption and other things that are necessary to them in their mastery, regarding both individual and composite things. These [wise doctors] have known the dignity of the science of the judgments of the stars. And they know that the science of the stars is the beginning of the science of medicine. And they use the mastery of judgments in the mastery of medicine with frequent use in the recognition of diseases, and in their increase and decrease, and in the timing of cures. Their right intention in their mastery is multiplied, and the sick person is liberated through their hands, and the benefits to men are multiplied through them. 

Some indeed of the doctors—specifically those in whom ignorance abounds, while reason is shortened in the science of those things which are necessary to them—contend in their mastery so that they put profit first and put science last. Finally, they have contradicted the science of judgments and said that there is no power in this world from the movements of the planets. Against these, Albumasar alleges the saying of Hippocrates, saying: And Hippocrates has already said in the *Book of the Air* <note>Referring to the Hippocratic treatise *On Airs, Waters, and Places*, a foundational text connecting environment and health.</note>, while making mention of the diversity of the air and of the elements or natures, that the things we have said about the diversity of the air are [contained] in the science of the stars. And that the science of the stars is no small part of the science of medicine.

¶ **The ninth sect** is that of the general commonality <note>original: *uniuersitatis uulgi*; the general mass of the common people.</note>. And these are divided into two sects: for one is of those who do not know the dignity of judgments and other sciences, nor the dignity of the foreknowledge of things. The worthier man among them is he who has more substance <note>meaning material wealth or property.</note>. Of these, it was spoken before. Against these, Albumasar says that substance and fortune are fitted to the wise and the foolish, to the strong and the weak; and a man is not praiseworthy for that which is granted to him by fortune, because this is not granted to him through his science, nor through his foolishness, nor through his strength or weakness; but he is praised for his wisdom and knowledge, because the dignity of man over other animals is not established except through wisdom and knowledge, and through the knowledge of things that were and which are to come. And after some things are interposed, he says that the most worthy thing in man is the knowledge of future things.

¶ **The tenth sect** is also of the general commonality. And these have rejected the science of this mastery because of what they have seen of the multitude of errors of those who boast of it; because the common people receive things through the perception of appearances. When they have seen the multitude of errors of those who boast of this science in those things which are asked from the science of the judgments of the stars, thinking it a lie, they rejected it; and they refer the masters of this science to foolishness and said that it is a hollow science. Albumasar says that these are not to be blamed in their contradiction of the masteries...

It emphasizes astrology's vital role in medicine, citing Hippocrates as an authority.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the critique of fraudulent practitioners (completing the ten sects described by Albumasar) and introduces an eleventh sect: those who believe astrology is contrary to the Christian faith. It then transitions into a systematic division of astronomy and astrology, citing authorities like Ptolemy and Haly Abenragel, ultimately focusing on Alchabitius as the primary introductory source.</meta>

...of this science: because many of those who boast of it are foolish, unskilled, and frail men. And with certain things interposed <note>original: "quibusdam interpositis"; a common scholastic phrasing meaning "after some other remarks."</note>, he says that these men exercise various deceptions under the name of this mastery, by which they seduce weak men. He means to say that some perform geomancy <term>Geomancy: A method of divination that interprets markings on the ground or patterns formed by tossed handfuls of soil, rocks, or sand.</term> or through sorceries <note>original: "sortilegia"</note> under the name of this science. These men seek nothing but profit. And he says that when these men are questioned about any matter, they incline their response toward the thing that pleases the seeker and suits him, wishing to make him happy for the sake of their own greed. Nor is it enough [to speak] of the ten sects which Albumasar <note>Albumasar: Abu Ma'shar (787–886 AD), a prominent Persian astrologer whose works were foundational to Western astrology.</note> posits.

¶ And an eleventh sect could be added. These are those who say that this science is against the faith. To whom I say that they have not read the books of the ancient wise men; for if they had read them, they would know that this science is not against the faith, but rather for the faith. For the doctors of this science posit that the world was created, which is the first foundation of the faith. For Haly Abenragel <note>Haly Abenragel: Also known as Albohazen (10th-11th century), an Arab astrologer known for "The Complete Book on the Judgment of the Stars."</note> says in the first part of his book, in the chapter on the nature of Jupiter, that at the time when God began to create the world, He placed Jupiter in the ascendant. And Albumasar says in his *Introductory*: "Let us first say that the movement of the circle <note>i.e., the celestial sphere</note> is made by the power of the Prime Cause <term>Prime Cause (Latin: *prima causa*): A philosophical term for God as the first mover and creator of all things.</term>." And with certain things interposed, he says: "Behold how we have drawn [knowledge of] the Creator from things appearing and known which are reached by the senses—that He is eternal, having power without an essence of an end <note>meaning His essence has no limit</note>, immobile and incorruptible; most high be His name, blessed and exalted with the greatest exaltation."

¶ Concerning the division of astronomy, I dispatch myself briefly and set down the division which Albumasar posits in his *Great Introduction*. There are two species of astronomy: of which one is the science of the whole—namely, concerning the circles and their movements. The second is the art of astronomical judgments <note>This refers to astrology: the interpretation of planetary influence.</note>. Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), the Alexandrian polymath whose works defined medieval astronomy and astrology.</note> also posits this division in the beginning of his *Quadripartitum* <note>Also known as the *Tetrabiblos* or "Four Books," the seminal work on astrology.</note>. And Haly, in his commentary, says the same. The first species is handed down perfectly and completely, as far as principles and conclusions are concerned, by Ptolemy in the *Almagest* <term>Almagest: The influential 2nd-century mathematical and astronomical treatise on the motions of stars and planets.</term>. Or the first species could be subdivided. The science of movements <note>The OCR reads "moribus" (customs/morals), but the context of astronomical instruments strongly suggests "motibus" (movements).</note> is two-fold: one is instrumental, the other is tabular. That instrumental science is two-fold: for some are instruments for observing or investigating movements, such as the rulers of Ptolemy, armillary spheres, and similar things. Others are instruments for carrying out daily operations, such as the astrolabe <term>Astrolabe: A portable instrument used by astronomers to measure the altitude of celestial bodies and solve problems related to time and position.</term>, the solid sphere, and similar things. The second species—namely, the art of astrological judgments—has four principal parts. Of which the first is concerning interrogations <note>Horary astrology: answering specific questions based on the time they are asked.</note>. The second, concerning nativities <note>Natal astrology: the study of a person's life based on their birth chart.</note>. The third, concerning the revolutions of years; and this is two-fold: namely, the revolutions of the years of the world <note>Mundane astrology: predicting world events like wars or weather.</note>, and the revolutions of the years of nativities <note>Solar returns: predicting a person's year ahead.</note>. The fourth, concerning elections <note>Elective astrology: choosing the best time to begin an undertaking.</note>. Concerning these four parts, Haly Abenragel made one complete book. Ptolemy, however, in the *Quadripartitum*, omitted two parts: namely, interrogations and the elections of hours. But Haly, who commented upon him, excuses him, saying that these two parts can be sufficiently obtained from those things which Ptolemy says in that same place. Besides these, there are certain other parts of judgments: namely, concerning great conjunctions <note>The meeting of outer planets, especially Jupiter and Saturn, believed to signal major historical shifts.</note>, concerning images, and concerning seals, of which we have little or nothing. Many of the ancients and moderns, noticing however that these parts would be difficult to understand for those who ought first to learn that science, made introductory books in which they placed the principles and explained the terms which the masters of judgments use. Among other introductory works, however, the book of Alchabitius <note>Alchabitius: Al-Qabisi (10th century), an Arab astrologer whose work became the standard university textbook for astrology.</note> is most approved among the moderns. Therefore, having set aside the others, we intend [to treat] of him for the present. These things having been seen, let us descend to the text.

<meta>The previous page concluded a general defense of astrology against skeptics and a brief classification of the sciences, ending with the transition to Alchabitius as the primary introductory text for a student's curriculum.</meta>

# Preliminary Questions for the Master.

<note>original: "Postulata a domino." In a medieval scholastic context, these are the introductory questions or "postulates" a student might ask a master before beginning a new text.</note>

**P**reliminary questions for the master. At the beginning of this book, seven things may be asked: first, what is the intention of the book; second, what is its utility; third, what is the title of the book; fourth, under which part of philosophy is it classified; fifth, when it ought to be read; sixth, concerning the subject of the book; and seventh, concerning the division of the book. 

¶ The intention of the book is to make clear the knowledge of each single thing whose knowledge is necessary for a beginner to learn the science of the judgments of the stars <note>original: "iudiciorum astrorum"; this refers to judicial astrology, the art of making predictions based on planetary positions.</note>. Its utility is evident: for anyone who wishes to begin the science of judgments, the doctrine of this book will be sufficient for them; if they understand it well, they will be able to understand all other books of judgments on their own. The title of this book is as follows: *The Beginnings of the Introduction by Alchabitius to the Science of the Judgments of the Stars*. <note>Alchabitius: Al-Qabisi (died 967 AD), an influential Arab astrologer and mathematician whose work became the standard introductory textbook in European universities.</note> And it ought to be read before every other book on the judgments of the stars. Which part [of philosophy] it belongs to is clear from what has preceded: for it is part of the science of the judgments of the stars. Its subject is that which is the subject in all of judicial astronomy <term>Judicial Astronomy: In the Middle Ages, "astronomy" and "astrology" were often interchangeable; "judicial" refers specifically to the practice of making "judgments" or predictions.</term>, namely, the celestial body insofar as it causes generation and corruption in things existing under the sphere of the moon through its motion and light. <note>Generation and corruption: An Aristotelian concept referring to the birth and decay of physical matter in the "sublunary" world (the Earth), which was believed to be governed by the movements of the stars.</note> The division of the book will become clear in the process of reading. However, this book is divided in its first division into two parts: namely, into the introductory part and the executive part. The executive part begins there: "In the name of the Lord, the zodiac," and so forth. The executive part is divided into five parts according to how the author divides it in the text, which he calls the "five differences," as will be clear immediately. The introductory part can be divided into four parts: because in the first, he invokes divine help for the completion of his work, and with this, he seeks the goodwill of his king, at whose instance he perhaps composed this book. In the second part, he shows the cause of his book or the motive that moved him to make this book. In the third part, he excuses himself regarding the reasons [the theoretical proofs]. In the fourth part, he sets down the division of his book. The second part begins there: "When I had seen." The third part begins there: "And I did not introduce." The fourth part begins there: "And I divided it into five differences." First, briefly, like a faithful man, he asks God for length of life so that he may complete his work. And he asks for the durability of the honor of the king’s sword, the protection of his works, and the expansion of his empire.

## When I had seen.

<note>original: "cum vidissem"; these subheadings refer to the opening words of specific sections in Alchabitius's original text.</note>

In this part, he sets down the causes that moved him to make this book. And he says that when he had seen many of the ancients having made introductory books to the science of the judgments of the stars, and that some of them had not included all the things necessary for an introduction, and some had delivered what they taught too prolixly <note>original: "prolixe"; at great or tedious length.</note>, and that among their confused sayings, those things which are necessary for an introduction had perished—that is, they were obscured. And also that some had not proceeded in their sayings in a disciplined order, by putting first those things which ought to be put first, and putting after those things which ought to be put after, but had done the opposite. Therefore, he began to publish this book and collected in it from the sayings of the ancients those things which were necessary for an introduction, and omitted the useless things.

## And I did not introduce.

<note>original: "et nō introduxi"</note>

Here he excuses himself regarding the reasons [the proofs] and says that he did not put in this book the reasons that would be necessary to defend what he said, because these have been placed in the book of Ptolemy, namely, in the *Quadripartitum*. <note>Quadripartitum: Also known as the *Tetrabiblos* (The Four Books) by Claudius Ptolemy, the foundational text of Western astrology.</note> For there, Ptolemy confirms the judgments through many reasons. And he says also that he himself made one book on the "Confirmation of Judgments" where he placed reasons sufficient for this purpose.

<sig>d iiij</sig>

<summary>This page introduces Alchabitius's "Introduction to the Science of the Judgments of the Stars," outlining the book's structure, its pedagogical purpose for beginners, and the author's motivation to provide a more organized and concise textbook than his predecessors.</summary>
<keywords>Alchabitius, introduction to astrology, judicial astronomy, Ptolemy, Tetrabiblos, scholasticism, sublunary world, generation and corruption</keywords>

<meta>The previous page established the introductory questions (accessus) for studying Alchabitius, defining the book's purpose as a foundational guide for judicial astrology. This page moves from the preface into the formal division of the book and begins the first section on the nature of the zodiac.</meta>

<margin>Here he provides the division of his book. And he says that he divided it into five differences <note>original: "differentias"; in scholastic texts, a "difference" refers to a major section or chapter.</note> as is evident by reading the text.</margin>

# And I divided it.

<margin>With the preface finished at:</margin>
->In the name of the Lord. The Zodiac.<-

The treatise begins. And it can be divided into five parts, which are the five differences according to how the author divides them in the text. The first difference concerns the essential and accidental being of the circle of signs <note>original: "circuli signorum"; the zodiac belt.</note>. The second is about the natures of the seven planets. The third is about what happens to the seven planets. The fourth is about the explanation of terms or names used by the masters of judgments <note>original: "iudiciorum"; practitioners of judicial astrology who predict events based on the stars.</note>. The fifth is about the projection of parts <note>original: "projectione partium"; a calculation method for determining specific astrological "lots" or points of influence.</note>. The second difference begins there: "And with God's help." The third there: "For the being of the planets." The fourth there: "The beginning of these." The fifth there: "And when we have noted." The first part is divided into two, for in the first part he dispatches with the essential being of the circle of signs. Second, with the accidental being.
The second starts there: "For the circle is figured in every hour." The first part can be divided into four parts. In the first, he determines the division of the circle of signs and those things which follow from that division. In the second part, he determines the dignities <note>original: "dignitatibus"; the relative strength or influence a planet has when located in a specific sign.</note> of the distinguished planets. In the third, about the figures of the signs. In the fourth, what the signs possess regarding the members of a human being, and what they possess regarding regions and plants. The second part begins there: "The planets also have." The third there: "The figures of the signs follow these." The fourth there: "And each one." The first part can be divided into five. In the first part, he provides the division of the circle of signs. In the second part, he compares them to one another. In the third, he compares them to the four seasons of the year, to the four ages of life, and to the four humors <note>original: "humores"; the bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, black bile) thought to govern health and temperament.</note>. In the fourth, he provides the order of the planets and explains how it is understood when he says "a planet is in a sign."
In the fifth, he adds something regarding the head and tail of the dragon <note>original: "capite et cauda draconis"; the north and south lunar nodes where the moon's orbit crosses the ecliptic.</note>. The second begins there: "And six of these signs are northern." The third there: "And he calls those quarters." The fourth there: "But for all planets." The fifth there: "It also signifies through the head and tail." First, he says that the circle of signs is divided into twelve equal parts which are called "signs," and he names them with the names of the twelve images that are in that circle of signs: which are Aries, Taurus, Gemini, etc. And any sign is divided into thirty equal parts which are called degrees <term>gradus: the 360 degrees of a circle, with 30 per sign</term>. And any degree is divided into sixty, and they are called minutes. And any minute into sixty, and they are called seconds. And any second into sixty, and they are called thirds. And any third into sixty, and they are called fourths, and so on into infinity.
Around this part, two doubts can be raised. The first is concerning the number of signs. The second is concerning the order. Albumazar <note>Albumazar (Abu Ma'shar, 787–886 AD) was the most influential Persian astrologer of the Middle Ages.</note> disputes the number of signs, saying: "Certain of those who felt differently than us contradicted the sayings of the ancients regarding the number of signs. And they said: 'Why do you think the signs are twelve, neither more nor less?'"
To whom Albumazar responds, saying: that Aratus <note>original: "Aracis"; Aratus of Soli (c. 315–240 BC), a Greek poet whose work on constellations was a standard reference.</note> the philosopher revealed forty-eight images which are in the circle (that is, in the eighth sphere) and their names; and all the ancient wise men authorized his opinion and agreed with him upon this. And twelve of these images are in the circle of signs. For this reason, they said there are twelve. Albumazar assigns another cause, saying: that the philosophers found that all things which are created and destroyed in this world are composed of elements, and in every individual thing that is made from the four elements, there are three states: the beginning <note>original: "finitum"; though usually meaning 'end', here used in the context of limits or starting boundaries.</note>, the middle, and the end.
Therefore, the four elements multiplied by three make twelve; thus the signs are twelve. A third cause is because through the motion of the sun, generation and corruption occur in things in...

<summary>This page outlines the structural division of Alchabitius's astronomical treatise into five "differences" and explains the numerical and philosophical reasons why the zodiac is divided into twelve signs based on the four elements and the three stages of existence.</summary>

<meta>Continuing from the division of the book, this page begins a detailed exploration of the zodiac's structure. The author now moves from the general table of contents to the specific astronomical and philosophical justifications for the arrangement of the twelve signs, addressing common scholastic "doubts" or questions regarding why the zodiac begins with Aries.</meta>

...lower bodies, and its work appears more in this world than the work of other planets. And it rotates within these twelve images <note>original: "imaginibus"; referring here to the constellations of the zodiac.</note>, and they are its place. And according to its course within these twelve images, the seasons of the year are varied. Therefore, the philosophers placed the twelve images as being more worthy of significance. And they attributed a universal significance to them. But to the other thirty-six images <note>The 48 Ptolemaic constellations minus the 12 zodiac signs.</note>, they attributed a specific significance.

### The Second Doubt

The second doubt <note>A "dubitatio" is a standard scholastic method of raising a potential counter-argument to clarify a point.</note> concerns the order and the beginning. For someone could ask why they began with Aries, since a circle has neither beginning nor end. To this, Ptolemy says in the first section of the first part of the *Tetrabiblos* <note>original: "quadripartiti"; the foundational four-part work on astrology by Claudius Ptolemy.</note>: that the beginning is from Aries because it is a masculine sign, and the active agent is more worthy than the passive, and the equinoctial <note>The celestial equator.</note> passes through it. From that point—that is, from the equinoctial—the greatest change of the whole firmament begins.

But someone might say that by this reasoning, it seems the beginning should be from Libra; because it is a masculine sign, the equinoctial passes through it, and the greatest motion of the firmament is there. Why, therefore, did they begin from Aries rather than from Libra? It can be solved by the words of Ptolemy in the 14th proposition <note>OCR reads ".i. 40.", likely referring to the 14th chapter/proposition of Book 1.</note> of the first part, where he says that the beginning in the firmament is that place in which the complexion <term>complexion: the specific balance of the four qualities (hot, cold, wet, dry) in a person or time.</term> of the time is more moist, just as the beginning of life for all animals is the age in which the complexion is more moist. It could be said otherwise: that from Aries generation begins, but from Libra begins corruption <note>original: "corruptio"; in the Aristotelian sense of decay or the breaking down of matter.</note>.

### On the Order of the Signs

Regarding the order, someone might ask why they placed Taurus after Aries, and then Gemini, and afterwards Cancer. Albumasar <note>Abu Ma'shar, a highly influential 9th-century Persian astrologer.</note> says that there are four simple qualities of the elements: namely heat, dryness, moisture, and coldness. And two of these are like agents—namely heat and cold—and the other two are like passive things—namely dryness and moisture. Agents, however, are more worthy than passive things; therefore they began with the agents. And of the agents, one is the principle of generation (heat), and the other the principle of corruption (coldness).

A thing, however, is generated before it is corrupted; therefore they placed Aries at the beginning, which is a hot and dry sign of the nature of fire (which is primarily hot). And they placed Cancer at the end <note>The end of the first quadrant of the year.</note> which is a cold and moist sign of the nature of water (which is primarily cold). And they placed Taurus after Aries, which is a cold and dry sign of the nature of earth (which is primarily dry); for the dryness of earth is compatible with the heat of fire. And they placed the sign of Gemini before Cancer because it is a hot and moist sign of the nature of air (which is primarily moist); and the moisture of air is compatible with the moisture of water.

For the evidence of the aforesaid, it must be understood that in the eighth sphere <note>The sphere of the fixed stars, situated beyond the spheres of the seven planets.</note> there are forty-eight images, upon which there is agreement among all the ancients, as was said. And there are in them 1,022 stars which are called "fixed stars," and they all move with one motion—namely, according to the motion of the eighth sphere—approximately one degree every hundred years. And the ancients placed them in six orders <note>The six "magnitudes" of stellar brightness.</note> so that those which are larger were placed in the first order, and there are fifteen of them. And those which are below these they placed in the second order, and there are 45. And those which are below these they placed in the third order, and there are 208. And those which are below these they placed in the fourth order, and there are 474. And those which are below these they placed in the fifth order, and there are 217. And those which are below these they placed in the sixth order, and there are sixty-three.

Of these 48 images, 12 are in the path of the sun <note>The ecliptic.</note> as has been said; and there are 346 of the stars within them. The names of those images are Aries, Taurus, etc. And there remain 36 images, of which 21 decline from the path of the sun toward the north and 15 toward the south. Those which decline to the north contain 360 of the aforementioned stars. And the names of these images are these:
1. The first is the Lesser Bear <note>Ursa Minor.</note>.
2. The second is the Greater Bear <note>Ursa Major.</note>.
3. The third is the Dragon <note>Draco.</note>.
4. The fourth is the Master of the Flame or the Kindled One <note>original: "dominus flamme vel succensus"; Cepheus.</note>.
5. The fifth is the Barking or Howling Dog <note>original: "canis latrans vel ululans"; Boötes.</note>.

Sixth, the **Northern Crown** <note>original: "corona septentrionalis"; known today as Corona Borealis.</note>. Seventh, the **Kneeler** <note>original: "genuflexus"; the constellation Hercules, often depicted as a kneeling man.</note>. Eighth, the **Falling Vulture** <note>original: "vultur cadens"; the constellation Lyra.</note>. Ninth, the **Hen** <note>original: "gallina"; the constellation Cygnus.</note>. Tenth, the **One Sitting on a Chair** <note>original: "sedens super sedem"; the constellation Cassiopeia.</note>. Eleventh, the **One Carrying the Head of Algol**, that is, the Devil <note>The constellation Perseus. "Algol" comes from the Arabic "al-Ghul," meaning the Ghoul or Demon.</note>. Twelfth, the **One Holding the Reins** <note>original: "tenens habenas"; the constellation Auriga.</note>. 13, the **Serpent-Bearer** <note>original: "serpentarius"; Ophiuchus.</note>. 14, the **Serpent**. 15, the **Arrow** <note>original: "amisa to"; likely a corruption of "sagitta".</note>. 16, the **Flying Eagle**. Another book has it as the "Flying Vulture" <note>original: "vultur volans"; the constellation Aquila.</note>. 17, the **Dolphin**. 18, the **First Horse**. Another book has it as the "Head of the Horse" <note>original: "caput equi"; the constellation Equuleus.</note>. 19, the **Second Horse**. Another book has it as the "Winged Horse" <note>original: "equus alatus"; Pegasus.</note>. 20, the **Woman Who Has Not Known a Man** <note>original: "mulier que non novit virum"; the constellation Andromeda.</note>. 21 is the **Triangle**.

As for those that decline toward the south from the aforementioned stars, there are 16 <note>The text lists 15; medieval counts often varied depending on whether certain stars were grouped together.</note>, the names of which images are these: The first of them is the **Marine Lion**, which some call the **Bear** <note>original: "marin⁹ leo... vrsum"; referring to Cetus, the Whale or Sea Monster.</note>. 2, **Algebar**, that is, the **Strong Dog** <note>original: "algebar... canis validus"; the constellation Orion.</note>. 3, the **River** <note>Eridanus.</note>. 4, the **Hare**. 5, the **Greater Dog**. 6, the **Lesser Dog**. 7, **Noah’s Ark** <note>original: "archa noe"; the constellation Argo Navis, often given a biblical interpretation in the Middle Ages.</note>. 8, the **Serpent** <note>The constellation Hydra.</note>. 9, the **Vessel**. 10, the **Raven** <note>original: "vas... corvus"; the constellations Crater and Corvus.</note>. 11, the **One Carrying the Lion**, half of which is of human figure and the other of a horse <note>original: "deferens leonem"; the constellation Centaurus.</note>. 12, the **Leopard** <note>original: "leopardus"; the constellation Lupus.</note>. 13, the **Censer** <note>original: "thuribulum"; the constellation Ara.</note>. 14, the **Southern Crown**. 15, the **Southern Fish**.

<margin>
In this part, the author compares the signs to one another. It can be divided into three parts. First, he compares them regarding the parts of the world, or regarding their declinations. Second, regarding their ascensions. Third, regarding their qualities. The first part is at the beginning. The second is there: "And six of these are said to be directly ascending." The third is there: "And it is called the half."

## And six of these signs.

First, he says that six of the aforementioned twelve signs are **northern**: because they decline from the equinoctial line <note>The celestial equator.</note> toward the northern pole, namely from the beginning of **Aries** to the end of **Virgo**. And the other six—namely from the beginning of **Libra** to the end of **Pisces**—are **southern**: because they decline from the equinoctial line toward the southern pole. Concerning this part, it must be understood that the **equinoctial** is the circle which the sun describes by the "snatching of the firmament" <note>original: "raptu firmamenti"; the rapid daily rotation of the heavens.</note>, that is, through its daily motion when it is at the first point of Aries, and it describes the same at the first point of Libra. Therefore, it is called by **Ptolemy** the "equator of day and night": because at that time, the day and night are of the same length throughout the whole world where the sun rises and sets.

I say "where the sun rises and sets" because there are two places in the world over which the sun does not rise or set when it is at the beginning of Aries or Libra: one of which is under the **Arctic pole** and the other under the **Antarctic pole**. Here, a question could be raised: **Whether there is habitation under the equator?** It seems not. For where there is perpetual heat, there is no habitation; but under the equator there is perpetual heat. For the sun always runs over that part and declines very little from it. Likewise, the author of the *Sphere* <note>Referring to Johannes de Sacrobosco’s *De Sphaera Mundi*, a standard medieval astronomical textbook.</note> says there are five zones in the heavens: one of which is the **torrid zone**, and that, according to him, is under the equator and extends on both sides to the tropics; and two are under the poles, and two are in the middle. There, he maintains that three of them are uninhabitable: namely, the torrid zone because of excessive heat, and the two existing under the poles because of excessive cold; and the other two, according to him, are habitable because they are temperate.

Briefly, I believe along with **Avicenna** <note>The Persian polymath Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), whose works on medicine and philosophy were central to medieval European universities.</note> that there is habitation under the equator, and indeed the **best habitation**. And I will briefly set down one reason that moved me. The best habitation is where there is the greatest temperance; but under the equator there is the greatest temperance; therefore, etc. The "major premise" is well known. I declare the "minor premise": for under the equator, day and night are equal, as has been said; and the air can be cooled as much in the night as it is heated in the day. Likewise, the declination of the sun varies most greatly there; for the declination changes in one day by 24 minutes <note>A "minute" in this context is 1/60th of a degree.</note>. Therefore, it can perhaps hardly happen that the sun passes directly over their heads, because this does not happen unless the sun enters the beginning of Aries or Libra exactly at midday. Likewise, the sun never remains at the same height above them; but immediately, in an instant—if motion could exist in an instant—the altitude is varied. For as quickly as the "prime mover" <note>The outermost celestial sphere that drives the motion of all others.</note> moves, it approaches the meridian and recedes from the meridian.

<vocab>
**Equinoctial line**: The celestial equator, where day and night are equal.
**Torrid zone**: The region between the tropics, once thought too hot for human life.
**Declination**: The angular distance of a heavenly body north or south of the celestial equator.
**Prime mover**: In Aristotelian physics, the outermost sphere of the heavens that initiates all movement.
</vocab>

<summary>This page completes the list of the 48 Ptolemaic constellations and presents a classic medieval debate on the habitability of the Earth's equator. The author argues, following Avicenna, that the equator is actually the most temperate and habitable region because the equal length of day and night allows the air to cool sufficiently.</summary>

<meta>The previous page concluded a catalog of the 48 classical constellations and began a scholastic debate regarding the habitability of the Earth's equator and the nature of celestial heat.</meta>

And where a great heating must occur, it is necessary that the rays be fixed for some time; just as we see in a **burning mirror** <note>original: "speculo ardenti"; a concave or parabolic mirror used to concentrate sunlight into a beam hot enough to start fires.</note>, if the mirror were moved, it would not set fire to the straw or other things placed before it. Likewise, the cold planets restrain the heat of the hot ones there; because all are always nearby, although some say that planets do not cause coldness but that their "coldness" is actually just a lesser degree of heat.

In truth, I do not believe this, saving their grace. For I have seen that the same cause of cold does not produce as much cold in far northern parts as it does in less northern ones. It could also be proven by reason. If it were so that no planet influenced coldness but only caused heat, it would follow that by the presence of **Saturn** <note>In medieval astrology, Saturn was considered the "Great Malefic" and naturally cold and dry.</note> in summer, the summer would become hotter; because adding more heat to heat makes the thing more hot. But we do not see this, wherefore etc. 

And if someone were to say that its lesser heat tempers the greater heat of the **Sun**, just as lukewarm water tempers hot water: this is not valid, because in that case there is a material mixture, but in celestial influences there is a **virtual** <term>virtualis: referring to the "virtue" or inherent power of a celestial body rather than its physical substance.</term> mixture. One must imagine the influences of greater and lesser heat like two fires heating one pot, where one is stronger than the other. It is certain that the smaller fire will not cool the pot nor hinder the stronger fire in its heating. The arguments to the contrary are resolved: for the first says that there is perpetual heat there. I say that there is no perpetual heat there that would hinder habitation. I deny the **Author of the Sphere** <note>Johannes de Sacrobosco (c. 1195–1256), whose "De Sphaera Mundi" was the standard astronomical textbook of the Middle Ages. The author here is disagreeing with Sacrobosco’s view on equatorial habitability.</note> in his proposal regarding this.

# And six of them are said to be directly asce<gloss>nding</gloss>ting.

<margin>This part</margin>
could be divided, but there is no need. He says briefly that six signs from the aforementioned signs ascend "rightly" (directly) and six obliquely. They ascend rightly from the beginning of **Cancer** to the end of **Sagittarius**. The other six ascend obliquely. These things are found in the *Treatise on the Sphere*, so there is no need to linger here. 

Afterward, he says that those ascending tortuously or obliquely **obey** those ascending rightly. Regarding this, it should be noted that two people might be born at certain times in which all other astrological significations are equal regarding their "root" <note>The "root" refers to the primary astronomical data of a birth chart.</note> both superior and inferior; but one of them is born under a sign of direct ascension and the other under a sign of oblique or tortuous ascension. The one born under the oblique sign will "obey" the other born under the direct sign, provided those signs are of the same distance from the beginning of Cancer. 

For example: he who is born under **Gemini** will obey him who is born under **Cancer**; and he under **Taurus** will obey him under **Leo**; and he under **Aries** will obey him under **Virgo**, and so on for the others. Afterward, he says that any two signs that are of the same distance from the beginning of **Aries** are called "concordant": such as **Pisces** and **Aries**, **Aquarius** and **Taurus**, **Capricorn** and **Gemini**, and so on. And this can be applied as before. And this is because the ascensions of those signs are equal. And this is what the author of the *Treatise on the Sphere* says: "Any two signs equally distant from either of the equinoctial points are equal in their ascensions." 

Afterward, he says that the half of the circle from the beginning of **Leo** to the end of **Capricorn** is called the **greatest half** on account of the ascensions. And it is the half of the **Sun**, because Leo is the "house" or domicile of the Sun, and the ascensions begin to increase notably there. And the Sun has dignity in that whole half, just as planets do in their "terms" <note>A "term" is a specific subdivision of a zodiac sign ruled by a particular planet.</note>. And the other half belongs to the **Moon**, namely from the beginning of **Aquarius** to the end of **Cancer**; and that is called the **least half** because the ascensions notably begin to be named <note>original: "nominari"; possibly a scribal error for "minui" (to decrease), given the context of the "least half."</note> there. And the Moon has dignity there, just as the Sun has in the greatest half.

## And it is called a half.

And briefly, he says that the half which is from the beginning of **Aries** to the end of **Virgo** is the warm half: because it creates summer for us. And the other half is cold: because it creates winter for us.

<margin>
Here he compares these twelve signs to the four seasons of the year, to the four ages of life, and to the four humors or complexions.

## And that is called a quarter part

And he says that the quarter part which is from the beginning of **Aries** until the beginning of **Cancer** is hot and moist, like spring, and like the age of childhood, and like the **sanguine** <term>sanguine: one of the four traditional temperaments, associated with the element of air and the humor of blood; it was thought to make a person lively and social.</term> complexion. And the other quarter part, which is from the beginning of Cancer until the beginning of **Libra**, is hot and dry like summer, and like the second age of life <note>referring to the prime of youth or early adulthood</note>, and like the **choleric** <term>choleric: associated with the element of fire and yellow bile; thought to make a person ambitious or quick-tempered.</term> complexion. And the other quarter part, which is from the beginning of Libra until the beginning of **Capricorn**, is cold and dry like autumn, and like the third age, which is the beginning of decline <note>The period of life when physical strength begins to wane, corresponding to middle age.</note>, and like the **melancholic** <term>melancholic: associated with the element of earth and black bile; thought to make a person serious or introspective.</term> complexion. And the quarter part which is from the beginning of Capricorn until the end of **Pisces** is cold and moist like winter, and like the final age of life, and the **phlegmatic** <term>phlegmatic: associated with the element of water and the humor of phlegm; thought to make a person calm or stoic.</term> complexion.

<margin>
Here he sets down the order of the seven planets and how they are understood when it is said "a planet is in a sign," as is clear in the text. But note here the triple way of understanding a "sign," just as the author of the *Sphere* <note>Johannes de Sacrobosco, whose 13th-century textbook *De sphaera mundi* was the standard astronomical authority for centuries.</note> sets it down; and because you will find it there, I do not include it here.
</margin>

## For all the planets.

<margin>
Previously, the author determined the division of the circle of signs and certain things that follow from that division. Here he wishes to determine the dignities of the planets in the signs.
</margin>

## Moreover, the planets have

And this is divided into two parts. For first, he determines the essential **dignities** <term>dignities: in astrology, these are specific positions in the Zodiac where a planet's influence is naturally strengthened, similar to a guest being honored in a specific home.</term>. Second, he determines the accidental ones. The second part is found there: "from the accidental powers of the planets," at the end of this section. The first part is divided into two: because in the first he does what was said, namely, he determines the dignities of the planets in the signs. In the second part, he determines the strengths they have within those dignities themselves. That second part is there: "and because now with God's help." 

The first part is divided into five parts according to the five dignities: for first he determines the dignity of the planets called **house** <note>original: "domus"; also known as domicile, the sign where a planet is most "at home."</note>. Second, the dignity called **exaltation**. Third, the dignity called **triplicity**. Fourth, the dignity called **term**. Fifth, the dignity called **face**. The second part begins: "these are the exaltations." The third begins: "we distinguish the triplicities thus." The fourth begins: "they also have in each sign." The fifth begins: "the faces of the signs." 

The first part can be divided into two sub-parts: because in the first part he shows which are the houses of the planets. In the second part, [he shows] how each of them, except for the Sun and Moon, has two houses, one of which is more primary to it, and that is called its **joy** <term>joy: a specific sign where a planet is said to be particularly comfortable or well-placed.</term>; therefore he shows which are those houses in which they "joy." That second part begins: "the signs in which." The first [sub-part] is divided into two. First, he sets down what is to be determined concerning the dignities of the planets and sets down the division in the text. Second, he follows up there: "these are the houses." He says that the planets have in the aforementioned signs certain dignities or powers by nature, and some by accident. Those which are by nature are: **house**, **exaltation**, **triplicity**, **term**, and **face**. Concerning those dignities which they have by accident, he wishes to speak later.

It then introduces the "essential dignities," the five ways a planet gains strength based on its position in the Zodiac.</summary>
