--- Page 69 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the zodiacal signs, moving from their general qualities (hot, cold, moist, dry) and their associations with the seasons and humors to their specific relationships with the planets, known as "houses" or domiciles. It explains the logic behind why certain planets rule certain signs.</meta>

# These are the houses.

He says that **Aries** and **Scorpio** are the houses of **Mars**; **Taurus** and **Libra** the houses of **Venus**; **Gemini** and **Virgo** the houses of **Mercury**; **Cancer** the house of the **Moon**; **Leo** the house of the **Sun**; **Sagittarius** and **Pisces** the houses of **Jupiter**; and **Capricorn** and **Aquarius** the houses of **Saturn**. 

Afterward, he says that the seventh sign from the house of any planet is called its **detriment** <term>detriment: In astrology, a planet is in detriment when it is in the sign opposite the one it rules; it was thought to be a position of weakness or disharmony.</term>. For example: just as **Leo** is the house of the **Sun**, so **Aquarius**, which is the sign opposite to Leo, is the detriment of the Sun; and you should understand the same for the others. 

Then he says that the two signs which are the houses of a single planet are said to have **concordance** <note>original: "concordantia"</note> in the "girdle"—that is, in the circle of signs or the **zodiac**, which is wide in the middle and narrowed at the binding—that is, near the poles. To understand this, one must imagine circles passing through the beginnings of the signs and through the poles of the zodiac; the entire space included between any two circles is one sign, as was explained in the preceding lesson. 

This "concordance" seems to me to do little to explain why two signs should be the houses of one planet, because any two signs would concord in that manner. Therefore, the author's word can be explained or glossed in another way: that any two signs which are the houses of one planet are said to have concordance in the zodiac in terms of their distance from the houses of the **luminaries** <term>luminaries: The Sun and the Moon, the two brightest objects in the sky.</term>. In this way, it is true. For example: **Gemini** and **Virgo** are the houses of **Mercury** and they are equally distant from **Leo** and **Cancer**, which are the houses of the luminaries; and **Taurus** and **Libra**, which are the houses of **Venus**, are equally distant from Leo and Cancer. You will find this concordance in all of them.

**Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), the most influential astronomer and geographer of antiquity.</note> assigns the reason why **Leo** is the house of the **Sun** and **Cancer** the house of the **Moon**, and similarly for the others in the 73rd proposition <note>original: "73. pponitione"</note> and the six following propositions of the first part concerning the houses of the luminaries, the Sun and the Moon. He says that the two northern signs which are closer to the **zenith** <term>zenith: The point in the sky directly above an observer's head.</term> of our heads than the others—and for this reason they create heat—are **Cancer** and **Leo**. Both were designated as the houses for the two luminaries, which are great and noble. He assigned **Leo** to the house of the Sun because it is masculine, and **Cancer** to the house of the Moon because it is feminine.

**Haly** <note>Likely Haly Abenragel, an 11th-century Arab astrologer whose work was widely translated into Latin.</note> says in his commentary, explaining the aforementioned words, that the two most northern signs are **Cancer** and **Gemini**; but Gemini is a hot and moist sign, so it does not harmonize with either of them. In the fact that it moistens, it does not harmonize with the **Sun**, which heats and dries; nor does it harmonize with the **Moon**, because she is feminine and nocturnal, while the sign of **Gemini** is masculine and diurnal. But **Leo** harmonizes with the Sun because it is a hot and dry sign; therefore, it is fitting that Leo be the house of the Sun. And because the Moon follows the Sun, it is fitting that the house of the Moon be near the house of the Sun.

Then someone might ask: why then is **Virgo** not the house of the Moon, since it is near Leo, which is the house of the Sun? **Haly** says that the sign of Virgo is not suitable for the Moon because Virgo is a dry sign, while the Moon is moist. And for this reason, they designated **Cancer**, which is moist, as the house of the Moon. 

Regarding the houses of the other planets, he assigns two reasons: one is taken from the order of the planets and the signs; the other from their **aspects** <term>aspects: The specific geometric angles (like 90 or 180 degrees) that planets make with each other in the zodiac.</term>. Briefly, to conclude his intention in a few words, he means to say that **Capricorn** and **Aquarius** are the houses of **Saturn** because these two signs are furthest from the houses of the luminaries, and **Saturn** is the highest of all the planets. And **Sagittarius** and **Pisces** are the houses of **Jupiter** because these two signs are next to the houses of Saturn, just as Jupiter is below Saturn. **Scorpio** and **Aries** are the houses of **Mars** because they are next to the houses of Jupiter, just as Mars is below Jupiter. **Libra** and **Taurus** are the houses of **Venus** for the same reason. And **Virgo** and **Gemini** are the houses of **Mercury**. And if you begin the order from the lower [planets], you will find the same: for example, Virgo and Gemini are next to the houses of the luminaries and are the houses of Mercury, because Mercury follows after the Moon in the order; and so on for the others. 

The other reason he assigns is taken from the aspects, and he means to say that **Saturn** is the greatest in misfortune; therefore его houses should be distant from the houses of the luminaries by an **opposite aspect** <note>An aspect of 180 degrees, considered the most challenging or "difficult" relationship in traditional astrology.</note>...

<summary>This page explains the assignment of zodiac signs to planetary "houses," justifying these pairings based on the physical qualities of the signs, their geometric distance from the Sun and Moon, and the hierarchical order of the planetary spheres.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, planetary houses, zodiac, Ptolemy, Haly, Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, Venus, Mercury, Sun, Moon, detriment, concordance, planetary order</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of why specific zodiac signs are assigned to specific planets. It builds on the previous page's discussion of "houses" by explaining the geometric relationships (aspects) between the signs and the houses of the Sun and Moon.</meta>

...to [opposition], which is the aspect of perfect enmity. In this same way, Capricorn and Aquarius are distant <note>The author is explaining why Saturn's houses are opposite those of the "luminaries" (the Sun and Moon), reflecting Saturn's cold nature being opposite to their light and heat.</note>. For Capricorn is in the sign opposite the house of the Moon, namely Cancer; and Aquarius is in the sign opposite the house of the Sun, namely Leo. And Jupiter is the **greater fortune**; therefore its houses ought to be distant from the houses of the luminaries by a **trine aspect**, that is, the aspect of perfect friendship. In this way Sagittarius and Pisces are distant: for Sagittarius looks upon Leo with a trine aspect, and Pisces looks upon Cancer. And Mars is the **lesser malefic** <note>The original Latin says "fortuna minori" (lesser fortune), but the context of "enmity" and standard astrology suggests the author means the "lesser malefic" or is contrasting its power with Jupiter.</note>; therefore its houses ought to be distant from the houses of the luminaries by a **quartile aspect** <term>quartile: also known as a "square" aspect, occurring when signs are 90 degrees apart.</term>, which is the aspect of middle enmity. In this way Scorpio and Aries are distant. For Scorpio looks upon Leo with a quartile aspect, and Aries looks upon Cancer. Venus is the **lesser fortune**; therefore her houses ought to be distant from the houses of the luminaries by a **sextile aspect** <note>original: "aspectu fertili," literally a "fruitful aspect," a common medieval term for the sextile (60 degrees).</note>, which is the aspect of middle friendship. In this way Libra and Taurus are distant. For Libra looks upon Leo with a sextile aspect, and Taurus looks upon Cancer. And there are left for Mercury the two remaining signs, namely Virgo and Gemini, which are next to the houses of the luminaries; because Mercury can never be elongated from the Sun beyond one sign; indeed, it cannot be elongated from the Sun according to the equations of the **Alphonsine Tables** <term>Alphonsine Tables: A set of astronomical tables used to calculate the positions of the planets, named after King Alfonso X of Castile.</term> beyond 28 degrees and 7 minutes.

# The signs in which they rejoice.

<margin>Since each of the five planets has two houses</margin>
He shows which is the principal house of each of them. He says that Saturn rejoices in **Aquarius**, Jupiter in **Sagittarius**, Mars in **Scorpio**, Venus in **Taurus**, and Mercury in **Virgo**.

# These are the exaltations.

<margin>He says that the Sun is exalted in the 19th degree of Aries, etc. The text is straightforward.</margin>
Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century astronomer whose work "Tetrabiblos" (or Quadripartitum) was the foundation of medieval astrology.</note> assigns the cause of the exaltations of the planets in the 19th proposition of the first part of the *Quadripartitum* and in the six following. He means to say that **Aries** is the exaltation of the **Sun** because when the Sun enters Aries, the days begin to increase over the nights and its heat begins to appear more there. And **Libra** is its **fall** <term>fall: The sign opposite a planet's exaltation, where its influence was thought to be weakest.</term> because there the nights begin to increase over the days and the heat begins to diminish. And because **Saturn** is the opposite of the Sun—since where heat increases, cold is diminished and vice versa—therefore Libra is the exaltation of Saturn, and Aries is truly its fall. And when the **Moon** is joined to the Sun in Aries, its light first begins to appear in **Taurus**; therefore Taurus is the exaltation of the Moon and **Scorpio** is its fall, because it is the opposite sign. And because **Jupiter** produces northern winds and **Cancer** is a northern sign, therefore Cancer is the exaltation of Jupiter, and the opposite sign, namely **Capricorn**, is its fall. And because **Mars** is scorching by nature, and especially when it is in Capricorn it burns much more because it inclines toward the southern part; therefore Capricorn is its exaltation and is the sign opposite to the exaltation of Jupiter, and Cancer is its fall. And because **Venus** naturally moistens and especially when she is in **Pisces**, therefore Pisces is the exaltation of Venus and the opposite sign, namely **Virgo**, is her fall. And because **Mercury** dries by itself and in this is contrary to Venus, therefore Virgo is its exaltation, which is the sign opposite to the exaltation of Venus, and Pisces is its fall. **Albumazar** <note>Abu Ma'shar, a famous Persian astronomer/astrologer whose works were translated into Latin.</note> assigns almost the same causes, though he uses other words, and he took them from Ptolemy; therefore let these things suffice concerning the causes of the houses and exaltations.

<margin>Previously the author determined the houses and exaltations of the planets.</margin>

...the first part determines the **triplicities** <term>triplicities: groups of three zodiac signs that share the same elemental nature (Fire, Earth, Air, or Water)</term>. In the second part, he speaks of the **aspects** <note>original: "aspectibus," the geometric angles between planets or signs</note> at the point: *Secondly, the signs are said to look upon one another.* The first part can be divided into two. In the first, he does what has been said. In the second, he shows which signs are **movable**, which are **fixed**, and which are **common**. The second part begins there: *Four also of these signs.* The first part is divided into four sections according to the four triplicities. The second section begins: *triplicity*; the third: *triplicity*; the fourth: *the fourth triplicity*. The first is divided into two. In the first, he determines these triplicities in general. In the second, in specific detail. The first is at the beginning; the second is here: *Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius.*

He says first that all three signs that are of the same nature make one triplicity. Afterward, he says that **Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius** make the first triplicity, because those three signs are of the same nature: namely fiery, masculine, diurnal <note>day-oriented</note>, choleric <note>associated with yellow bile and a hot, dry temperament</note>, hot, and dry, with a **bitter taste**; and these three signs are eastern. And this triplicity, like each of the others, has three lords who are called the **lords of the triplicity**. 

The first lord of this triplicity in **nativities** <note>birth charts</note>, or **questions** <note>horary astrology, where a chart is cast to answer a specific question</note>, or any other things that occur in the day, is the **Sun**. The second is **Jupiter**. The third is **Saturn**. In things that occur at night, the first lord of this triplicity is **Jupiter**; the second is the **Sun**; the third is **Saturn**. 

It should be noted here what **Albumazar** <note>Abu Ma'shar (787–886 AD), a Persian astrologer whose "Introduction to Astrology" was a standard medieval textbook</note> says in his introduction: that the first sign of this triplicity, namely **Aries**, produces a tempered heat. The second, **Leo**, produces an injurious heat. The third, **Sagittarius**, produces a corrupting heat. When any of these signs falls in the **ascendant** <term>ascendant: the sign rising on the eastern horizon at a specific moment</term> of a conjunction or opposition of the Sun and Moon <note>a New Moon or Full Moon</note> in the winter time, the cold is tempered. In the summer time, there is excessive heat, especially if the lord of that sign is present in that sign or in any of the **angles** <note>the four most powerful houses in a chart: 1st, 4th, 7th, and 10th</note> looking upon the ascendant itself.

Afterward, he says that **Taurus, Virgo, and Capricorn** make the second triplicity because they are of the same nature; all three are feminine, nocturnal <note>night-oriented</note>, earthy, cold, and dry, melancholic <note>associated with black bile and a cool, dry temperament</note>, with a **harsh taste**, and are southern. The lords of this triplicity in the day are: first **Venus**, then the **Moon**, then **Mars**. And in the night: first the **Moon**, then **Venus**, then **Mars**. 

Albumazar says that the first sign of this triplicity <note>Taurus</note> produces a tempered coldness that strengthens animals and crops. The second <note>Virgo</note> produces an injurious coldness. The third <note>Capricorn</note> produces a corrupting coldness. When any of these signs falls in the ascendant of a conjunction or opposition of the Sun and Moon in winter—and especially **Capricorn**—it becomes very cold, most especially if **Saturn** is in that triplicity.

Then he says that **Gemini, Libra, and Aquarius** make the third triplicity, because they are of the same nature. For they are masculine, diurnal, sanguine <note>associated with blood and a warm, moist temperament</note>, hot, and moist, airy, western, and **sweet in taste**. Of these lords, in the day the first is **Saturn**, the second **Mercury**, and the third **Jupiter**. In the night, the first is **Mercury**, the second **Saturn**, and the third **Jupiter**. 

And Albumazar says that the first sign of this triplicity <note>Gemini</note> produces tempered winds that are strengthening and sweet. The second <note>Libra</note> produces harmful winds. The third <note>Aquarius</note> produces winds that scatter animals and crops. When any of these is the ascendant at the hour of a conjunction of the Sun and Moon, winds will move during that lunation; and you should understand the same for an opposition.

Afterward, he says that **Cancer, Scorpio, and Pisces** make the fourth triplicity, because all three are feminine, nocturnal, northern, aquatic, cold, and moist, with a **salty taste**. The lords of this triplicity in the day are: first **Venus**, second **Mars**, and third the **Moon**. In the night, the first is **Mars**, second **Venus**, and third the **Moon**. 

Albumazar says that the first sign of this triplicity...

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of triplicities, specifically their influence on weather, and transitions into the classification of signs as fixed or movable, followed by the geometric "aspects" or relationships between signs.</meta>

...makes the humidity temperate. The second [lord] makes it abundant. The third makes it destructive. When any of these signs <note>the signs of the watery triplicity</note> is in the **ascendant** <term>ascendant: the sign rising on the eastern horizon at a specific moment</term> of the **conjunction** <note>New Moon</note> or **opposition** <note>Full Moon</note> of the sun and moon, rain must be predicted for that month. For it is from this triplicity, and from Venus and the Moon especially, that rains begin. **Abraham ibn Ezra** <note>original: "Abrahā auēnezre," a famous 12th-century Jewish scholar and astrologer</note> says that if in the conjunction or opposition which precedes the entry of the sun into Aries—whether it be the conjunction or the opposition—any of these signs is ascending, and with this the **luminaries** <note>the Sun and Moon</note> are in the **angles** <term>angles: the four most powerful points in a chart: the 1st, 4th, 7th, and 10th houses</term> (that is, in the ascendant, or the midheaven, or the descendant, or the angle of midnight), there will be excessive rain in that year. But if the luminaries are falling away from the angles, rain will not descend in that month nor in the greater part of that year.

And I have experienced many times that when the ascendant of the conjunction is a watery sign, and the moon enters that sign before the opposition, on the same day when the moon enters the degree that was in the ascendant, rain begins; and it lasts at least as long as the moon stays in that sign. You may understand the same regarding windy and cold signs. This I have found through experience; but afterward, I found this in the book of **Abraham ibn Ezra**, and I was greatly rejoiced because I was more certain of the truth. But Abraham says that this is especially true if the ascending sign was one of the angles of the chart for that land; I understand this to refer to the chart for the **revolution of the year** <term>revolution: a chart cast for the moment the Sun enters Aries, used to predict the year's events</term> in that land. For that chart varies across different regions. And if it is not one of the angles of the chart for that land, there will be clouds without rain. The lords of the triplicities are almost the same as the causes of the houses, so I pass over them.

# Four also of these signs.

<margin>Here he shows which signs are movable, which are fixed, and which are middle or common.</margin>
...are movable, and which are fixed, and which are middle or common. And he assigns the causes and provides examples; the text is plain. It should be noted here that **Ptolemy** says in the 22nd proposition of the *Centiloquium* <note>a collection of 100 astrological aphorisms attributed to Ptolemy</note>: "When the moon is in Leo, one must fear to make or wear new clothes." And **Haly** <note>Ali ibn Ridwan, an Egyptian physician and astrologer</note> says in his commentary on the same proposition that all **fixed signs** <term>fixed signs: Taurus, Leo, Scorpio, and Aquarius; they represent stability and persistence</term> are forbidden in this regard, and especially Leo; because besides being fixed, it is a sign of great victory, and signifies the victory of all things which are performed and a bad dominion over them. And he says that by "making," Ptolemy means cutting and sewing. By "wearing," he means putting them on and using them. From this we can take a rule: that all things which we want to be **fixed** <note>permanent or lasting</note> we should begin when the moon is in a fixed sign, such as building houses and the like. And things which we want to change quickly, we should begin when the moon is in a **movable sign**, such as travel and the like.

# The signs are also said to look upon one another.

<margin>First Authority</margin>
<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'D' with internal scrollwork and leaf patterns, set within a double-lined square border.</image-desc>
**The signs are also said to look upon one another.** The author has determined the dignity of the planets which is called triplicity. And because triplicity is derived from the **trine aspect** <term>trine: an aspect of 120 degrees, considered very harmonious</term>, he therefore determines in this part, incidentally, the **aspects** <note>original: "aspectibus," the geometric angles between signs</note>. This can be divided into two parts: in the first part, he determines the aspects. In the second part, the projection of rays. The second part begins: *When, however, a planet is in any sign.* The first part can be divided into four sections according to the four aspects. In the first, he determines the **sextile aspect**. In the second, the **quartile aspect** <note>square aspect, 90 degrees</note>. In the third, the **trine**. In the fourth, the **opposition**. The second begins: *and it looks at the fourth before itself.* The third begins: *it also looks at the fifth before itself.* The fourth begins: *it also looks at the seventh.* He says first that the signs are said to look upon one another in this manner: Every sign looks at the third sign after itself and the third sign before itself, just as **Aries** looks at the sign of **Gemini**, which is the third before it, and the sign of **Aquarius**, which is the third after it. And this aspect is called **sextile** because it occupies a sixth part of the circle, that is, 60 degrees, which is the quantity of two signs. And this aspect is called the aspect of **moderate friendship**.

--- Page 73 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of how zodiac signs relate to one another through geometric "aspects," moving from the sextile aspect to the square, trine, and opposition, and their specific applications in medieval medicine (specifically "crises" in disease).</meta>

The reason can be explained thus: any two signs separated by this distance <note>referring to the sextile aspect of 60 degrees mentioned on the previous page</note> agree in one quality as well as in their gender. For example, **Aries** and **Gemini** agree in heat and in masculinity; likewise **Aries** and **Aquarius**. **Taurus** and **Cancer** agree in coldness and femininity; likewise **Taurus** and **Pisces**, and you may understand the same for all others. Afterward, he [the author] says that any sign beholds the fourth sign before it and the fourth sign after it. This aspect is called **square** <term>square: also called "tetragonus," an aspect of 90 degrees where signs are three signs apart</term> or "the fourth," because it occupies a fourth part of the circle—namely 90 degrees, which is the space of three signs. This aspect is called the "aspect of medium enmity," because any two signs separated in this way usually conflict in one quality, and sometimes in both. They also disagree in gender, for if one is masculine, the other will be feminine.

And this is the reason why in **acute diseases** <note>diseases with a rapid onset and short duration, like a high fever</note>, a **crisis** <term>crisis: a turning point in a disease that determines recovery or death</term> usually occurs when the **Moon** comes to the square aspect of the place where it was at the beginning of the illness. This is especially true if there is a conflict in both qualities, and commonly a healthy crisis occurs then. For example, if the Moon was at the beginning of the sickness in any sign of the fiery **triplicity** <note>Aries, Leo, or Sagittarius</note>, especially in **Aries**, when it reaches the fourth sign <note>Cancer</note> which conflicts in both qualities and in gender, a crisis toward health commonly occurs. For instance, if it was in **Aries**, the square aspect will be in **Cancer**; if in **Leo**, the square aspect will be in **Scorpio**; if it was in **Sagittarius**, the square aspect will be in **Pisces**. In other signs, however, there is only a conflict in one quality and in gender. For example: **Taurus** and **Leo**. **Taurus** is a cold, dry, and feminine sign. **Leo** is hot, dry, and masculine. You will find this conflict in others in their own way. Because of this, crises in these signs sometimes lead to health and sometimes to death, depending on the **fortunate or unfortunate** <note>benefic or malefic planets, like Jupiter or Saturn</note> aspect to the Moon.

Next, he says that any sign beholds the fifth before it and the fifth after it <note>using inclusive counting, this is 120 degrees</note>. This is called the **trine** <term>trine: also called "trinus," an aspect of 120 degrees, considered very harmonious</term> because it occupies a third part of the circle, namely 120 degrees. This is called the "aspect of perfect friendship," because any three signs separated in this way form one **triplicity** and agree in their natures, as was said in the chapter immediately preceding. Afterward, he says: any sign also beholds the seventh from itself—that is, the opposite sign. This is the "aspect of perfect enmity" by reason of opposition. Every planet that is in a certain degree of any sign beholds a planet existing in another sign at the same degree with the same aspect by which those two signs behold each other. The author is speaking of the degrees of the **zodiac**, but according to the teaching of **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century foundational authority on astronomy and astrology</note>, aspects are measured on the **equinoctial** <term>equinoctial: the celestial equator</term>; therefore the text can be explained this way: this should be understood regarding the degrees of the equinoctial. For example: if there is a planet in **Aries** and another in **Gemini**, and the **ascensions** <note>the time or degrees it takes for a sign to rise</note> of the direct circle of the one in **Aries** are subtracted from the ascensions of the direct circle of the one in **Gemini**, if 60 degrees remain, they behold each other with a **sextile** <term>sextile: an aspect of 60 degrees, considered helpful</term> aspect. Understand the other aspects in this same way.

# But when there shall be a planet...

In this section the author touches upon the **projections of rays** <term>projections of rays: how a planet "casts" its influence or light to other parts of the zodiac</term> and dispatches the matter briefly, saying: If there is any planet in a sign, it projects its rays to the same degree of the sign which it beholds by some aspect. For example: if some planet is in the first degree and second minute of **Aries**, it projects its **sextile** rays to the first degree and second minute of **Gemini**. The author was far too brief in this chapter; therefore, it is necessary to dwell a little longer on the explanation of the aspects and the projection of rays. It must first be understood that aspects are considered in three ways. One way is according to equal degrees, and in this way the author has spoken in the text about aspects and projections...

It also introduces the technical concept of how planets project their influence (rays) across the celestial sphere.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of planetary "aspects" (geometric relationships) and how to calculate the "projection of rays"—the influence a planet casts onto other parts of the zodiac. It transitions from a general discussion of sign qualities to specific mathematical methods used by medieval astrologers.</meta>

of the rays. And they are called equal degrees of the zodiac, not considering the ascensions <note>original: "ascensiones"; this refers to how signs rise over the horizon at different speeds depending on the latitude</note>. In the second way, the aspect is considered according to the degrees of the equinoctial <term>equinoctial: the celestial equator, used as a reference for time and "right ascension"</term> in the direct circle; and in this way **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century authority on astronomy and astrology</note> commands one to take the aspects in the 60th proposition of the *Centiloquium* <note>A collection of 100 astrological aphorisms attributed to Ptolemy</note>, where he speaks about the causes of critical days <note>days when a disease reaches a turning point</note>; and in this way the aspect is commonly taken in "interrogations" <note>questions asked of an astrologer to find lost objects or predict outcomes</note> and the "revolutions of the years of the world" <note>predictions for the coming year based on the moment the Sun enters Aries</note>, and in many other matters. In the third way, aspects and the projections of rays are considered according to the method of "directions"; and in this way they are taken in nativities <note>birth charts</note>.

It must be understood, according to the chapter on aspects, that aspects are taken in three ways, and the projections of rays are also taken in as many ways. In one way, the aspects and projections of rays are taken according to the degrees of the circle of signs <note>the zodiac</note> so that the degrees of the zodiac which are between one planet and another are counted; and this is the method which the author sets down in the text. And **Abraham Ibn Ezra** <note>original: "Abrahā auenezre"; a famous 12th-century Jewish scholar and astrologer</note> holds this method. And he says that it has been proven by almost infinite experience that aspects ought to be taken in the zodiac and not in the equinoctial; and this method is very easy. And this was sufficiently exemplified in explaining the text. 

In the second way, aspects are taken in the equinoctial according to the ascensions of the direct circle <term>direct circle: refers to the "Sphaera Recta," where the celestial equator is perpendicular to the horizon (the Earth's equator)</term>, according to which method **Ptolemy** commands one to take them, as was said regarding the text. Therefore, when you wish to calculate aspects according to this method, seek the ascensions of the planet in the direct circle which begin from **Capricorn**; and save them. Then, in the same way, seek the ascensions of the degree of the other planet in the same direct circle; and save them. Then subtract the ascensions of the one which is closer to **Capricorn** according to the order of the signs from the ascensions of the one which is further away; and if 60 degrees remain, they behold each other with a **sextile** aspect. If 90 degrees remain, they behold each other with a **square** aspect. And if 120 degrees remain, they behold each other with a **trine** aspect. For the **opposite** aspect, however, it is not necessary to seek the ascensions; because when two planets are opposite in the zodiac, they are also opposite in the equinoctial. This method is easy; therefore, there is no need to linger much here. 

The third method of aspects and the projection of rays, however, is very difficult, especially for those not practiced in calculations. It must first be understood regarding this method that the projection of rays is taken according to the ascensions of the direct circle when the planet whose rays we wish to project is in the **midheaven** <note>the highest point of the southern sky</note> or in the **angle of the earth** <note>the lowest point of the sky, the northern midnight point</note>. And when it is in the degree of the **rising** <note>ascendant</note> or in the degree of the **setting** <note>descendant</note>, it is taken according to the ascensions of the region or the oblique horizon according to the tables made for that region. If, however, the planet is not in any of these four places, but in the intermediate places, then they are taken as a mixture. 

Therefore, when you wish to project the rays of any planet, consider if that planet whose rays you wish to project is in the midheaven or in the angle of the earth; and then seek the ascensions of that degree in the direct circle, to which you should add 60 degrees if you want the **left sextile radiation**—namely, that which precedes according to the order of the signs from the place of the planet. And reduce the degrees resulting after the addition to equal degrees in the direct circle according to the teaching of the canons of the **Primum Mobile** <note>the "First Mover"; the outermost celestial sphere whose movement drives the others</note>; and the degrees of the zodiac which result therein are the sextile rays. And the same is said for the 60 degrees for sextile radiation; understand the same for 90 for the square radiation, and for 120 for the trine radiation. If, however, you want the **right radiation**—namely, that which precedes from the place of the planet toward the west—subtract 60 degrees from the ascensions of the degree of the planet for the sextile radiation, or 90 for the square, or 120 for the trine. And reduce the number that remains to equal degrees as before, and the degrees of the zodiac which come out from that are the rays. 

For an example in the left radiation, I suppose that **Saturn** is in

<meta>This page continues a technical manual on calculating planetary "aspects" or "rays" (the geometric angles between planets). The previous page introduced the three ways to calculate these: by zodiac degrees, by right ascension (the "direct circle"), and by oblique ascension (for specific latitudes). This page provides the mathematical "how-to" for these calculations using examples involving Saturn and Jupiter.</meta>

At the angle of the Midheaven <term>Midheaven: the highest point of the zodiac at a given time, often called the Tenth House</term> at the end of the sixth degree of Virgo, the ascensions <note>original: "ascensiones"; these are measurements along the celestial equator rather than the zodiac</note> of that degree in the **direct circle** <term>direct circle: the celestial equator in a "right sphere" where the poles are on the horizon, used for universal calculations</term> are 247 degrees and 48 minutes. If I wish to have a **sextile radiation** <note>a "sextile" is an aspect of 60 degrees, considered harmonious</note>, I will therefore add 60 degrees; and there will result 307 degrees and 48 minutes, which I will reduce to equal degrees in the direct circle; and they result in 10 degrees, 14 minutes, and 14 seconds of **Scorpio**. There, in this case, are the left sextile rays of **Saturn**.

Example for the **right sextile radiation** <note>In medieval astrology, "left" (sinister) rays follow the order of the zodiac signs, while "right" (dexter) rays go against the order</note>: I will subtract 60 degrees from the aforesaid ascensions, and there remain 187 degrees and 48 minutes. I will reduce [them] to equal degrees in the direct circle, and they result in 7 degrees, 9 minutes, and 15 seconds of **Cancer**. Therefore, in this proposed case, there are the right sextile rays of **Saturn**.

If, however, the planet whose rays you wish to project is in the degree of the **Orient** <term>Orient: the Ascendant, or the point rising on the eastern horizon</term>, look for the ascensions of the planet's degree through the table of ascensions of the **oblique circle** <term>oblique circle: the celestial sphere as seen from a specific latitude on Earth</term> serving your region. And if you desire a left radiation, add 60 degrees to the ascensions of the planet's degree for a sextile ray; or 90 for a **fourth radiation** <note>also called a "quartile" or square aspect</note>; or 120 for a **trine radiation**.

Example in the left sextile radiation: I suppose that **Jupiter** is in the degree of the Orient at the end of the 10th degree of **Sagittarius**. The ascensions of that degree in the oblique horizon are 275 degrees and 27 minutes. And I wish to have the left sextile radiation; I will add 60 degrees to them, and 335 degrees and 27 minutes will result, which I will reduce to equal degrees in the oblique circle, and they will result in 12 degrees, 44 minutes, and 21 seconds of **Aquarius**. Therefore, there are the sextile rays of **Jupiter**.

Example for the right sextile radiation: I will subtract 60 degrees from the ascensions of **Jupiter's** degree, and 215 degrees and 27 minutes remain. I will reduce [them] to equal degrees, and they result in 25 degrees, 54 minutes, and 50 seconds of **Libra**. There are the sextile rays of **Jupiter**.

If, however, the planet is in the degree of the **Occident** <term>Occident: the Descendant, or the point setting on the western horizon</term>, you will work with the opposite degree; that is, you will take the ascensions of the **nadir** <note>the point 180 degrees opposite on the zodiac</note> of the planet's degree in the oblique horizon, and you will do exactly in the same way as has been said. But after you have reduced [the numbers] to equal degrees, the location of the rays is the location opposite to that place which you find.

And so that it may be more clear, I will put an example of this. I suppose that **Jupiter** is in the degree of the Occident at the end of the 10th degree of **Sagittarius**; I will seek the ascensions of the 10th degree of **Gemini** <note>Gemini is 180 degrees opposite Sagittarius</note> and I find 41 degrees and 9 minutes. I will add 60 degrees for the sextile radiation, and there result 101 degrees and 9 minutes. I will reduce [them] to equal degrees in the oblique horizon, and they result in 2 degrees, 30 minutes, and 45 seconds of **Leo**. Therefore, the rays are in the opposite of this: namely, 2 degrees, 30 minutes, and 45 seconds of **Aquarius**.

If, however, the planet whose rays you wish to project is not in any of the said places, then it is necessary to take the radiation in a "mixed way," according to the proportion of the distance of the planet's degree between one angle and another. The method is this: if the planet whose rays you wish to project is between the **Midheaven** and the **Ascendant**: first take the ascensions of the degree of the Midheaven, which you subtract from the ascensions of the planet's degree in the direct circle, and what remains is the distance from the Midheaven, which you should save.

Then, to the ascensions of the planet's degree in the direct circle, add 60 degrees for the sextile radiation, or 90 for the fourth [aspect], or 120 for the trine; and what results, reduce to equal degrees in the direct circle, and save what results. And let it be called the **radiation of the direct circle**.

Then seek the ascensions of the planet's degree in the oblique horizon by the table of your region, to which add similarly 60 degrees for the sextile radiation, or 90 for the fourth [aspect], or 120 for the trine; and what results, reduce to equal degrees by the tables of your region; and save what comes out, and let it be called the **radiation of the region**.

Then see the difference between the radiation of the direct circle and the radiation of the region; multiply this difference by the distance of the planet's degree from the Midheaven...

I recommend the first method. I set an example in this: that the degree of the Midheaven is the 20th degree of **Leo** complete, and **Saturn** is in the sixth degree of **Virgo** complete. Since I see that the radiation of the direct circle is greater than the radiation of the region, I will subtract the lesser from the greater, and I find the difference to be 20 degrees and 22 minutes, which I will reduce to the same kind <note>converting degrees into minutes: (20 × 60) + 22 = 1222 minutes</note>; and there result 1222. I will also reduce the distance of Saturn from the Midheaven to the same kind. The distance was 15 degrees and 22 minutes; I will reduce it to the same kind: namely, 920 minutes. I will multiply the difference by the distance, and there result 1,126,684 seconds. Then I will reduce the half of the diurnal arc of Saturn to the same kind. The half of the diurnal arc was 100 degrees and 40 minutes; reduced to the same kind, there result 6,040 minutes, by which I will divide the number that came from the multiplication. And what results, reduce to equal degrees in the oblique circle, and let it be called the **radiation of the oblique circle** <note>also referred to as the "radiation of the region"</note>.

Next, look for the ascensions of the planet's degree in the **direct circle** <term>direct circle: the celestial equator as it appears at the Earth's equator (right ascension)</term>; to which add 60 degrees for the sextile radiation, and so on; and what results, reduce to equal degrees in the direct circle; and save what results, and let it be called the **radiation of the direct circle**.

And what results after the addition or subtraction will be the location of the rays.

I set an example in this: suppose the ascending degree is 18° of **Scorpio**, and **Jupiter** is in 10° of **Sagittarius**. I look for the ascensions of the ascending degree at a horizon of 48 degrees <note>likely referring to the latitude of Central European cities like Munich or Vienna</note>, and I find 245 degrees and 55 minutes. Then I look for the ascensions of the degree of Jupiter in that same oblique horizon, and I find 275 degrees and 27 minutes. I will subtract the ascensions of the ascending degree from the ascensions of the degree of Jupiter, and 29 degrees and 32 minutes remain, which is the distance of Jupiter from the ascendant.

Next, I subtracted the ascensions of the degree opposite Jupiter from the ascensions of the degree of Jupiter and halved the remainder; I found 117 degrees and 9 minutes, which is the half of the nocturnal arc of Jupiter's degree. Then I added 60 degrees to the ascensions of Jupiter, and 335 degrees and 27 minutes resulted, which I reduced to equal degrees in the oblique circle, and I found 12 degrees and 45 minutes of **Aquarius**. This is the radiation of the region; I therefore saved it.

Then I sought the ascensions of Jupiter's degree in the direct circle and found 338 degrees and 21 minutes, to which I added 60 degrees, and 398 degrees and 21 minutes resulted. I subtracted one revolution, that is 360 degrees, and 38 degrees and 21 minutes remained, which I reduced to equal degrees in the direct circle, and I found 50 degrees and 57 minutes of Aquarius <note>Note: This refers to the cumulative degrees from the start of the zodiac; 50° 57' corresponds to roughly 21° of Taurus, but the text treats it as a measurement for Aquarius</note>; and this is the radiation of the direct circle.

Then I subtracted the smaller radiation from the larger and found a difference of 6 degrees and 48 minutes. I multiplied this difference by the distance of Jupiter from the ascendant and divided by the half of the nocturnal arc, and I found for the proportional part 1 degree, 42 minutes, and 47 seconds. I subtracted this from the radiation of the region, because that was larger. And there remained after the subtraction 11 degrees, 2 minutes, and 13 seconds of Aquarius; and there is the location of the rays of Jupiter.

If, however, you wish to project the rays toward the **right** <note>original: "versus dextrum"; referring to 'dexter' aspects which are cast backwards against the order of the zodiac signs</note>, then you should find the distance of the planet's degree from the **angle of the earth** <term>angle of the earth: the Imum Coeli (IC), the lowest point of the zodiac directly below the observer</term> by the ascensions of the direct circle, which you find by subtracting the ascensions of the planet's degree in the direct circle from the ascensions of the angle of the earth in the direct circle. And then you should add the proportional part of the two radiations to the radiation of the direct circle if it was less than the radiation of the region, or subtract it if it was greater. The work differs in nothing else except where I instructed to add 60 degrees, etc., you ought to subtract 60, etc.

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for the "projection of rays" (calculating astrological aspects), specifically addressing complex cases where a planet and its aspected point (the "ray") cross different quadrants of the sky, such as moving from the area above the horizon to the area below it.</meta>

the position of the degree is between the ascendant and the **angle of the earth** <term>angle of the earth: the Imum Coeli (IC), or the lowest point of the sky directly opposite the Midheaven</term>. And the opposite degree of the planet is just as far from the ascendant as the planet's degree is from the degree of the **descendant** <term>descendant: the western horizon where planets set</term>: therefore the operation is the same: neither more nor less. But after you have completed the operation, the place of the rays is the place opposite to that place which you found, just as I exemplified for you when the planet is in the degree of the descendant.

¶ If, however, it should happen—as often happens—that the planet's degree is before the angle, and the place where its rays fall is beyond the angle: for example, where the planet's position is between the **midheaven** <term>midheaven: the highest point of the sky, or the meridian</term> and the ascendant, and the rays fall beyond the ascendant, namely, between the ascendant and the angle of the earth. This case occurs quite frequently in the **trine radiation** <term>trine: an aspect of 120 degrees</term>, and many times in the **quadrate** <term>quadrate: a square aspect of 90 degrees</term>, and sometimes in the **sextile** <term>sextile: an aspect of 60 degrees</term>.

The method is this: if the position of the planet whose rays you wish to project is between the midheaven and the ascendant, and its rays must fall beyond the ascendant: look at the distance of the planet from the midheaven by the method previously described; and look at the half of the **diurnal arc** <term>diurnal arc: the path a planet travels above the horizon from its rising to its setting</term> of the planet's degree by the method previously described; and save both, namely the distance and the half of the diurnal arc. 

Then find the ascensions of the planet's degree in the **direct circle** <term>direct circle: the celestial equator as it appears from the Earth's equator; essentially right ascension</term>. Find also the ascensions of the ascending degree in the same direct circle. Then subtract the ascensions of the planet's degree from the ascensions of the ascending degree. And save what remains; let it be called the **part of radiation of the direct circle**. 

Next, find the ascensions of the planet's degree in the **oblique circle** <term>oblique circle: the celestial equator adjusted for a specific geographic latitude</term>; and find the ascensions of the ascending degree in the oblique circle: and subtract the planet's ascensions from the ascensions of the ascending degree: and save what remains, and let it be called the **part of radiation of the region**. 

Then subtract the smaller part of radiation from the larger, and the difference remains; multiply this by the distance of the planet from the midheaven and divide the product by the half of the diurnal arc of the planet's degree, and the result will be the **proportional part**. Add this to the part of radiation of the direct circle if it was smaller than the part of radiation of the region, or subtract it if it was larger: and what results will be the **equated part of radiation**. 

If this part is less than 60 degrees, the sextile rays fall beyond the angle of the ascendant; if it is larger, they fall before the angle. And if this part is less than 90 degrees, the quadrate (fourth) rays fall beyond the angle; if larger, they fall before the angle. And if this part is less than 120 degrees—as is quite frequent—the trine rays fall beyond the angle; if it is larger than 120, they fall before the angle. 

If, therefore, the rays fall beyond the angle, subtract the equated part of radiation from 60 degrees for the sextile radiation, or from 90 for the quadrate radiation, or from 120 for the trine radiation: and add what remains to the ascensions of the ascending degree in the oblique circle, and reduce what results to equal degrees in the oblique circle; the degree of the sign that comes out will be the location of the rays. If you know this operation, you will know how to "direct" a planet existing in one quadrant to a location existing in another quadrant. I have not seen this method explained or set down in any other book.

¶ To make what I have said clearer, I will provide an example of this. I posit that the degree of the midheaven is 10 degrees of Cancer, and **Saturn** is at the 4th degree of Virgo; and the ascending degree in the oblique horizon, in a region whose latitude is 48 degrees, is 7 degrees and 59 minutes of Libra. I sought the ascensions of the degree of the midheaven in the direct circle and found 190 degrees and 53 minutes. Then I sought the ascensions of the degree of Saturn in the right circle and found 245 degrees and 55 minutes. I subtracted the ascensions of the midheaven from the ascensions of Saturn's degree, and there remained 55 degrees and 2 minutes: which is the distance of Saturn from the midheaven. Then I subtracted the ascensions of the degree of Saturn in the oblique circle from the ascensions—

# <image-desc>Decorative historiated initial 'H' containing a scene of a scholar seated at a desk or lectern, wearing a cap and robes, engaged in writing or study.</image-desc> **The five planets also have terms in every sign.**

<margin>
First the author determined...
</margin>
...concerning the three **essential dignities** <term>essential dignities: the specific strengths a planet gains by being in a particular sign or degree</term> of the planets. In this part, the author determines the fourth essential dignity which the planets have in the signs. And it is divided into two parts: because in the first part he sets out the **terms** <term>terms: also called "bounds," these are specific degrees within a zodiac sign where a planet exerts strong influence</term> of the planets. In the second part, he resolves a certain controversy that exists between some regarding the **triplicities** <term>triplicities: a dignity based on the four elements—fire, earth, air, and water—each shared by three signs</term> and the terms. The second part begins there: "Certain people propose..." <note>original: "Quidā p̄ponūt"</note>. The first part can be divided into two parts: so that first, he begins to treat the terms of the planets literally. Second, he provides a table of these terms. The second begins there: "And because of the diversity of these..." <note>original: "ꝛ pp diuersitatē eorūdē"</note>. He says first that the five planets—namely Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, Venus, and Mercury—have terms arranged through various degrees. And the first term is of **Jupiter** from the beginning of **Aries** up to the end of the 6th degree of the same. And from the beginning of the 7th degree of the same Aries up to the end of the 12th is the term of **Venus**. And from the beginning of the 13th degree up to the end of the 20th is the term of **Mercury**. And from the beginning of the 21st up to the end of the 25th is the term of **Mars**. And from the beginning of the 26th up to the end of Aries is the term of **Saturn**. Afterward, he says: and because these degrees are diverse—such that they do not proceed equally nor according to the order of the planets—it would be difficult to hold them in memory; therefore, he placed them in a table as is seen in the text.

<summary>This page concludes a complex mathematical example of calculating planetary aspects (rays) and begins a new section on the "Terms" or "Bounds" of the planets, explaining how signs are subdivided among the five traditional planets.</summary>
<keywords>astrological aspects, sextile, Saturn, ascension, oblique circle, essential dignities, terms, bounds, Aries, Jupiter, Venus, Mercury, Mars</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the technical calculations of planetary "rays" and "projections" discussed on the previous page into a theoretical discussion regarding the hierarchy of essential dignities—specifically the "Triplicities" and "Terms." It then moves on to define the final dignity, the "Faces" or decans.</meta>

# Some prefer triplicities to **terms**. <margin>Here</margin>

He resolves a certain controversy that exists among the wise concerning **triplicities** <term>triplicities: a group of three zodiac signs belonging to the same element (Fire, Earth, Air, Water)</term> and **terms** <term>terms: also known as "bounds," these are specific subdivisions of a zodiac sign ruled by a particular planet</term>. And he says that some prefer triplicities to terms, and some prefer terms to triplicities. He resolves this controversy and says that the lords of the triplicities are stronger regarding the **nourishment of children** <note>original: "nutritionē puerorū"; in medieval astrology, triplicity rulers were used to determine if an infant would survive and thrive</note>; because in **nativities** <term>nativities: birth charts</term> they signify nourishment, and from them the indication is taken whether the child is likely to live or not. However, the lords of the terms are stronger in **direction** <term>direction: a predictive technique where degrees of the chart are "moved" forward to time future events</term>; because when the degree of the **ascendant** <term>ascendant: the sign rising on the eastern horizon at birth</term> of any nativity is directed, it is directed to the terms of the **fortunes and misfortunes** <note>the "fortunes" are Jupiter and Venus; the "misfortunes" or malefics are Saturn and Mars</note>. And the lord of that term is called the "divider" or "disposer" for as long as the direction lasts in that term; and if there is any planet there or its rays, it will be a participant. This will be discussed more extensively in what follows, the Lord leading the way.

It should be noted here regarding the terms that there was discord among the ancient wise men concerning them. And there were three famous opinions about the terms: one was of the Indians, another of the Egyptians, and the third of the Chaldeans. **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century author of the Tetrabiblos, the foundational text for Western astrology</note> in the 8th proposition of the 2nd part of the **Quadripartitum** <note>original: "q̄dripartiti"; another name for Ptolemy's Tetrabiblos</note> most approves of the terms of the Egyptians and says he found an ancient book about terms which, due to its great age, was mostly destroyed. And that book agreed with the terms of the Egyptians; and in that book, the sum of the degrees of the terms of each planet was as great as the number of its **greater years** <term>greater years: specific long time-cycles attributed to each planet in ancient chronocrator systems</term> which they give in nativities. For example: the greater years of Saturn are 57 years; of Jupiter, 79; of Mars, 66; of Venus, 82; of Mercury, 76; and all these collected together make 360. If you wish to test this, compute the degrees of all the terms of any planet—for example, Jupiter—and you will find the number of its years, namely 79, and so on for the others.

**Haly** <note>likely Ali ibn Ridwan, a prominent 11th-century Arabic commentator on Ptolemy</note> in the commentary on the 206th proposition of the first part of the *Quadripartitum* says: Those of Egypt were magical wise men who lived in ancient times; for they were of great study and exercise in all sciences and wisdoms by which a person can help themselves. And we know this through the chronicles of the ancient wise men according to how they spoke of them, and through those things of their works which have remained from many thousands of years until the present day. Nevertheless, I say that which I saw from one experience which I found in the place called the **Eye of the Sun** <note>original: "oculus solis"; likely referring to Heliopolis in Egypt</note>; for in a short time I saw great miracles. These are the words of Haly; however, he is silent about the experience itself. In the **Centiloquium** <note>a collection of 100 astrological aphorisms falsely attributed to Ptolemy</note>, in the commentary on that proposition "the faces of this world," he tells of an experience regarding a **seal of Scorpio** <note>original: "sigillo scorpionis"; an astrological talisman engraved when the Moon was in Scorpio, often used for healing or protection</note> made in stone according to what can be seen there; and let whoever wishes to see it look there.

---

<margin>Here he determines the fifth and last essential dignity that the planets have in the signs, which is called the **faces**. And it can be divided into three parts: in the first part he puts the division and ordering of the faces. In the second part he teaches how to find the faces by numbers. In the third part he provides a table of them. The second part begins: "When you have the degree in any sign." The third is at the end where he provides the table.</margin>

# **Faces** of the signs.

<image-desc>A large ornamental drop cap 'F' set within a square frame, decorated with intricate foliate scrollwork and organic patterns.</image-desc>

**Faces** of the signs. He says that any sign is divided into three equal parts which are called the three **faces** <term>faces: also called decans, these are 10-degree subdivisions of a zodiac sign</term>, and each face is 10 degrees. And these faces begin from the beginning of **Aries** and proceed according to the order of the signs and the planets. Thus, the first face is of **Mars**, namely the first 10 degrees. The second face of Aries, namely the following 10 degrees, are of the **Sun**, who succeeds Mars in the order of the spheres. The third is of **Venus**, who succeeds the Sun. And the first face of **Taurus** is of **Mercury**, who succeeds Venus. The second of the **Moon**, who succeeds Mercury. The third of **Saturn**. And the first of **Gemini** is of **Jupiter**, and so on according to this order.

<summary>This page discusses the hierarchy of astrological dignities, specifically the "Terms" and "Faces," citing ancient Egyptian and Chaldean sources preserved by Ptolemy and Haly. It explains the mathematical relationship between planetary terms and their "greater years" and introduces the system of 10-degree "faces" ruled by planets in descending order of their celestial spheres.</summary>

<keywords>triplicities, terms, faces, decans, Ptolemy, Haly, Quadripartitum, greater years, Egyptian astrology, planetary dignities, Aries, Taurus, Gemini, seal of Scorpio, Eye of the Sun</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the technical definitions of triplicities and terms into a practical calculation for determining the "Faces" (decans) of the planets. It then moves into a qualitative discussion of planetary "fortitudes" (strengths) and the symbolic representation of the zodiac signs.</meta>

...up to the end of the signs. Afterward, he teaches how to find through numbers which planet rules any **face** <term>face: also known as a decan, this is a 10-degree subdivision of a zodiac sign; there are three faces per sign</term>. He says that when you have the degree of any sign and wish to know which planet's face is in that degree, compute the number of signs from the beginning of Aries up to the beginning of the proposed sign. Multiply 그 number by three, and to the resulting product, add the completed faces of the proposed sign. For example: if 10 full degrees have passed, add one; if 20 are complete, add two. Then, subtract 7 from the total as many times as possible. Count whatever remains starting from Mars according to the order of the planets <note>The "Chaldean order" of planets used here is: Mars, Sun, Venus, Mercury, Moon, Saturn, Jupiter</note>. Where the number ends, the following planet is the lord of the sought face.

For example, I suppose that I have the 15th degree of Leo and I want to know whose face it is. I know that from the beginning of Aries there are 4 complete signs. I will therefore multiply 4 by 3, which equals 12. And I know that in Leo, one complete face has passed; therefore I will add one, and there will be 13 complete faces from the beginning of Aries. I subtract seven from that and 6 remain. I give the first unit to Mars, the second to the Sun, the third to Venus, the fourth to Mercury, the fifth to the Moon, and the sixth to Saturn. Therefore, the following face—which was not yet completed—will belong to Jupiter. 

Afterward, he provides a table, the use of which is easy. For you look for the name of that sign whose lord of the face you seek, and you will find directly who is the lord of the first face, who of the second, and who of the third.

<margin>After the author determined the [dignities...]</margin> 

And because, with God’s help, he has completed the discussion of the **dignities** <term>dignities: the various conditions (house, exaltation, etc.) that give a planet power in a specific sign</term> of the planets in the signs, in this part he determines the **fortitudes** <term>fortitudes: the specific numerical or qualitative strengths a planet gains from its position</term> which they have in those dignities. This can be divided into two parts: in the first part, he shows how many "strengths" a planet has in any given dignity. In the second part, he compares these dignities to sensible things <note>Meaning he uses real-world metaphors like kings or homeowners to explain abstract concepts</note>. The second part begins at: "Some say of this matter."

The first part can be divided into two: first, he connects to what is to be said; second, he follows through. He says that with God’s help he has finished with the dignities. Now he wishes to show how many strengths each has in those same dignities. Afterward, he says that the **Lord of the House** <note>the planet that "owns" the sign, like Mars in Aries</note> has five strengths. The **Lord of Exaltation** has four. The **Lord of Triplicity** has three. The **Lord of the Term** has two. The **Lord of the Face** has one.

Afterward, he repeats that controversy regarding the terms. He sets out a comparison for the dignities of the planets, saying that some have made such a comparison: a planet being in its own house is like a man being in his own home and his own domain. A planet in its exaltation is like a man in his kingdom and his glory—this is clear in the text. From this text, it can be noted that if any planet suffers a great misfortune while in its own house or exaltation, this is considered very detestable; just as it would be very detestable if a king were conquered in his own kingdom and in the strongest place of his realm. Therefore, when the **luminaries** <note>the Sun and the Moon</note> are eclipsed in their own houses or exaltations, it is an especially bad sign.

### The figures of the signs follow.

The figures of the signs follow. Previously, the author determined the dignities of the planets in the signs and the strengths they have in those dignities. In this part, he determines the figures or forms of the signs themselves; that is, he shows us which signs have human forms and which have the forms of other animals. He says that certain signs are called **rational** <term>rational: signs associated with human intellect because their constellations represent human figures</term>, namely those whose images are shaped after the likeness of humans. These are Gemini, Virgo, Aquarius, Libra, and half [of Sagittarius...] <note>The text likely refers to the human half of the Centaur in Sagittarius</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the zodiac signs, moving from the mathematical calculation of their "faces" to a qualitative description of their physical and vocal characteristics. ...the first part of **Sagittarius**. And these are said to be those having beautiful voices. Their power is greater when they are in the East. Some of the signs are said to be **double-bodied** <note>original: "balas," likely a variant of "bicorporea" or "dualia"</note>, namely **Gemini**, **Virgo**, and **Pisces**. Some of them are called **four-footed**, namely **Sagittarius**, **Leo**, **Aries**, **Taurus**, and **Capricorn**. Some of these are domestic, namely those figured according to the shapes of domestic animals; these are **Aries**, **Taurus**, and **Capricorn**. Their power appears more when they are in the South.

However, the power of **Virgo**, **Capricorn**, and **Aquarius** appears more when they are in the North. Some of the signs are seen as defective, ugly, and crooked, namely **Aries**, **Taurus**, **Cancer**, **Scorpio**, and **Pisces**; their power appears more in the West. Some are indeed **sterile**, namely **Gemini**, **Leo**, and **Virgo**. Some are said to have **few children**, namely **Aries**, **Taurus**, **Libra**, **Sagittarius**, **Capricorn**, and **Aquarius**. Some of these signs are very **lustful**, namely **Aries**, **Taurus**, **Leo**, and **Capricorn**. And some are said to have **many children**, namely **Cancer**, **Scorpio**, and **Pisces**.

Some of the signs are said to have a **half-voice**: namely those that have the voices of bleating, roaring, and lowing animals, such as **Aries**, **Taurus**, **Leo**, **Capricorn**, and the last part of **Sagittarius**. Some of the signs are said to lack a voice entirely, namely those figured according to the images of voiceless animals, such as **Cancer**, **Scorpio**, and **Pisces**. **Haly Abenragel** <note>A famous 11th-century Arab astrologer, also known as Al-Rijal</note> briefly explains the forms of the signs, saying: some signs are in the form of humans, others in the form of beasts, others in the form of beasts of prey, and others in the form of reptiles. Those in human form are **Gemini**, **Libra**, **Aquarius**, and **Virgo**. In the form of beasts are **Aries**, **Taurus**, **Capricorn**, and **Sagittarius**. In the form of beasts of prey—that is, rapacious animals—is **Leo**. In the form of reptiles are **Cancer**, **Scorpio**, and **Pisces**.

# And each one of the signs has its own proper meaning.

<margin>
The author first determined...
</margin>
...the shapes of the signs. In this part, he shows us what the signs govern regarding the members of the human body, the regions of the world, and the planets. It can be divided into two parts: in the first part, he does what has just been said; in the second, he determines the "pains" <note>likely referring to planetary debilitations or "afflictions"</note> of the planets in the signs. The second part begins there: *And the same planets shall signify pain.*

The first part can be divided into twelve sections, according to the twelve signs. What is said in the text is clear, except that the names of the regions are not familiar to us. It should be noted here that **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century Greco-Egyptian polymath</note> divides the whole habitable earth into four parts. According to Ptolemy’s division, one can imagine a line passing from East to West through the middle of the inhabited world, so that this line divides the southern part from the northern part. **Haly** says that this line is 36 degrees distant from the **equator** <note>original: "equinoctialis"</note>. According to this division, it is about 30 degrees distant from the farthest habitation in the North, so that the total width contains 66 degrees. And such is the distance from the equator to the **Arctic Circle**; indeed, it is 66 degrees, 26 minutes, and 30 seconds. Therefore, Haly says "or thereabouts."

He says that at the end of this line toward the West is the **Passage of Hercules** <note>The Strait of Gibraltar, traditionally known as the Pillars of Hercules</note>. This is where one crosses into Spain, and the sea there is so narrow that someone standing on one shore can see someone on the other. And there are three islands there, one of which can be seen by those crossing into Spain by...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of astrological correspondences, shifting from the physical characteristics of the signs to their geographical distribution across the known world. It introduces the "Pillars of Hercules" concept and the division of the earth into four quarters ruled by specific planetary combinations.</meta>

...the land of the west. And there stands in it an idol holding keys in its hand to demonstrate that beyond that place there is no habitation. And in each of the two other islands stands an idol in the same manner. And in the eastern part, at the end of the aforesaid line, stand idols in the same way, holding keys and demonstrating that there is no habitation beyond that place toward the east. **Haly** <note>original: "Haly"; referring to Haly Abenragel or Haly Heben Rodan, influential Arab astrologers whose works were translated into Latin</note> says that **Hercules** placed these idols as a sign that he himself had acquired the whole world. The length of the aforesaid line, according to what **Haly** says, is 180 degrees. 

Having seen these things, according to the opinion of **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century Greco-Egyptian astronomer</note>, imagine this line to be divided through the middle so that 90 degrees remain toward the east and another 90 toward the west. And imagine one line passing through the poles of the world through the middle of the division of the line mentioned above—specifically, so that it passes through the middle of the habitable earth according to latitude. However, the first line was said to pass through the middle of the habitable earth according to longitude. 

It is clear, therefore, that the two aforesaid lines which divide the whole habitable earth into four parts meet at the place of intersection of the lines, which is the point in the middle of the habitable earth. This division being understood, it is easy to see the opinion of **Ptolemy** in the partition of the signs and the lands. 

**Ptolemy** says, therefore, that these three signs—**Aries**, **Leo**, and **Sagittarius**—which make the first <term>triplicity</term> <note>A group of three zodiac signs associated with the same classical element; in this case, Fire</note>, belong to the fourth part of the habitable earth which is between the north and the west. And **Jupiter** governs that quarter because he is northern, and **Mars** has participation with him because he belongs to the western part. 

And the three signs of the second triplicity—namely **Taurus**, **Virgo**, and **Capricorn**—belong to the part which is between the south and the east; and **Venus** governs it because she is southern, and **Saturn** has participation with her because he belongs to the eastern part. 

And the three signs of the third triplicity—namely **Gemini**, **Libra**, and **Aquarius**—belong to the part which is between the east and the north; and **Saturn** governs it because he belongs to the east, and **Jupiter** has participation with him because he belongs to the north. 

And the three signs of the fourth triplicity—namely **Cancer**, **Scorpio**, and **Pisces**—belong to the part which is between the south and the west; and **Mars** governs it because he belongs to the west, and **Venus** has participation with him. 

From the aforesaid, anyone will be able to conjecture to which sign any land belongs. For he will know through the longitude and latitude of the region which quarter it is in. For example: all land whose latitude is greater than 36 degrees, and its longitude from the west is less than 90 degrees, is of the northwestern quarter, and so on for others in their own manner. 

Therefore, having identified the quarter in which your land is located, you know that one sign out of the three signs of that quarter's triplicity relates to that land. And you can know which of those three relates to it through the manners and customs of the people living in it. For example: you know that the land of the Christians is in the northwestern part; I know that **Aries**, **Leo**, and **Sagittarius** rule over it. And if I wish to know which part of it each of these signs rules, I look to the manners and conditions of the people, seeing with which signs they greatly agree. 

It is established, moreover, that the **Romans** are the most famous and noble of that quarter, and therefore it is appropriate that the most noble sign among these three be attributed to them; and this is **Leo**, the house of the **Sun**. It is also established that the **Germans** <note>original: "almani"</note> are angry and furious men of the nature of **Mars**; therefore it is appropriate that **Aries**, the house of **Mars**, be attributed to them. It is also established that the **Spaniards** <note>original: "hispani"</note> are good soldiers of great practice in arms; therefore it is appropriate that **Sagittarius** be attributed to them, which is the sign of military affairs and wars. 

And **Ptolemy** places this partition in the text in the second part of the **Quadripartitum** <note>original: "quadripartiti"; also known as the *Tetrabiblos*, Ptolemy's foundational work on astrology</note>. For he says thus: "and the provinces agreeing with Aries are the land having..."
