<meta>This page continues the technical exposition of planetary natures, moving from the specific years assigned to planets in astrology to the detailed physical and symbolic qualities of Saturn and Jupiter. It draws heavily on authorities like Ptolemy, Al-Farghani (Alfraganus), and Ibn Abi al-Rijal (Haly Abenragel).</meta>

It must be understood that these refer to the years that the planets grant for the duration of things—namely in <term>sects<note>original: "sectis" — in medieval astrology, this refers to the lifespan of religions, empires, or major social movements governed by "Great Conjunctions."</note></term> and other great matters. However, when it mentions the "greater," "middle," and "lesser" years, it should be understood as the years that the planets grant in the birth charts of men for their lifespan. As I speak of Saturn, so you should understand the other planets. Ptolemy says in the 82nd proposition of the first part <note>Likely referring to the *Centiloquium*, a collection of 100 astrological aphorisms attributed to Ptolemy.</note> that the greatest and strongest work of Saturn is to cool and to dry slightly because of the intensity of its cold. This is seen to be because it is very far removed from the heat of the Sun and the moist vapors of the Earth. 

Here, <term>Albumazar<note>Abu Ma'shar (787–886), a highly influential Persian astrologer.</note></term> refutes Ptolemy, and, God willing, I will set down his reasons at the end of this part concerning the natures of the planets. Haly says in his commentary on the aforementioned proposition that Ptolemy means that Saturn performs this work because its influence is far removed from the work of the Sun, which is the work of heat, and from the Moon, whose work is to attract humors. For when one examines the relationship of Saturn with the Sun and Moon, one finds that it is contrary to both; for this reason, he said it is appropriate for it to be cold and dry. And Ptolemy himself explained this comparison in what follows. Ptolemy says that knowing the power of this planet and the others is achieved by rectifying the figures <note>astrological charts or aspects</note> in respect to the Sun and Moon, since we see some of them changing the air in one manner of change, and others in another manner, in various sequences of increasing and decreasing. 

Haly says in the commentary: "If you have a memory of what we told you about how we can prove the powers of the stars, you need no explanation in this. But if you have forgotten this, listen. I tell you that the Sun and Moon are used because their effects are more apparent than the effects of other stars and are more manifest to us; the effects of the remaining stars seem hidden. It is fitting that you look to the stars that have a relationship with the two luminaries <note>The Sun and the Moon</note> through 'figures'—that is, through <term>aspects<note>Geometric angles between planets that determine how they interact.</note></term>—to see if they increase in their effects or decrease because of them. Thus you shall proceed by observing them all until you know the certainty of all of them, not just once but many times. And if you find that they always follow one manner of operating, you will know for certain what kind of power they have."

<term>Haly Abenragel<note>Ibn Abi al-Rijal (died c. 1037), an Arab astrologer known for his book *Pre-eminent on the Judgments of the Stars*.</note></term> says of the nature of Saturn: Saturn is a planet that is old, great, weary, despised, and characterized by anxieties, long sadnesses, and illnesses. Its nature is cold and dry and is likened to <term>melancholy<note>In the medieval theory of the four humors, melancholy was the cold and dry fluid associated with sadness and the earth element.</note></term>, which is governed by all humors, yet none of them govern it. When it is the <term>almuten<note>The planet that holds the strongest dignity and power in a specific birth chart.</note></term> of any birth, and is itself in Cancer, the person born will have a most hideous face and be a marvelous creature in form and appearance, so much so that all who see him or hear talk of him will be terrified and amazed. <term>Alfraganus<note>Al-Farghani (died c. 860), a famous astronomer who calculated the sizes and distances of the planets.</note></term> says that the body of Saturn is 91 times as large as the Earth.

<margin>Here he sets down the nature</margin>
# Jupiter, the fortune, masculine.
<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'J' featuring a stylized letter within a rectangular frame with floral and scroll motifs.</image-desc>
Of Jupiter, and what is said in the text is clear. The power of Jupiter is a work of a temperate <term>complexion<note>The physical and psychological constitution of a person or planet; here, a healthy balance of heat and moisture.</note></term>, and the place of its motion is midway between the coldness of Saturn and the heat of Mars; because of this, it happens that it heats and moistens. Haly says in the commentary: "Many believed, when they heard this word of Ptolemy, that the works of the stars according to his opinion followed the locations of the <term>spheres<note>The concentric celestial shells in the geocentric model of the universe.</note></term> according to how one exists above another, as <term>Hiza Auenquiton<note>Likely a corruption of Ibn al-Haytham (Alhazen).</note></term> and <term>Al-Kindi<note>The "Philosopher of the Arabs" (c. 801–873), who wrote extensively on astrology and physics.</note></term> and others believed, who hindered us with such reasons. I, however, say that if the works of the stars..."

<keywords>Saturn, Jupiter, Ptolemy, Haly Abenragel, Albumasar, Alfraganus, planetary years, almuten, melancholy, planetary spheres, Al-Kindi</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the natures of the planets, moving from the coldness of Saturn to the temperate nature of Jupiter and the fiery heat of Mars. It addresses the philosophical tension between Aristotelian physics—which claimed celestial bodies have no elemental qualities—and astrological practice.</meta>

...are according to nature. How can <term>quadrivial propositions<note>original: "propositiões q̄druuiales" — refers to the Quadrivium, the four mathematical sciences of arithmetic, geometry, music, and astronomy, which were seen as interconnected.</note></term> be made when the roots of these two sciences are different? One is subordinate to the other. Furthermore, if all celestial bodies are not of any "complexion" <note>Humoral temperament: hot, cold, moist, or dry.</note>—neither hot, moist, cold, nor dry, as **Aristotle** proved—how can the complexion of **Jupiter** be temperate just because its sphere exists between those of **Mars** and **Saturn**? For if you look well into all of **Ptolemy’s** works, you will find that things are not as they [the critics] believed. 

And after he taught us the way by which the virtues of the stars might be known, he addresses this in another way in this place, saying that when we test the manner of the work that is performed in the air surrounding us, we find that Jupiter is a mediator between the great coldness produced by **Saturn** and the great heat produced by **Mars**. This affirms what he said: that it heats and moistens. **Haly Abenragel** <note>Ibn Abi al-Rijal (died c. 1037), a famous Arab astrologer whose work was central to medieval European astrology.</note> says Jupiter is the planet of equality, community, goodness, improvement, intellect, sense, and piety; because it is temperate and balanced, and a "fortune" <note>A "Greater Benefic" planet that brings good luck.</note> by its aspect and physical conjunction, it signifies goodness, improvement, law, simplicity, and chastity. It guides and does not condemn; it populates and does not destroy; it abhors Saturn and its natures. When God began to create the world, it was in its ascending house; it shines in **Aries**, **Taurus**, and **Gemini**, and its light diminishes in **Cancer**, **Leo**, and **Virgo**. It is obscured in **Libra**, **Scorpio**, and **Sagittarius**, and its obscuration diminishes in **Capricorn**, **Aquarius**, and **Pisces**. **Alfraganus** <note>Al-Farghani (died c. 860), a Persian astronomer who calculated the sizes and distances of the planets.</note> says that the body of Jupiter is 95 times as large as the Earth.

# Mars the Masculine <note>original: "Ars masculinus" — likely a scribal or transcription error for "Ares," the Greek name for the planet Mars.</note>

<margin>Here he sets down the nature of Mars as it appears in the text. Ptolemy says:</margin>
**Mars** properly dries and, by the force of its nature, burns because it has a fiery color, and because it is near the Sun, and because the sphere in which the Sun resides is placed beneath it. **Haly** says in his commentary: **Ptolemy** wishes to say that the work of Mars's virtue—that is, what we experience—we find to be of great dryness. Regarding what he said, that the virtue of Mars burns because it has a fiery color, I say that the stars are of a "simple substance" and not composed of the natures of different elements. The appearance of its color is "true" because there is nothing there to change the color suitable to that substance. 

**Ptolemy**, however, teaches us through this statement a rule by which we may know the virtue of the stars, which we cannot understand through ourselves, but we can understand through their color. For in this statement there is a doubt that we must resolve. Many believe that color does not show the virtue of a thing, and this is true in earthly things because of the mixing of elements within them. However, in celestial things, it is not so, because their substance is in no way composed of any element. 

And because of this, **Ptolemy** returned to proving this virtue in another way, saying: "and because it exists near the Sun, and because the sphere in which the Sun is, is placed beneath it." He means to say that this thing happens to be true because the virtue of Mars burns since it exists near the Sun; and because the Sun is beneath it, and its virtue is mixed with that of the Sun, it becomes hot and dry, so much so that it burns without temperance. **Haly Abenragel** says Mars is a hot, dry, and fiery planet: a destroyer, angry, victorious, loving to kill and slaughter, fond of quarrels and litigation. It is quickly provoked to strong anger; it puts its whole heart into the things it must do; it starts battles and destroys populations. Its <term>exaltation<note>The zodiac sign where a planet's influence is strongest.</note></term> is in the house of Saturn—namely, in **Capricorn**—which signifies outcries, hindrances, foolish people, and wars. **Saturn** matches **Mars** in misfortune, but resists it in coldness and darkness. And they incline toward the **Sun** out of love for it, and are defended by its help.

<summary>This page describes Jupiter as a temperate mediator between the extremes of Saturn and Mars, listing its positive social and moral attributes. It then introduces Mars as a "burning" planet, explaining that its fiery color reflects its true nature as a simple celestial substance influenced by the Sun.</summary>
<keywords>Jupiter, Mars, Ptolemy, Haly Abenragel, Alfraganus, temperate, astrology, celestial physics, fiery, color of stars, Quadrivium</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of planetary natures. The preceding text concluded the description of Mars, noting its intense heat and its relationship to the Sun's influence. The narrative now shifts to the Sun, described as the central "King" of the celestial spheres, and begins the section on Venus.</meta>

...because the Sun exalts <note>original: "exaltat" — in astrology, a sign where a planet's influence is particularly strengthened.</note> Mars in its own house and governs it through the heat and dryness that Mars receives from it. Its property and nature agree with and approach the property and nature of the Sun. He [the person influenced by Mars] is ignorant and forgetful, of little intellect and diminished sense, nor does he consider the ends of things. Through Mars, and through its ascent and descent in its circle, the heat of summer and the cold of winter increase and decrease every year. **Alfraganus** <note>Al-Farghani (9th century), an astronomer whose "Elements of Astronomy" was a primary source for medieval scholars.</note> says that Mars contains the mass of the earth once, plus a half and an eighth part.

---

<margin>Here he sets forth the nature of the Sun.</margin>

# The Sun: a fortune by aspect.

**T**he Sun is a "fortune" <note>A "benefic" or lucky planet.</note> by its aspect <note>The angular relationship between planets; the Sun’s light is beneficial from a distance.</note>. Here he sets forth the nature of the Sun, and the text does not require an explanation. **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), the foundational authority for medieval astronomy and astrology.</note> says it is a known thing that the work of the Sun's substance is to heat and to provide a certain amount of dryness. These effects are felt more properly and are more easily known than all others because of the Sun's great size and because of the manifest changes it causes through the seasons of the year; for the more it approaches the zenith above our heads, the more it heats. **Haly Abenragel** says: "Know that the Sun is the light and candle of heaven, the governor of the world, and the maker of the seasons." Through it, the planets become "oriental" and "occidental" <note>Referring to whether they rise before the sun in the east or set after it in the west.</note>; through it, they become visible or hidden; and through it, every moving thing is moved. Through the Sun, every living thing is born, every leaf grows, and every fruit ripens. It is the great spirit of heaven, giving life to the [Zodiacal] signs; and whatever sign the Sun is in holds superiority over the other signs. For the Sun vivifies and illuminates that sign, giving it strength and heat, and applies the heat, strength, and virtue of that sign to the earth. Its nature and deeds appear in all things, in all living creatures, and in those existing upon the earth. And when the Sun departs from the sign in which it was staying, that sign remains like the body of a dead man. 

It is a planet of great dominion, power, nobility, height, and magnitude. It is a "fortune" by its aspect, but a "misfortune" <note>original: "infortuna" — a malefic influence.</note> by physical conjunction. For you see that when any planet is joined to it, the Sun burns it <note>This refers to "combustion," where a planet within 8.5 degrees of the Sun is considered weakened or "burned up" by its power.</note>, overcomes it, and extinguishes its light and glow. Its place in heaven is the fourth—that is, the middle—of the seven planets, like a wise king who maintains his kingdom through his senses and, by careful consideration, places his seat in the middle of his kingdom so that he may reach all sides. 

The Sun gave to **Mars** its military service, making him the leader of its army, because the sphere of the Sun is below the sphere of Mars, and its nature and heat agree with the nature and heat of Mars. To **Jupiter**, it gave its judgments because of his temperance, honesty, and the health of his "complexion" <note>The balance of his qualities (hot, cold, moist, dry).</note>, and because there is no evil quality nor sinful nature in him. It gave the kingdom to **Saturn** because all planets send their lights and their "considerations" <note>Astrological influences or aspects.</note> to him. To **Venus**, it gave the collection of revenues and the buying and selling of goods, because her sphere is joined to the Sun's and they are neighbors. To **Mercury**, it gave the office of the scribe, because his status is like that of a king’s secretary who goes when the king goes and sits when the king sits. To the **Moon**, it gave the office of the messenger <note>original: "algazilaticum" — from the Arabic *al-wazir* or Spanish *alguacil*, meaning a bailiff, officer, or messenger who carries out a king's orders.</note>, for she is like the king's bailiff who carries out his commands and brings them wherever he is ordered. **Alfraganus**, on the authority of **Ptolemy**, says that the body of the Sun contains the body of the earth one hundred and sixty-six times and a little more.

---

# Venus: a fortune, a nocturnal female.

**V**enus is a "fortune," a nocturnal female. Here he sets forth the nature of Venus, and it is clear from the text what he says. **Ptolemy** says Venus performs the same works as **Jupiter** because of the temperance of her complexion, except that she is contrary to Jupiter in one thing: for she heats...

It also includes astronomical data from Alfraganus regarding the relative sizes of Mars and the Sun compared to Earth.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of planetary natures. The previous page ended while describing the Sun's influence on the seasons and its proximity to the zenith. This page completes the discussion on the Sun's physical qualities before transitioning to a detailed characterization of Venus and the beginning of the section on Mercury.</meta>

...around it <note>Continuing from the previous page's discussion of the Sun's influence.</note> because it exists near the sun and moistens according to the moon because of the great light that this planet has, for it attracts to itself the vapors that are raised from the humors <note>In medieval science, "humors" refers to the bodily fluids or moisture of the earth.</note> of the earth. **Haly** <note>original: "Haly in commēto" — refers to the commentator Haly Heben Rodan (Ali ibn Ridwan), an 11th-century Egyptian physician and astrologer.</note> says in the commentary that he means its heat is much less than that of Jupiter; and because of the humidity it produces, we say it is in a way cold and more humid. As for the statement that it exists around the sun, he said this because its power is united with that of the sun. And regarding what he said about it moistening like the moon: he means that Venus produces humidity around itself just as the moon does. And where he mentioned "great light," he means that its moderate heat causes the humors to flow. **Haly Abenragel** <note>Albohazen Haly (965–1066), a famous Arab astrologer whose work "The Complete Book on the Judgment of the Stars" was highly influential.</note> says Venus is a "fortune" <note>A lucky or beneficial planet.</note> that is cold, humid, nocturnal, cheerful, and joyful; she is of good appetite, clear, and beautiful. She loves embraces, songs, eating, drinking, and vices; she is gentle and of few movements. She is the "significator" <note>In astrology, the planet that represents or "points to" a specific person or topic.</note> of women, and of lying with them, and of love, friendship, and society. She receives Mars through the nature of fornication and clarity, but she abhors him because of the property of his nature, which is heat and dryness. She separates herself from him and repels his ill fortune from herself through her gentleness, good words, pleasant comfort, and mild speech. She agrees with Saturn in coldness and in the suitability she has with him in the sign of Libra; for it is the "house" <note>domus: the sign a planet rules.</note> of one and the "exaltation" <note>exaltatio: the sign where a planet's power is most elevated.</note> of the other. However, she disagrees with him in her habits, and in his sorrows and pains, and because Saturn has little use for women. The state of Venus compared to the Sun is like the state of a woman toward a man. **Alfraganus** <note>Al-Farghani, the 9th-century astronomer.</note> says that the body of Venus is one part out of 37 parts of the earth’s mass.

### Mercury: mixed.

<margin>
Here he sets forth the nature of Mercury, and it is clear in the text.
</margin>

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'M' decorated with intricate floral and foliate scrollwork patterns.</image-desc>

**Ptolemy** says of Mercury: the dryness and humidity of Mercury are in equal measure; for sometimes it dries and drives away humors because it is never far from the heat of the sun at any time, and sometimes it moistens because it is positioned above the sphere of the moon, which is closer to the earth than all other spheres. It changes between these two states and causes winds because of its evening changes <note>Referring to Mercury's rapid movement and appearance as an evening or morning star.</note> around the sun. **Haly** says in the commentary: he means that Mercury's work is not one single thing, since it sometimes dries and sometimes moistens. When it dries, it is when it "applies" <note>An astrological term for one planet moving toward an aspect with another.</note> to the sun, and then its work in heating and drying is joined with that of the sun. Its moistening, however, is due to its power being mixed with that of the moon positioned below it, so that its work is like the work of the moon. And when Ptolemy makes a comparison of Mercury’s work with that of the sun, he found that it sometimes performed a work like the sun's. And when he makes a comparison with the moon's work, he found that it sometimes performed a work like the moon's. He tested this many times and found that it always performed one of these two works, and he knew for certain that its work follows the work of the other stars with which it is involved; it has no work proper to itself <note>Mercury was considered a "chameleon" planet that took on the nature of whatever planet it was near.</note>. **Haly Abenragel** says: Mercury is the planet of spells, violence, documents, writing, calculations, and sciences; he is hot and dry, of a changeable form and nature: masculine with masculines, feminine with feminines, a fortune with fortunes, an ill-fortune with ill-fortunes. He is well-reasoned, well-spoken, bold in speech, of beautiful appearance, and of a composed person; he loves books and calculations. Masteries please him, as do well-made things, verified reasons, books, and sciences; he is agile in movement and of a restraining property; cheerful and mobile in all his affairs; and he is one of the three planets...

<summary>This page describes the astrological natures of Venus and Mercury. Venus is characterized as a moist, feminine "fortune" associated with love and social harmony, while Mercury is depicted as a neutral, "mixed" planet that adopts the qualities of whichever celestial body it is near.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical description of Mercury's astrological effects on weather and politics before beginning a new chapter on the Moon. It includes a significant historiographical debate by Albumazar regarding the identity of Ptolemy and the physical reasons for the planetary natures.</meta>

...signifying rains, and through him <note>Referring to the planet Mercury, continued from the previous page.</note> and through his change from sign to sign: and through his state and his retrogradation and direction, and through his oppositions and conjunctions, the movements of the winds and their strengths are known, namely whether they will be strong or weak in all parts of that year. For when he leaves one sign and enters another, or when he is stationary or retrograde, he signifies strong winds, humidities, and rain, and atmospheric events in the air regarding clouds, disturbances, and similar things. Similarly, when he is in opposition to the moon while the moon is in any of the watery or airy signs, it signifies what we said before. In the revolution of the years of the world <note>A term for "Mundane Astrology," which predicts world events and the fates of nations.</note>, he has a great and truthful significance over those holding the offices of kings. **Alfraganus** <note>original: "Alfraganus" — Al-Farghani, a 9th-century Persian astronomer whose works were a primary source for European astronomy.</note> says that the size of Mercury's body is one part out of 32,000 parts of the body of the earth.

## The Moon, a Feminine Nightly Fortune.

Here he treats the nature of the moon. The meaning of the text is clear. **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century mathematician and astronomer.</note> says the greatest power of the moon is to moisten, because she is very near the earth and because of the cold smokiness <note>Vapors or mist arising from the earth.</note> that is raised by her. And because of this, visible bodies change to such a state with a manifest transformation: because she causes bodies to change and rot for the most part; and she also has a slight participation with the sun in heat because she receives light from him. **Albumazar** <note>Abu Ma'shar (787–886), one of the most influential Persian astrologers in the medieval West.</note> refutes Ptolemy regarding the cause of the natures of the planets, and these are his words: that many kings of Greece were wise men after Alexander, son of Philip; and each of them was called Ptolemy; and they were ten in number, and one woman; and they were reigning in Egypt; and the years of their reign were 275. They were all wise men, among whom was one Ptolemy who published the book of the *Almagest* <note>The defining astronomical manual of the Middle Ages.</note> concerning the cause of the motion of the circle and whatever planets are in it. And one of them published a book on the judgments of the stars <note>Referring to the *Tetrabiblos*, Ptolemy's work on astrology.</note>, attributing it to Ptolemy, the author of the book of the *Almagest*. And it is said that he who published the book of judgments also published the book of the *Almagest*; the truth of this matter is unknown; but he among them who published the book of judgments recounted the natures of the planets and their causes in his book. 

And he began to say that the sun heats with gentleness and duration, and that its work in this is more evident and stronger than the work of the other planets because of its size, and the more it is elevated to the zenith <note>The point directly overhead.</note> of our heads, the heat is increased for us. And he thought that the nature of the moon is moist because of the proximity of its circle to the earth and because of the reception of vapors, since they are raised to it from the earth. And he thought that the nature of **Saturn** is cold and dry because of the distance of its circle from the heat of the sun and its distance from the humidity of the earth's vapors. And he thought that **Mars** by nature was hot and dry because its color is similar to fire; and because it is near the sun and because it is below it, its heat is raised to it and heats it. And he thought that **Jupiter** is of an equal complexion <note>A balanced or temperate nature.</note>: because its circle is between the circles of Saturn and Mars. And for this reason its nature is hot, moist, equal, and temperate. And he thought that the nature of **Venus** is hot, moist, and equal; her heat comes from the proximity of her circle to the sun, but her humidity comes from the moist vapor that reaches her from the earth. And he thought the nature of **Mercury** is dry at certain times and moist at others. Its dryness is because of its proximity to the sun and because it is not distant from it by a very great elongation <note>In astronomy, the angular distance between the sun and a planet.</note>,

<summary>This page concludes the astrological profile of Mercury before beginning a chapter on the Moon's moistening nature. It features a historical discussion by Albumazar regarding the identity of Ptolemy and a physical explanation for why each planet possesses its specific elemental qualities based on its distance from the Sun and Earth.</summary>
<keywords>Mercury, Moon, Saturn, Mars, Jupiter, Venus, Ptolemy, Albumazar, Alfraganus, planetary natures, weather prediction, Almagest, Egypt, astronomical circles</keywords>

--- Page 115 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the moon, transitioning from a general description to a scholarly debate between historical authorities regarding the physical mechanism behind the moon's humidifying influence. It specifically addresses a criticism by Albumazar (Abu Ma'shar) against Ptolemy’s theories.</meta>

Its humidity is indeed due to the primacy of its circle <note>Meaning its proximity or the fact that its orbital sphere is the first one above the Earth.</note> to the moon's circle. This is what **Ptolemy** thought regarding the natures of the planets, and this is the reasoning he used for this. But we will now say where his words are to be challenged—that is, where they are worthy of correction. For although he believed that through the sun and the heating of things by it, its work is found in its pleasantness and duration; however, what he said—that the nature of the moon is humid because of the proximity of its circle to the earth and the receiving of vapors that rise from the earth to it—this is rejected by the wise. This is because the space between the face of the earth and the nearest place where the moon is located is approximately 128,044 miles, by the measure where one mile is 3,000 cubits.

And this is clear in the book in which the distances of the higher bodies from each other are recounted. And vapors do not rise higher from the surface of the earth, according to what Ptolemy himself recounted, than 16 stadia—given that a stadium is 400 cubits. For 16 stadia make two miles and about a tenth and a thirteenth of a mile. Yet the distance of the moon when it is closest to the surface of the earth is, as we said, about 128,044 miles. Therefore, from where or how can the vapor of the earth reach the moon so as to corrupt or change its nature? In a similar way, he <note>Albumazar</note> reproves him <note>Ptolemy</note> regarding the natures of the other planets, but I pass over this for the sake of brevity.

**Haly** <note>Ali ibn Ridwan, an 11th-century Egyptian physician and astrologer.</note> in his commentary on the aforementioned propositions <note>original: "ppositionū ꝑallegate" — likely referring to Ptolemy's *Centiloquium* or *Tetrabiblos*.</note> about the nature of the moon responds to the arguments of **Albumazar** <note>Abu Ma'shar, a 9th-century Persian astronomer.</note>. Haly says, explaining the words of Ptolemy: "First, after Ptolemy taught us that the virtues of the stars follow the size of their bodies, and the body of the moon is small, as he recounted in the *Almagest* <note>Ptolemy's foundational work on astronomy.</note>, and its work follows the work of the sun in one part. He says here that this is because it is very near the earth, and its power reaches us before it changes; because of this, its effect appears greater than the effect of other stars." And we see visibly that it attracts the humors <note>The liquid elements of the earth and the fluids in living bodies.</note> of the earth and of the bodies following it. And because it has light from the sun, it produces a slight accidental heat in earthly things, with which it causes the humors existing in bodies to putrefy and change, as is shown in physics and in nature. And it is clear that light creates heat; this can be proven by mirrors that burn and similar things. And since the moon has light from the sun, it is appropriate that it has a small heat which it produces as if by accident.

And this is what Ptolemy means to say. And because Albumazar did not understand this, he believed that he erred in his statement, saying: "How can humid smokiness ascend to the moon, since smokiness cannot ascend beyond 16 stadia and the moon is far more distant?" But I have responded to such statements as these, and I said how he did not understand this, because Ptolemy did not mean to say that humid smokiness ascends all the way to the moon. Instead, he said that the ascent of humid smokiness toward the moon teaches us that it attracts the humors of the earth and of other bodies following it, just as a magnet <note>original: "adamas" — in this context, the loadstone or magnet that attracts iron.</note> draws iron.

**Alfraganus** <note>Al-Farghani, a 9th-century astronomer.</note> says on the authority of Ptolemy that the size of the moon's body is one part out of 39 parts of the earth. And he says that the largest body of all the bodies of the universe is the sun. And after the sun, the fifteen greatest stars <note>Stars of the first magnitude.</note> are the largest; for he says that each one of them contains the body of the earth 107 times. Third in magnitude is Jupiter, and fourth is Saturn. After Saturn are the fixed stars of the second order, of which each one equals the earth 90 times. After them are the stars of the third order, of which each one equals the earth 72 times. After them are the stars of the fourth order, of which each one equals the earth 54 times. After them are the stars of the fifth order, of which each one equals the earth 37 times. After them are the stars of the sixth order and the smaller ones among them—indeed, the smallest of all that are seen, the proof of which is...

<summary>This page discusses a medieval astronomical debate concerning why the moon is considered "humid," contrasting Ptolemy's theory of rising vapors with Albumazar's distance-based critique, while Haly defends Ptolemy using the metaphor of magnetic attraction. It also provides a list of planetary and stellar sizes relative to the Earth according to Alfraganus.</summary>
<keywords>Ptolemy, Albumazar, Haly, Alfraganus, lunar nature, humidity, planetary distances, stellar magnitudes, humors, medieval astronomy</keywords>

--- Page 116 ---
<meta>This page continues from a discussion of the relative sizes and distances of celestial bodies, citing the authority of Alfraganus (the Latinized name of the 9th-century Persian astronomer Al-Farghani) and addressing the astrological influences of planets on human development.</meta>

...[Jupiter] contains the earth <note>The text begins mid-sentence; Alfraganus typically states that Jupiter is ninety-five times the size of the Earth.</note> ninety-five times and one eighth. The next largest body after these is **Mars**. After Mars, the **Earth**; after Earth, **Venus**; after Venus, the **Moon**; and finally **Mercury**. It is clear, therefore, according to **Alfraganus**, that the largest of all bodies is the **Sun** and the smallest is **Mercury**, with the exception of those things subject to generation and corruption <note>original: "generabilibus et corruptibilibus"; this refers to the "sublunary" world—everything below the moon—which is subject to change, birth, and death, unlike the eternal heavens.</note>. Concerning the distance <note>original: "longitudine"</note> of these bodies from the Earth, Alfraganus says that the closest distance of the Moon from the Earth is 33 times as much as half the diameter <note>The radius.</note> of the Earth plus a 20th part; this will be 104,112 miles.

And the furthest distance of the Moon, which is also the closest distance of Mercury, is 64 times as much as half the diameter of the Earth plus a 6th part, which is 208,542 miles. And the furthest distance of Mercury, which is the closest distance of Venus, is 167 times as much as half the diameter of the Earth, which is 542,750 miles. And the furthest distance of Venus, which is the closest distance of the Sun, is 1,120 times as much as half the diameter of the Earth, which is 3,640,000 miles. And the furthest distance of the Sun, which is the closest distance of Mars, is 1,220 times as much as the semi-diameter of the Earth, which is 3,965,000 miles. And the furthest distance of Mars, which is the closest distance of Jupiter, is 8,876 times as much as the semi-diameter of the Earth, which is 28,847,000 miles. And the furthest distance of Jupiter, which is the closest distance of Saturn, is 14,405 times as much as the semi-diameter of the Earth, which is 46,816,250 miles. And the furthest distance of Saturn, which is equal to the distance of the fixed stars and is the quantity of half the diameter of the circle of the signs <note>The Zodiac.</note>, is 20,110 times as much as the semi-diameter of the Earth, which is 65,357,500 miles. And when this is doubled, it will reach the quantity of the total diameter of the circle of the signs, that is, of the eighth sphere <note>The sphere containing the fixed stars.</note>, which is 130,715,000 miles. And when this is multiplied by 3 and one-seventh <note>An approximation of Pi (π).</note>, it will reach the quantity of the circumference of the circle of the signs or any other major circle described in the eighth sphere, which is 410,818,570 miles; and the measure of each individual degree of the major circle will be 114,116 miles.

## Regarding the order of the planets.

<margin>
Here he sets out the order of the planets.
</margin>
[He sets out] the order of the planets in the conception of children, namely the order in governing the fetus while it is in the mother's womb. He says that **Saturn** governs it from the hour of conception until the end of one month. In the second month, **Jupiter** governs it. In the third, **Mars**. In the fourth, the **Sun**. In the fifth, **Venus**. In the sixth, **Mercury**. In the seventh, the **Moon**; and because of this, as it is said, a child in the seventh month seeks to exit, because the works of all the planets are completed in it; and those who are born then can live for this same reason. Then the governance returns to **Saturn**, and he governs it in the eighth month; and then those who are born do not live because of the malice of Saturn <note>In medieval astrology, Saturn was considered the "Greater Malefic," associated with coldness and death.</note>. Then **Jupiter** governs it in the ninth month; and then they are commonly born and live because of the goodness of Jupiter <note>Jupiter was the "Greater Benefic," associated with life and growth.</note>. Then he sets out the order of the planets in the rule of the body after birth regarding the progression of ages: and he says that the **Moon** begins to rule the newborn from its entry into the world, and rules it for four years, which are the years of nourishment. Then **Mercury** rules it for ten years. Then **Venus** eight. Then the **Sun** nine. Then **Mars** 15. After this, **Jupiter** 12. Then **Saturn** until the end of life.

### The Dragon's Head.

<margin>
Here he sets out the nature of the Dragon's Head and Tail, and it is clear.
</margin>
<note>The "Dragon" refers to the nodes of the Moon—the points where the Moon's path crosses the Sun's path. The Head (Caput Draconis) was generally considered lucky, and the Tail (Cauda Draconis) unlucky.</note>

# If there should be any day.

<margin>Here he presents the order of the planets regarding the lord of days and hours.</margin>
**I**f there should be any day of the planets regarding the lord of days and hours: He says that a certain day belongs to a certain planet. The first hour of that day—given that the first hour begins from sunrise—belongs to that same [planet]. The second hour is of the planet which succeeds it in the descending order of the heavens <note>The "Chaldean order" of planets from furthest to closest: Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, Sun, Venus, Mercury, Moon.</note>. And the third [hour] belongs to the third planet in the order; and so through the order until the end of the 12 hours of the **artificial day** <note>The "artificial day" refers to the period from sunrise to sunset, which was divided into twelve equal hours regardless of the season.</note>. And that planet which holds the 13th hour is the lord of that night; and the one which holds the 25th hour—namely, the first hour of the following day—is the lord of that following day. And the author provides an example in the text, so it is not necessary for me to provide one; and thus the explanation of the second distinction of this book is completed.

# And because, with God’s consent.

<margin>This is the third [distinction]</margin>
**A**nd because, with God's consent, [this is] the distinction of this book in which the author determines the being of the planets in themselves and their being in relation to one another—that is, of one to another. And it is divided into two parts: for in the first he determines their being in themselves. In the second part, their being in relation to one another. The second [part begins] there: "Concerning their being in relation to one another." And that is divided into two: for first he determines the being of the five planets with respect to the **luminaries** <note>The "luminaries" are the Sun and the Moon.</note>. The second [is] concerning their being among themselves. The second [begins] there: "But first he determines their being in themselves." And the sense of the text is as follows: The planets are sometimes said to be ascending and sometimes descending, and sometimes they are said to be increased in light and magnitude, and sometimes diminished in light and magnitude. And sometimes they are said to be increased in number and sometimes diminished in number. And sometimes they are said to be increased in motion and sometimes diminished in motion. And all planets except the Sun are sometimes said to be northern and sometimes southern. And they are said to be ascending and descending in the north and south. These are the things that happen to planets in themselves. And the author explains these in order in the text, saying that a planet sometimes ascends in its **eccentric circle** <note>In the Ptolemaic system, an "eccentric" is an orbital circle whose center is not the Earth, used to explain why planets seem to change speed and distance.</note> so that it is in the upper part of its eccentric, not distant from the **apogee** <note>original: "auge"; the point in an orbit furthest from the Earth.</note> by more than 90 degrees before or after; and then its light appears less, and it also appears smaller in size, and its course is then slow with respect to the motion of the center of the **epicycle** <note>A smaller orbital circle whose center moves along the circumference of a larger circle (the deferent).</note>, and it is then said to be "stronger." However, when it is at one of the mean distances of its eccentric—namely, distant from the apogee by exactly 90 degrees—then it is equal in light, magnitude, and course; that is, it is not called large or small in light or magnitude, and the motion of the center of the epicycle is equal to its **mean motion** <note>The average speed of a planet, assuming its motion was perfectly uniform and circular.</note>. But when it is outside these locations—namely, distant from the apogee by more than 90 degrees before or after—then it descends and appears larger in light and magnitude, and the center of the epicycle then moves faster than its mean motion. And when the **equated argument** <note>A coordinate used to find a planet's position within its epicycle.</note> of the planet is less than 180 degrees—namely, when the equation of the argument is added to the mean motion—then it is said to be "increased in number." And if the equated argument is more than 180 degrees—namely, when the equation of the argument is subtracted from the mean motion—then it is said to be "diminished in number." But in the case of the **Moon**, when the equation is added it is said to be increased in number, and when it is subtracted it is said to be diminished in number. And when any of the three **higher planets** <note>Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note> is moved...

--- Page 118 ---
<meta>This page continues from the third distinction of the book, which began discussing the "existence of the planets in themselves"—referring to their individual motions and speeds before moving on to their relationships with one another.</meta>

[When a planet moves] faster than its mean <term>mean motion: The average speed at which a planet is calculated to move, as if its orbit were a perfect circle.</term> motion, it is called "increased in motion"; and when it goes slower than its mean motion, it is called "diminished in motion." In the same way, this is understood regarding the Sun and the Moon. And when it moves exactly as much as its mean motion, then it is said to be "equal in motion." And when Venus or Mercury moves faster than the Sun, it is called "increased in motion"; when slower, it is called "diminished in motion." 

And when any of the six planets passes the node <note>original: "nodum"</note> of intersection of its carrying circle <term>carrying circle: Also known as a "deferent," the main circular path along which the center of a planet's smaller epicycle moves.</term> with the ecliptic—which node is called the **genzahar** <note>The "genzahar" (from the Persian *jawzahar*) refers to the points where a planet's path crosses the Sun's path, or the ecliptic.</note>—it is northern. And it is "northern ascending"—that is, increasing in latitude—until it has been distanced from the intersection by 90 degrees. And when it has been distanced by more than 90 degrees from the intersection, it is "northern descending"—that is, decreasing in latitude—until it has been distanced from the intersection by 180 degrees; and then it is at the intersection opposite the first and has no latitude—that is, it is on the ecliptic. And when it has passed that point, its latitude becomes southern, and it is "southern descending" until it has been distanced from the first intersection by 270 degrees. And when it has passed this number, it is "southern ascending" until it returns to the first intersection where its latitude will be zero. This is the summary of the text regarding the existence of the planets in themselves.

# <image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'D' featuring floral motifs and geometric patterns.</image-desc> Now let us treat their existence in relation to one another.

He previously determined the existence of the planets in themselves. Here he determines their existence in relation to one another: and first, concerning the existence of the five planets in respect to the luminaries <note>The "luminaries" are the Sun and the Moon.</note>. And this is divided into four parts according to the four states that the planets have toward the Sun and the Moon. The first is "face-to-face vision." The second is "security." The third is "combustion." The fourth is "oriental and occidental." 

First, therefore, he determines face-to-face vision. Second, security. Third, combustion. Fourth, being oriental <note>rising before the Sun</note> and occidental <note>setting after the Sun</note>. The first part is at the beginning. The second begins there: "and from this leadership." The third there: "and every planet." The fourth there: "and from which higher." 

The logic of the first part is this: When any planet is occidental to the Sun—that is, it rises after the rising of the Sun—and there are as many signs between it and the Sun as there are between the house of that planet and Leo, which is the house of the Sun, such a planet is said to be in "face-to-face vision" with the Sun. For example: Capricorn is the house of Saturn and is the sixth sign from the house of the Sun, namely, Leo. When, therefore, Saturn is in the sixth sign from the Sun, counting the number of signs from the Sun toward Saturn according to the order of the signs—otherwise it would not be occidental if it were not counted in this way—then it is said to see him "face to face." 

And the sign of Sagittarius is fifth from Leo. When, therefore, Jupiter is in the fifth sign from the Sun, beginning from the Sun toward Jupiter according to the order of the signs, it is said to see him "face to face"; and so with the others. And when any planet is oriental to the Moon, and there are as many signs between it and the Moon according to the order of the signs as there are between the house of that planet and the house of the Moon—namely, Cancer—then it is said to be in her "face-to-face vision." 

For example: Cancer is the sixth sign from the other house of Saturn, namely, from Aquarius; therefore, when the Moon is in the 6th sign from Saturn, there is "face-to-face vision" between them. And also Cancer is the fifth sign from the house of Jupiter, namely, from Pisces; therefore, when the Moon is in the 5th sign from Jupiter according to the order of the signs, there is "face-to-face vision" between them: and so you will be able to provide examples for the others in their own way.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "existences" or conditions of the planets. The previous page concluded by describing the northern and southern latitudes of the planets as they cross the ecliptic at the nodes.</meta>

# On the Leadership of a Planet
<margin>Here he determines the second way of being for the planets regarding the luminaries <note>the Sun and the Moon</note>.</margin>
The fifth condition <note>referring to a list of planetary states</note> of the planets regarding the luminaries is concerning **leadership** <term>leadership: original Latin *ductoria*, also known as "spear-bearing" (*doryphory*). This is a condition where a planet acts as a bodyguard or herald for the Sun or Moon, depending on its position.</term>, which is the same as guardianship. The meaning is this: when any planet is in its *hayyiz* <term>hayyiz: A planet's preferred condition—for example, a masculine planet being in a masculine sign above the earth during the day.</term> and in one of the "angles" of the ascendant—namely in the 1st house (the ascendant), the 10th, the 7th, or the 4th house—and one of the luminaries is in one of the angles from that planet, so that the planet is **oriental** <term>oriental: Rising in the east before the Sun.</term> to the Sun during the day, and **occidental** <term>occidental: Setting in the west after the Moon.</term> to the Moon at night. This means that during the day it is in the 10th sign from the Sun (for then it is in an angle from the Sun and oriental), or at night it is in the 4th sign from the Moon (for then it is in an angle from the Moon and occidental). In such cases, the planet is said to be in its "leadership." The angle of opposition, however, has no place here. The reason is that a planet positioned directly opposite the Sun or Moon is considered neither oriental nor occidental. Afterward, he says that every planet, from the time it is hidden under the rays of the Sun until the time it begins to appear, is called **combust** <term>combust: Literally "burnt," this describes a planet so close to the Sun that its light and astrological influence are "consumed" or hidden.</term>. It begins to be "burnt" when it first enters the rays; and when it comes near the Sun within 12 degrees, it is called **oppressed**; and when it is with the Sun in the exact same degree, and the distance between them is less than the semi-diameter of the Sun, and the planet's latitude is also less than the semi-diameter of the Sun, it is called **united** or **incorporated** with the Sun <note>This rare and powerful state is often called *cazimi*, or being "in the heart" of the Sun.</note>. And when it has passed this union, it is said to be in **convalescence** <note>original: *convalescentia*</note> until it is finally freed from the rays of the Sun.

---

# How the Three Higher Planets Appear
Here he determines the oriental and occidental status of the planets. He says that the three "superior" planets <note>Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn, which are further from the Sun than the Earth is</note>, when they emerge from under the rays of the Sun and begin to appear in the morning before sunrise until they reach a point opposite the Sun—that is, when they are closer to the eastern horizon than the western when the Sun is at the midnight line—are then called **oriental** and **right-handed**. They are called oriental because they rise before the Sun; they are called right-handed because they are on the right side of the sky relative to the Sun. For the western part is considered "right" and the eastern part "left." Imagine a man turning his face toward the south: it is clear that the East is to his left and the West is to his right. And when any of the three superior planets has passed the point of opposition to the Sun, it is called **occidental** and **left-handed**. It is called occidental not because it is in the western part of the sky relative to the Sun—indeed, the opposite is true—but it is called occidental because it sets *after* the Sun. And it is called left-handed because it is in the eastern part of the sky relative to the Sun, which is the left side, as was said.

Venus, however, and Mercury: each of them, during the time it circles its epicycle <term>epicycle: A small circular orbit whose center moves around the circumference of a larger circle (the deferent).</term>, joins the Sun twice. One time occurs through "direct" motion (in the upper part of the epicycle) and the other time through "retrograde" motion <term>retrograde: When a planet appears to move backward in the sky.</term> (in the lower part of the epicycle). Therefore, when one of them separates from a conjunction with the Sun in the middle of its retrogradation—that is, in the lower part of its epicycle—it moves away from the Sun toward the western part of the sky. When it is distant enough from the Sun to be visible, it is seen in the morning before sunrise; therefore, it is then called **oriental** and **right-handed** until it is "burnt" by the Sun again—that is, until it joins the Sun in the upper part of its epicycle through direct motion. And when it has separated from the Sun through direct motion, it is seen in the evening after sunset; therefore, it is then called **occidental** and **left-handed** until it is "burnt" once more. 

Thus, there is a difference between Venus and Mercury and the three superior planets. For Venus and Mercury, sometimes in their departure from the Sun or when emerging from the rays, become oriental, and sometimes they become occidental. But the superior planets always become oriental when they emerge from under the rays of the Sun. And they are said to increase in **strength** <term>strength: original *fortitudo*, referring to the planet's astrological potency.</term> until they are 30 degrees away from the Sun; and from 30 up to 60 degrees, they are called "strong." And after...

<summary>This page explains the astrological conditions of planetary "leadership" (doryphory) and the various stages of a planet's proximity to the Sun, including combustion and cazimi. It also details how planets are classified as oriental/right-handed or occidental/left-handed based on their rising and setting times relative to the Sun.</summary>

<keywords>leadership, doryphory, hayyiz, combustion, cazimi, oriental, occidental, superior planets, epicycle, retrograde motion, planetary strength</keywords>

--- Page 120 ---
<meta>This page continues the detailed technical breakdown of planetary phases relative to the Sun and begins a new section on how the seven planets interact with one another through aspects and conjunctions.</meta>

When they have moved further than 60 degrees from the Sun, they are called **oriental** <note>rising in the east before the Sun</note>, moving toward weakness until the beginning of retrogradation. From the start of retrogradation until they are in opposition to the Sun, they are called **oriental retrograde**. From the point of opposition until they resume direct motion, they are called **occidental retrograde**. From the station <term>station: the point where a planet appears to stop moving before changing direction</term> until 30 degrees after the station, they are called **occidental and strong**. From 30 degrees after the station until they come within 30 degrees of the Sun, they are called **occidental**, moving toward weakness. Afterward, they are called **occidental and weak** until they are **combust** <term>combust: a planet "burnt up" by being so close to the Sun that it becomes invisible to the eye</term>. 

Venus and Mercury, however, when they separate from the Sun through retrogradations, become oriental and are called weak until they come to their station. From that station until they are as far from the Sun as they usually are at the beginning of retrogradation, they are called **oriental and strong**. Then they become **oriental and weak** until they are overwhelmed under the rays of the Sun. Afterward, they are said to be united with the Sun, then combust, moving toward their appearance. From their appearance until they are 60 degrees from the upper part of their **epicycle** <term>epicycle: in older astronomy, a small circle on which a planet moves, while that circle itself moves around the Earth</term>, they are **occidental and strong**. From this place until retrogradation, they are **occidental**, moving toward weakness, and afterward, until the conjunction, they are **occidental and weak**. If the text were not understood in this way, it would be false, since it is not possible for these planets <note>Mercury and Venus</note> to be as far as 60 degrees away from the Sun in the zodiac. 

Afterward, he says that when any planet exits from under the rays of the Sun and is joined to no one by body or aspect, it is in its own "proper light."

### Their Existence Relative to One Another.

<margin>
Previously
*determined.*
</margin>
He has determined those things which happen to planets in themselves, and those which happen to the five planets in relation to the luminaries <note>the Sun and the Moon</note>. Here he determines those things which happen to the seven planets relative to one another. This could be divided into as many parts as there are modes of accidents that happen to the planets mutually. These can be reduced to sixteen modes according to the meaning of the text, which are: **conjunction**, **voiding** <note>original: *uacuatio*, usually called "void of course"</note>, **course**, **ferality** <note>original: *feralitas*, meaning a planet not making any aspects</note>, **translation of light**, **return of light**, **prohibition**, **pulsation of nature**, **reception**, **return of nature**, **refraining** (or "quietness"), **frustration**, **cutting off of light**, **veneration**, **destruction**, **obsession** <note>original: *obsessio*, often called "besiegement"</note>, **love**, and **hatred**. The author explains these in the text in order until the end of this section. 

The meaning of the first part is this: **Conjunction** occurs when two planets are in two signs that "behold" each other by some aspect, and the one that is "lighter" <note>original: *leuior*, meaning faster-moving</note> between them has traveled through fewer degrees of its sign than the "heavy" one <note>original: *põderosus*, meaning slower-moving</note> has traveled in its sign, provided that this difference is 6 degrees or less. In that case, it is said that the lighter planet "goes to conjunction" with the heavier one. 

For example: suppose the Sun is in **Aries** and has traveled 6 degrees of Aries, and Mars is in **Leo** and has traveled 12 degrees of Leo. These two signs behold each other in a **trine aspect** <term>trine aspect: an angle of 120 degrees, considered a harmonious relationship between planets</term>. The Sun, which is faster in motion than Mars, has traveled fewer degrees of Aries than Mars has of Leo, and they are within a difference of 6 degrees. In this example, the Sun goes to a conjunction with Mars by a trine aspect. If the Sun were in the 12th degree of Aries and Mars in the 12th degree of Leo, then it would be a "complete conjunction." If the Sun were beyond the 12th degree of Aries while Mars was in the 12th of Leo, then there would be a "separation" between them. This is called a **conjunction of longitude**. 

A **conjunction of latitude**, however, is when two planets are joined bodily in one sign and are of equal latitude from the **ecliptic** <note>the apparent path of the Sun</note>, so that the latitude of one is ascending in the north and the other is descending.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the technical measurements of planetary movement, specifically focusing on the intersection of longitude and latitude during conjunctions, and introduces concepts of "Void of Course," "Feral" planets, and the "Translation of Nature" (commonly known as the translation of light).</meta>

...in the north, or one ascending in the south and the other descending in the south. Or, if there should be a conjunction from any of the aspects <note>original: "ex aliquo aspectuum"; referring to the geometric relationships like sextile or trine</note> and the latitude of one is northern ascending, while the other is southern ascending. For example: I propose that the Moon is joined to Saturn in some sign and in a specific degree and minute, such that Saturn is near its **node** <term>node: called "genzahar" in the text; the point where a planet's path crosses the ecliptic</term> moving toward the north in latitude, and its latitude is 3 minutes. Meanwhile, the Moon approaches the **Dragon's Tail** <term>Dragon's Tail: the "South Node" of the Moon, associated with decreasing latitude</term> coming from the north and decreasing in latitude, so that its latitude is likewise 3 minutes. This is a "truest conjunction" in both longitude <note>position along the zodiac</note> and latitude <note>position north or south of the center line</note>. And in this case, the Moon would **eclipse** <note>original: "eclypsaret"; to hide or obscure from view</note> Saturn, unless the **parallax** <term>parallax: the difference in the apparent position of an object viewed along two different lines of sight; original "diversitas aspectus"</term> of the Moon was so great that it prevented it. Although the author says that the latitude of one must be ascending and the other descending, it nevertheless seems to me that in a conjunction by body <note>two planets occupying the same degree</note>, in every case where it happens that both latitudes are equal and on the same side—that is to say, both latitudes are northern or both are southern—it will be a true conjunction of latitudes. In the aspect of opposition <note>planets 180 degrees apart</note>, if the latitudes are equal but in different directions—so that one is southern and the other northern—it will be a true aspect in terms of latitude.

# And when one planet is separated
<image-desc>Woodcut ornamental initial E featuring a scholar or scribe seated at a desk with an open book.</image-desc>
from another. Here he places the **Void of Course** <term>Void of Course: "vacuatio cursus," a period when a planet makes no further major aspects before leaving its current sign</term>. He says that when any planet has been joined to another by body or by aspect, and after separating from it, it does not join any other by body or aspect as long as it remains in that sign, it is then said to be "Void of Course." For example: I propose that the Moon is joined to Jupiter bodily in Sagittarius; after its separation from Jupiter, if there is no other planet in Sagittarius which it can join, nor any rays <note>aspects</note> of any planet it can see, in this case, the Moon is Void of Course until it leaves Sagittarius and joins something by body or aspect.

# And if a planet should be in some sign.
<image-desc>Woodcut ornamental initial E featuring a figure in a robe holding an object, possibly a teacher or authority figure.</image-desc>
He says that when any planet is in a sign where no other planet is, nor are there the rays of any other planet, that planet—as long as it remains in that sign in such a state—is said to be **feral** <note>original: "feralis," meaning wild or beast-like</note> or "of the woods," like a man who has no society with anyone else.

# And when it has been separated.
<gloss>He says that when a planet of faster motion separates itself from a planet of slower motion, and in separating from it joins another, it then "transfers the nature" of the first to the second. For example: I propose that the Moon separates from a bodily conjunction with Jupiter, and before that separation is completed, it joins Saturn by some aspect or bodily: the Moon then transfers the nature of Jupiter to Saturn.</gloss>
The "translation" <note>the transfer of influence between planets</note> also happens in another way: namely, when a light planet joins one heavier than itself, and that heavier one joins another even heavier than itself, then the middle one transfers the nature of the light one to the heavier one. For example: I propose that the Moon aspects Jupiter by some aspect, and Jupiter aspects Saturn. Jupiter in this case transfers the nature of the Moon to Saturn.

# But if it is not joined.
<gloss>He says that when two planets are in this way dis...</gloss>
