<meta>This page continues the technical exposition of planetary interactions, specifically detailing the "Return of Light," the "Prohibition" of conjunctions by intervening planets, and how planets "send nature" or "strength" based on their zodiacal dignities.</meta>

...positioned such that one of them is not joined to the other, but both are joined to some third planet. Then, if that third planet aspects any place among the locations of the circle, it projects the light of those two to that place. And if any planet should be in the location of its rays, it returns to it the light of those two. For example: I propose that the Sun is in Capricorn and Venus is in Pisces, such that they do not aspect each other. Then I propose that Mars is in Cancer, aspecting the Sun by opposition and Venus by a trine <note>original: "de tertio"; an aspect of 120 degrees</note>. In this case, Mars receives the light of the Sun and Venus, and returns their lights to the places in the circle where the rays of Mars are located; and this is called the **Return of Light** <term>Return of Light: original "redditus luminis"; a situation where a third planet transfers light back to others</term>. The **Return of Light** also happens in another way when two planets do not aspect each other, but a third transfers the light of one to the other, such that it separates from one and joins the other; and then it is the same as **Translation** <note>referring to the "Translation of Light" discussed on the previous page</note>.

<margin>
He speaks of when three planets are in one sign but in different [degrees].
</margin>

## The Prohibition follows.

...degrees, and the one who is slower in motion <note>a "heavy" planet</note> has traversed more degrees of that sign than either of the other two, then the one in the middle of them **prohibits** <term>Prohibition: original "prohibitio"; a condition where a faster planet is prevented from reaching a slower one because another planet stands between them</term> the conjunction of the lighter planet with the heavy one. For example: I propose that Saturn is in the 12th degree of Virgo, and I propose that Mars is in the 5th degree of the same Virgo. In this case, Mars seeks a conjunction with Saturn. But I propose that the Moon is in the 8th degree of the same Virgo; in this case, it should not be judged that Mars joins with Saturn, because the Moon is in the middle and prohibits its conjunction. 

A **Prohibition** also happens in another way: such that two planets are in one sign and the lighter is joined to the heavier, and another planet aspects the same heavy one, and its aspect is just as close as the bodily conjunction of the other; then the one who is joined bodily prohibits the one aspecting from completing the aspect. And if in this case one of them is closer than the other, the one who is closer prevails and is said to be joined to the heavy one. For example: I propose that Saturn is in the 12th degree of Virgo, and Mars is in the 8th degree of the same. And I propose that Jupiter projects its rays to the 8th degree of Virgo; in this case, Mars prohibits the aspect of Jupiter. But if Jupiter projected its rays to the 9th degree, it should be judged that Jupiter aspects Saturn, and by this, it would prohibit the conjunction of Mars. And if Jupiter projected its rays to the 7th degree, it should be judged that Mars would be joined to Saturn, and by this, it would prohibit the aspect of Jupiter.

## And if a planet is joined to the lord of that sign.

He says that when any planet is joined by aspect to the **lord** <note>the ruling planet</note> of that sign in which it is located, or to the lord of the **exaltation**, or **triplicity**, or **term**, or **face**, it is said to send the nature of the lord of that **dignity** <term>dignity: original "dignitas"; the specific zodiacal zones where a planet has varying degrees of power or "right" to rule</term> to the one whose dignity it is. For example: I propose that the Sun is in Capricorn, aspecting Saturn who is in Virgo. In this case, the Sun is in the **house** <note>domicile</note> of Saturn and sends the nature of Saturn himself to Saturn. 

And if a light planet is in some place where it has some dignity—for example, it is in its own house or its exaltation, etc.—and it aspects some planet heavier than itself that has no dignity in that place, the light planet is said to send its own **strength** <note>original: "fortitudinem"</note> to the heavy one. For example: I propose that the Moon is in Taurus—namely, in its exaltation—and it aspects Saturn, who is in Virgo, by a trine. In this case, the Moon sends its own nature or virtue to Saturn himself, even though Saturn has no dignity in the location of the Moon. But if Saturn has dignity in the location of the Moon—for example: I propose that the Moon is in the 24th degree of Taurus in its exaltation, and in the **term** and **face** of Saturn, and it aspects Saturn—it sends nature to Saturn through...

<summary>This page defines complex planetary relationships including the "Return of Light," "Prohibition" (where a third planet blocks a conjunction), and the "Sending of Nature," which describes how planets transmit power based on their zodiacal dignities.</summary>
<keywords>Return of Light, Prohibition, planetary aspects, zodiacal dignities, Saturn, Mars, Jupiter, Venus, Sun, Moon, Virgo, Taurus, Capricorn, planetary motion</keywords>

--- Page 123 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical exposition of planetary interactions, detailing how planets "send nature" or "strength" based on their zodiacal dignities, as well as the concepts of "Return," "Refrenation," and "Contrariety."</meta>

...nature of Saturn by reason of his term and his face <note>original: "ratione termini & faciei sue"; terms and faces are specific subdivisions of a zodiac sign that grant a planet power</note>.

# And when there are any of their dignities.
He says that when a lighter <note>faster-moving</note> planet aspects a planet heavier than itself, and the lighter one has a dignity <term>dignity: original "dignitatem"; a position of strength in a zodiac sign, such as being its ruler or "exalted" within it</term> in the place where it is, and the one it aspects also has some dignity in that same place, then the lighter planet sends to the heavier one both natures—that is, its own proper nature and the nature of the one it aspects. This sending is called **Reception** <term>Reception: original "receptio"; a core astrological concept where a planet is "welcomed" by the ruler of the sign it occupies</term>. For example: I propose that the Sun is in Aries in its exaltation and in the house of Mars, and it aspects Mars by some aspect. In this case, the Sun sends to Mars its own proper nature and, with this, the nature of Mars by reason of his house; and Mars receives the Sun from his own house. And if it happens that the lower planet is in the dignity of the superior, and conversely the superior is in the dignity of the lower, that is the best **reception** that can be. For example: let the Sun be in Aries in the house of Mars, and Mars be in Leo in the house of the Sun, and let the Sun aspect Mars by a trine aspect <note>original: "aspectu tertio"; an aspect of 120 degrees</note>. Such a reception is whole and perfect.

# From here follows the Return.
<margin>He says when a lower planet aspects a superior one, and the one he aspects...</margin>
...is combust <term>combust: original "combustus"; a planet weakened by being too close to the Sun's rays</term> or retrograde <term>retrograde: appearing to move backward in the sky, a sign of debility</term>. In this case, the superior planet returns to the lower the virtue <note>power or influence</note> that it sent to it, because on account of its own weakness it cannot retain it. And if in such a case both are in an angle <term>angle: the 1st, 4th, 7th, or 10th houses of a chart, the most powerful locations</term> or in a succeeding house <term>succeeding house: the 2nd, 5th, 8th, or 11th houses</term>, then the return is made with profit. And if the lower planet is in an angle and the superior is not, yet the superior still receives the lower, such a return is also made with profit. And if the lower is falling from an angle <note>original: "cadens"; in a weak house like the 3rd, 6th, 9th, or 12th</note> and the superior is in an angle, the return is made with profit. And if both are falling, the return is made with loss. For example: I propose that the Moon is in Taurus and aspects Mercury while he is in Capricorn and combust. In this case, the Moon sends her nature to Mercury. But because Mercury is combust, he cannot retain the nature sent to him; therefore, he returns that light which he received from her, and if in this case both were in angles, the return is profitable, etc.

# Next follows the Almene.
<margin>He says when a lower planet wishes to be joined to a superior one...</margin>
...but before the conjunction is completed, the lower planet becomes retrograde; this is called **Refrenation** <term>Refrenation: original "refrenatio"; literally "bridling" or "restraint," where a planet turns back before reaching its goal</term>. For example: I propose that Saturn is in the 12th degree of Virgo, and Venus is at the beginning of Virgo, moving toward a conjunction with Saturn. But when Venus reaches the 8th or 9th degree—that is, before she touches Saturn—she begins to move backward. In such a case, Venus is "bridled" from joining Saturn. Understand the same thing regarding an aspect, if a planet were moving toward an aspect but began to move backward before the aspect was completed.

# This is followed by the Alchorad, that is, Contrariety.
He says that **Contrariety** occurs when three planets are in one sign, of which one is heavy and the other two are light. One of the two light planets has passed the heavy one, and the other seeks a conjunction with the heavy one. But before it can join, the one that passed the heavy one becomes retrograde and joins the heavy one through retrogradation and meets the other light planet. Then the retrograde planet destroys the conjunction of the one it meets with the heavy planet. For example: I propose that Saturn is in the 8th degree of Virgo, and Mars is in the 12th degree of the same sign, and Jupiter is at the beginning of Virgo, seeking a conjunction with Saturn. But before he is joined to him, Mars begins to move retrograde to join Saturn through retrogradation, and he meets Jupiter...

<summary>This page defines complex planetary interactions: "Reception," where planets exchange power through shared dignities; "Return," where a weak planet returns light it cannot hold; and "Refrenation" or "Contrariety," where retrograde motion disrupts intended planetary connections.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, reception, return of virtue, refrenation, contrariety, retrograde, combustion, planetary dignities, Saturn, Mars, Venus, Jupiter</keywords>

--- Page 124 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical exposition of planetary interactions, specifically focusing on how intended planetary relationships (conjunctions) can be interrupted or "frustrated," as well as the conditions that grant planets strength or "good fortune" within a chart.</meta>

In this case, Mars destroys the conjunction of Jupiter with Saturn.

## Next follows *alphariz*, which is frustration.

<margin>He says that frustration occurs</margin>
when a light <note>fast-moving</note> planet seeks a conjunction with a heavy <note>slow-moving</note> planet, but before the conjunction is completed, the heavy planet enters another sign. If the rays of some other planet are at the beginning of that new sign, then when the light planet—wishing to join the heavy one—enters that sign, it joins with the rays of that third planet looking there instead. Thus, the first conjunction is annulled. For example: I propose that Saturn is at the 29th degree of Leo and Mars is at the 24th degree of the same sign, seeking a conjunction with Saturn. But before Mars joins Saturn, Saturn enters Virgo; and I propose that Jupiter aspects the first degree of Virgo. In this case, Mars, upon leaving Leo, joins with the rays of Jupiter, and thus the conjunction of Mars and Saturn is annulled.

## This is also followed by the cutting off of light.

He says that the **cutting off of light** <note>original: *abscisio luminis*</note> occurs when some light planet seeks a conjunction with a heavy one, and in the second sign from the heavy planet’s sign there is another planet. But before the light one can join the heavy one, that planet in the second sign becomes retrograde <note>appears to move backward in the sky</note> and joins the heavy one. In such a case, the retrograde planet "cuts off" the light of the heavy planet from the planet that intended to join it. This is almost the same as **interference** <note>original: *arietas*, likely a variation of *obvietatio*</note>, but it differs in this: in interference, the retrograde planet not only joins the heavy one but also meets the light one who was trying to reach the heavy one, coming between them; it can be illustrated as was described there. The cutting off of light also happens in another way: when a light planet seeks a conjunction with a heavy one, and that heavy planet is seeking a conjunction with an even heavier planet, and joins it before the light planet can reach the first heavy one. For example: I propose that Mars seeks a conjunction with Jupiter, and Jupiter seeks a conjunction with Saturn; before Mars can join Jupiter, Jupiter joins Saturn. In such a case, Saturn cuts off the light of Jupiter from Mars.

## There are also for these planets.

<margin>He says that planets are strengthened in certain</margin>
places and weakened in others; in some places they become "fortunes" <note>bringers of good luck</note> and in others "infortunes" <note>bringers of ill luck</note>. They become fortunes when in a **sextile** <note>an aspect of 60 degrees</note> or **trine** <note>original: *tertio*; an aspect of 120 degrees</note> aspect with good planets. They are unfortunate when they are "falling" from them. It should be understood that when any planet is in the same sign as another, or in the fourth, seventh, or tenth sign from it, it is said to be in an **angle** <term>angle: original *angulo*; the most powerful positions in a zodiacal chart, corresponding to the 1st, 4th, 7th, and 10th houses</term> from it. And if it is in the second sign from it, or the fifth, eighth, or eleventh, it is said to be in a **succedent** <term>succedent: original *succedenti*; houses that "follow" or "succeed" the powerful angles</term> from it. And if it is in the third, sixth, ninth, or twelfth sign from it, it is said to be in a **cadent** <term>cadent: original *cadenti*; houses that "fall away" from the angles, generally considered the weakest positions</term> from it. They also become "fortunes" when they separate from one fortunate planet and are joined to another, or when they are between the rays of two fortunes; this is called **veneration** <note>original: *veneratio*</note> by some. They also become fortunes when they are united with the Sun in the same degree, or when they are in its trine or sextile aspect, or in the trine or sextile aspect of the Moon; and also when they are increased in number <note>speed</note> and light, and when they are in any of their dignities or in their **haiz** <term>haiz: a complex state of "comfort" where a planet is in a sign and hemisphere that matches its own nature (day/night, masculine/feminine)</term>. And planets become stronger when they ascend in the **circle of the apogee** <note>original: *circulo augis*; the point in a planet's orbit furthest from Earth</note> and when they are northern or ascending in the north. Similarly, they are strengthened when they are in their **second station** <note>the moment a planet stops its retrograde motion to move forward again</note>, moving toward direct motion. Likewise, when they emerge from under the rays of the Sun <note>becoming visible in the sky again</note>, and when they are in angles or in succedents. And the three superior planets <note>Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars</note> are strengthened when—

It also lists the specific spatial and relational conditions—such as being in an 'angle' or 'haiz'—that make a planet strong and 'fortunate.'</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of planetary strength, specifically listing the conditions that make a planet "fortunate" (strong) or "unfortunate" (weakened). It moves from the benefits of solar aspects to a detailed list of debilities, such as being "combust" by the sun or "obsessed" by malevolent planets.</meta>

when they are oriental <note>rising before the sun in the morning</note>. And if they aspect the sun with a sextile <note>an aspect of 60 degrees</note> aspect, their strength is increased. Similarly, they are strengthened when they are in masculine quarters or masculine signs. The strength of the sun is also increased in masculine quarters and in masculine signs, except in Libra. For in Libra, the sun is not strengthened because it is in its "fall" from its place of exaltation <note>a "fall" is a sign where a planet's influence is weakened</note>, even though Libra is technically a masculine sign and located in a masculine quarter. Furthermore, the three "inferior" planets—namely those existing below the orbit of the sun <note>Venus, Mercury, and the Moon</note>—are signified as strong when they are occidental <note>setting after the sun</note> to the sun, and when they are in feminine quarters and feminine signs. The moon is strengthened when it is above the earth at night and below the earth during the day; also when it is in a feminine sign and a feminine place, and when it is in the exaltation of the sun, namely in Aries.

# Concerning the misfortune of the planets.

<margin>He says that planets are unfortunate</margin>
that is, they are "made unfortunate" when they are in aspects with the "malefics" <note>the "bad" planets, typically Saturn and Mars</note>, and when they are joined to these malefics by body <note>conjunction</note> or by aspect, such that the distance between them and the body or the rays of the malefic is less than the "term" <note>a specific division of a zodiac sign</note> of a single planet. They are also made unfortunate when they are in the terms of malefics or in the houses of malefics. For example: Jupiter or Venus are made unfortunate when they are in the terms of Saturn and Mars, or in their houses. They are also made unfortunate when the malefics are "elevated" above them, such that the malefics are in the 10th or 11th sign counting from the planet in question. Regarding this, it must be understood that a planet existing in the 10th sign from another is said to be "elevated" over it, just as a planet in the 10th house is elevated above the Ascendant <note>the rising sign</note>; and if this occurs without "reception" <note>a mutual dignity where planets "welcome" each other</note>, it is even worse. For example: I propose that Jupiter is at the beginning of Sagittarius and Saturn is at the beginning of Virgo. In this case, Saturn is elevated over Jupiter, for Virgo is the tenth sign from Sagittarius. 

Planets are also made unfortunate when they are in conjunction with the sun, or opposite the sun, or in its fourth <note>square, or 90 degree</note> aspect. They are also made unfortunate when they are at the "heads" of their *genzahar* <note>original: *genzahar*, a term derived from Persian referring to the nodes or the "dragon" where the planetary orbits intersect the ecliptic</note>; that is, at the intersections of their orbits with the ecliptic, whether at the "head" or "tail" of their "dragon" <note>the North or South Nodes</note>, or near these locations within 12 degrees or less. This is especially true if, in these places, the moon is hindered by them—or the sun, just like the moon—or if the sun or moon joins them in these places or looks upon them with a hostile aspect. The reason why they are made unfortunate in these places is because they can be eclipsed there. 

They are also made unfortunate when they are within four degrees of the sun, either before or behind it. Also, they suffer when they are placed between the bodies or rays of two malefics, such that they separate from one malefic body or aspect and immediately join another malefic; or if one malefic (or its rays) is in the sign before them and another malefic (or its rays) is in the sign after them. In such a case, the planet is said to be **obsessed** <note>original: *obsessus*, commonly known in astrology as "besieged"</note>. For example: I propose that Jupiter is in Virgo at the 2nd degree, while Mars (or the rays of Mars) is at the 1st degree of the same sign, and Saturn or Mars is at the 10th degree of that sign. In this case, Jupiter would be called "obsessed" by two unfortunates. In the same way, signs of the zodiac are said to be "obsessed" when one malefic is at the beginning of the sign and another is at its end. However, when the sun or a "fortune" <note>a lucky planet like Jupiter or Venus</note> looks upon the obsessed planet or sign with a sextile or third <note>trine, or 120 degree</note> aspect, and that aspect is within 7 degrees or less, then the "obsession" is dissolved by that aspect.

A planet is also made unfortunate when it is retrograde <note>appearing to move backward</note>, or "combust" <note>scorched by being too close to the sun</note>, or "falling" from the angles <note>the four primary points of a chart</note>. They are weakened when they are slow in their course—that is, when they move less than their average daily motion—and when they are in their "first station" <note>the pause before turning retrograde</note> moving toward retrogradation; or if they are in "dark degrees"; or if masculine planets are in feminine signs or degrees (and under the earth by day or above the earth by night); or if feminine planets are in masculine signs and degrees...

<meta>This page concludes the third "difference" or section of Alchabitius’s introductory text, focusing on the conditions that weaken planets. It then introduces the fourth "difference," which serves as a glossary and instructional guide for technical astrological terms and calculations used for predicting world events and individual fates. It specifically begins detailing the "great conjunctions" of Jupiter and Saturn, providing a specific historical timeframe (1325–1345 AD) for the current astrological era.</meta>

in masculine [signs] and during the day above the earth and at night below the earth. For this is contrary to their *haiz* <note>original: "haiz" - a technical term for a planet's dignity when it is in a sign and "quarter" of the sky that matches its own gender and relationship to the sun</note>, that is, their likeness. And they are weakened or made unfortunate when they are in locations opposite their dignities: or if they are in southern latitude away from the ecliptic <note>the sun's apparent path</note>, especially if they are descending in that same latitude; or if they are in the "combust path" <note>original: "via combusta" - a traditionally unlucky section of the zodiac, usually identified as the end of Libra and start of Scorpio</note>, namely from the midpoint of Libra to the midpoint of Scorpio; or if they are joined to a retrograde planet <note>a planet appearing to move backward</note>; or to a cadent planet <note>original: "cadenti" - one falling away from the powerful "angles" of the chart</note>; or if they are not "received" <note>original: "recepti" - mutual reception, where two planets occupy each other's home signs, allowing them to cooperate</note>. And the three superior planets—namely Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars—are weakened when they are occidental <note>setting after the sun</note> to the sun, or in feminine quarters. The sun is weakened in feminine signs and in feminine quarters, unless it is in the ninth house, because it "rejoices" <note>a term for when a planet is in a house where its influence is naturally boosted</note> in that house. The three inferior planets—namely Venus, Mercury, and the Moon—are weakened in masculine signs and in masculine quarters. ¶ And it is said of the planets that some are mutually loving. He says that certain planets love each other, and some hold each other in hatred, as is clear in the text. And thus the exposition of the third difference of Alchabitius's *Introduction to the Judgment [of the Stars]* is completed.

## The Fourth Difference: On the Explanation of Astrological Terms.

<margin>Completed three differences</margin>
<image-desc>A large decorative drop-cap "D" containing a floral design, set within a square frame with double-line borders.</image-desc>
With the differences of this book completed—in which the author determined the essential and accidental nature of the circle of signs in the first difference; the natures of the seven planets in the second difference; and those things which happen to the seven planets in themselves and to each other in the third difference—there follows the fourth difference. In this section, he explains the names used by the masters of judgments <note>professional astrologers</note> and teaches how to apply them to the work. And this entire difference can be divided into sixteen parts according to the sixteen chapters he places in it. In the first chapter, he speaks of the "great conjunctions," enumerating them. In the second chapter, he teaches how to investigate the ascending degree <note>the Ascendant</note> of any birth. In the third chapter, he teaches how to choose the "place of life" in nativities <note>birth charts</note>. In the fourth chapter, he teaches how to choose the "giver of the years of life" in nativities. In the fifth, he teaches how to choose the planet that is the ruler of the nativity, which presides over the newborn after the *hylech* and *alcochoden* <note>technical terms for the planets that determine the length of a person's life</note>. In the sixth, he treats the "profection" <note>a method of advancing the chart forward in time</note> of the years of the nativity and the world. In the seventh chapter, he teaches how to direct any significator <note>a planet representing a specific person or topic</note> to any place in the circle. In the eighth, he teaches how to direct the ascending degree in nativities. In the ninth, he teaches how to find the *duodenaria* <note>twelfth-parts</note> of the planets and houses. In the tenth, he teaches how to find the *novenaria* <note>ninth-parts</note>. In the eleventh, he teaches how to find the decan <note>a 10-degree division of a sign</note>. In the twelfth, he teaches how to find the lord of the orb or the hour. In the thirteenth, he teaches how to find the years of the *fridaria* <note>specific periods of life ruled by different planets</note> in nativities. In the fourteenth, he teaches how to recognize which of the planets is "elevated" above another. In the fifteenth, he teaches how to find when the "opening of the gates" <note>a technique used for weather prediction</note> occurs. In the sixteenth, he speaks of fortunate and unfortunate hours; where these parts begin will become clear in the course of the text. In the first chapter, therefore, he speaks generally about the great conjunctions and says that great conjunctions are things signifying destructions or changes that occur in this world, and they are six in number. ¶ The first, greater than all conjunctions, is the conjunction of Jupiter and Saturn at the beginning of Aries, and this happens once in 960 years. ¶ The second conjunction is the change of the conjunction of Jupiter and Saturn from one triplicity <note>a group of three signs belonging to the same element: Fire, Earth, Air, or Water</note> to another, and this happens once in 240 years. And when their conjunction comes to any triplicity, they are joined twelve times before it leaves that one. For example: the conjunction of Jupiter and Saturn is now in the "airy triplicity" and begins in the sign of Gemini in the year of our Lord 1325; from that time, in 20 years, namely in the year of our Lord 1345, they will be joined in water...

It specifically introduces the "great conjunctions" of Saturn and Jupiter, used by medieval scholars to predict major historical shifts and global changes.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of "great conjunctions" (the cyclical meetings of Jupiter and Saturn) and how they rotate through the four triplicities (groups of zodiac signs associated with the elements). It transitions into a list of all possible planetary combinations and then introduces the "Annimodar," a technical method for determining the exact degree of a person's birth sign.</meta>

<sig>b iiij</sig>

[Aquarius] and from that sign, in 20 years they will be joined in Libra, and afterward in Gemini: and so according to this order until they are joined twelve times in these three signs. And if you multiply 20 by 12, you will find 240 years, after which the conjunction will shift and enter the watery triplicity <note>original: "triplicitatem aquaticam" - the group of three zodiac signs associated with the element of water: Cancer, Scorpio, and Pisces</note> and will remain there similarly for 240 years; and you should understand the same regarding the other triplicities. And understand that just as it is said that the great conjunction returns to the beginning of Aries (that is, to the fiery triplicity) in 960 years, so a conjunction entering the beginning of any other triplicity does not return to the beginning of that same one except after 960 years. It will stay, therefore, as is said above, in each triplicity for 240 years. There are four triplicities. Multiply, therefore, 240 by four, and you will find 960 years.

The third conjunction is that of Mars and Saturn in Cancer every 30 years. The fourth conjunction is that of Jupiter and Saturn in whatever sign they happen to be, and it occurs once every 20 years. The fifth conjunction is the entry of the "great light" <note>the Sun</note> into the first minute of the vernal equinox, that is, Aries. The sixth conjunction is that of the Sun and Moon <note>the New Moon</note> and their opposition <note>the Full Moon</note>. And when the "ascendant of the great conjunction" is mentioned, it is understood to be the ascendant <note>the sign rising on the eastern horizon</note> at the moment of the Sun’s entry into Aries in that year in which the conjunction is due to occur. For example: the conjunction of Jupiter and Saturn was in Gemini in the year of our Lord 1325. And the ascendant of the Sun's entry into Aries in that year was 20 degrees of Taurus; therefore, Taurus is the ascendant of that conjunction. 

These conjunctions that have been described are called the "great conjunctions." There are many other conjunctions among the planets, the number of which is 120 according to Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy, a 2nd-century astronomer/astrologer whose work was the foundation of medieval science</note> in the 50th proposition of the *Centiloquium* <note>original: "centiloquij" - a famous collection of 100 astrological aphorisms attributed to Ptolemy</note>. For he says there: "Do not forget that there are 120 conjunctions that occur among the wandering stars [planets]." For in those resides the greatest knowledge of the things that happen in this world that undergo increase and decrease. Haly <note>original: "Daly" - referring to Haly Abenragel or Haly Abenrudian, famous Arabic commentators on Ptolemy</note> in his commentary there numbers them: and he says that some are binary conjunctions, some ternary, some quaternary, some quinary, some senary, and some septenary. A binary conjunction is when only two planets are joined, and this can vary in 21 ways. A ternary conjunction is when three planets are joined, and this can vary in 35 ways. A quaternary conjunction is when four planets are joined, and this can similarly vary in 35 ways. A quinary conjunction is when five planets are joined, and this can vary in 21 ways. A senary conjunction is when six planets are joined, and this can vary in 7 ways. A septenary is only one—namely, the conjunction of all seven planets. And if you calculate or add these predicted numbers together, you will find 120. Those who wish to count them individually will find them numbered in the commentary of the previously mentioned proposition.

# Concerning the Annimodar, which is this.

In this chapter, the author teaches how to investigate the degree of the ascendant of any birth <note>original: "natiuitatis" - a birth chart</note>, assuming that the rising sign is known but the specific degree is unknown. And he teaches this according to the opinion of Ptolemy, not according to his own intention. For Ptolemy says in the 34th proposition of the *Centiloquium*: The *Almuten* <note>original: "Almuſteli" or "Almuten" - the planet that has the most essential power or "dignity" over a specific point in the zodiac</note> over the place of the conjunction or prevention <note>the New or Full Moon immediately preceding the birth</note> is at a very similar degree to one of the angles of every human birth that occurs in that conjunction or prevention. And every degree that is not similar to the degree of the *Almuten* over the place of the conjunction or prevention is not an angle of any human birth. The meaning of the text—

<meta>This page follows the discussion of the "great conjunctions" of Saturn and Jupiter. Having finished describing the cycles of the triplicities, the author now turns to the "Annimodar," a technical astrological method used to rectify a birth chart—finding the exact degree of the Ascendant when the birth time is only known approximately. It transitions from general planetary cycles to the specific calculation of an individual's nativity.</meta>

It is such: when you wish to find the ascending degree of someone's birth <note>original: "nativitatis"</note>, consider the conjunction of the sun and moon that preceded the birth, or the opposition if an opposition preceded it. If a conjunction preceded it, the birth is called "conjunctional"; if an opposition preceded it—also called a "prevention" <note>original: "preventio" — a technical term for the full moon or syzygy occurring before a birth</note>, which is the same thing—the birth is called "preventional."

If the birth was conjunctional, consider the degree in which the lights <note>the Sun and Moon</note> were joined—namely, the degree of the zodiac in which the conjunction preceding that birth occurred—and see which of the planets is stronger in that degree by the multitude of testimonies <note>astrological "testimonies" are various dignities, like being the ruler of the sign or the term</note>. For that planet is the <term>almuten</term> <note>the planet with the most dignity or "power" over a specific point in the zodiac</note> over the place of the conjunction. Therefore, calculate its position <note>original: "equa" — meaning to calculate the planet's precise longitude for a specific time</note> for the hour of birth. Once calculated, establish the ascendant for the hour of birth according to estimation, yet in such a way that you are certain of the ascending sign. For if you were mistaken in the sign, this rule would be of no value to you.

Having therefore obtained the ascending sign at the hour of birth, consider whether the place of the planet you calculated (in its current arrangement) is closer to the degree of the ascendant or the degree of the tenth house <note>the Midheaven or "medium coeli," the highest point in the chart</note>. To whichever of these it is closer, you shall make that angle match the degree of the planet. That is: place as many degrees of the sign existing in that angle as the planet has passed through in the sign where it is located. 

And so that the meaning of the text may be more clearly revealed, I provide an example here.

¶ I suppose that at the hour of someone's birth, the ascendant was Libra, but I do not know which degree of it; and I suppose that the birth was conjunctional, and the conjunction preceding it was in the 6th degree of Aquarius. I see that Saturn is stronger in that degree; therefore, I will calculate Saturn for the hour of birth, and let it be supposed that I find it in the 20th degree of Virgo. I see that Saturn in this case is closer to the ascendant than to the midheaven <note>original: "medio celi"</note> according to the estimated ascendant; I will therefore make the angle of the ascendant match the degree of Saturn. That is, I will place the twentieth degree of Libra as the ascendant. And if it had been closer to the midheaven, I would have made the angle of the midheaven the twentieth degree of the sign existing there.

If, however, the birth was preventional, you will work with the degree of opposition just as I have told you regarding the degree of conjunction—that is, you will consider the planet having the most strengths in the degree of opposition. But there is a doubt concerning the degree of "prevention," since in prevention (or opposition), the sun is in one degree and the moon in the opposite; which degree, then, shall we take for the degree of prevention—the degree of the sun or the degree of the moon? The author says, according to the intention of Ptolemy, that we ought to take the degree of that light which was above the earth.

But a doubt still remains here. Suppose that one light is at the rising and the other at the setting, and thus neither will be above the earth nor below the earth. The author says that certain wise men have said we ought to work using the degree of that light which was in the east. 

Then the author says that Ptolemy said if several planets should agree in being the lord of the place of conjunction or prevention—namely, that two or three planets in that place have equal strengths, such that one has as many strengths there as the other—then we ought to take the one who is in its <term>haym</term> <note>also called "hayz" — a condition of strength where a planet is in a sign and house that matches its own nature and the time of day</note>; and if each of them were in its haym, then we ought to take the one which would more quickly be changed for the better—that is, the one which will more quickly enter a sign in which it has more strengths than in the sign where it is currently. And if they are oriental <note>rising before the sun in the morning</note> from the sun, you should choose the one which is closer to the sun, provided it is not <term>combust</term> <note>meaning "burnt" by being too close to the sun, which was thought to weaken a planet's power</note>.

¶ Concerning this part, it must be noted that there is another way of investigating the ascending degree of birth, and that method is taken from the opinion of Ptolemy in the 51st proposition of the <note>original: "Centiloquij" — a famous medieval collection of 100 astrological aphorisms attributed to Ptolemy</note> *Centiloquium*, where he says: "The place of the moon in the birth is the very degree..."

<summary>This page explains the "Annimodar" method for finding the exact degree of a person's birth sign by looking at the preceding New or Full Moon. It provides a detailed example using Saturn and discusses how to choose the most influential planet to determine the birth's precise timing.</summary>
<keywords>Annimodar, Ascendant, rectification, Ptolemy, Centiloquium, Almuten, Haym, syzygy, astrology, nativity</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for rectifying a birth chart. While the previous page discussed the "Almuten" (the most powerful planet) of the preceding conjunction, this page introduces the "Trutine of Hermes," a famous method that links the moment of conception to the moment of birth. It relies on the astrological principle that the Moon’s position at birth was the Ascendant at conception, and vice versa.</meta>

...the ascending degree <note>original: "gradus ascendens"</note> from the circle at the hour the seed falls into the womb, and the location of the moon at the hour the seed falls is the ascending degree at the hour of birth. It is also to be understood, for the sake of clarifying what is to be said, that the durations of infants in the wombs of their mothers are not equal, according to what Haly <note>Likely Haly Abenragel or Haly Ridwan, famous Arabic-language astrologers whose commentaries were standard in medieval Europe</note> says in the commentary of the proposition already cited. <margin>The durations of infants in the womb are three: major, middle, and minor</margin> These durations are distinguished into three types: namely, the major duration, the middle, and the minor. The minor duration contains 258 days. The middle duration contains 273 days. The major duration contains 288 days. And the difference between the major and the minor is 30 days. This is to be understood regarding those who are born according to the natural course. However, for those born prematurely <note>original: "abortiuis"</note> or those born in the seventh month, this rule does not hold. 

The length of the infant's stay in the womb is determined from the location of the moon in the chart of the hour of birth. For if, at the hour of birth, the moon was at the beginning of the seventh house above the earth—that is, above the western horizon—then the infant was in the mother's womb for the minor duration, namely for 258 days. <margin>Minor 258, Middle 273, Major 288</margin> And if the moon, at the hour of birth, was in the ascending degree <term>Ascendant</term> <note>the degree of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon at the moment of birth</note>, then the infant stayed in the womb for the middle duration, namely for 273 days. And if the moon was, at the hour of birth, in the degree which is before the western degree <note>the Descendant</note>, then the infant stayed in the womb for the major duration, namely 288 days. 

Note that when the moon at birth is in the western part near the horizon, if the astronomer believes it to be above the earth when it is actually below the earth, or vice versa, he will err in the hour of conception by 30 days. If, however, the moon was above the earth, distant from the west—that is, between the east and the west—the stay in the womb is longer than the minor duration and shorter than the middle. I say it is longer than the minor duration according to the amount of the moon's distance from the west. And if the moon was below the earth, distant from the ascendant, the stay in the womb is longer than the middle and shorter than the major, and it is longer than the middle according to the amount of distance from the ascendant.

When you wish to investigate the degree of the birth's ascendant by this path, establish the ascendant for the hour of birth according to the closest estimation you can get to the truth, and calculate the moon's position <note>original: "equa lunam" — finding its precise longitude</note> for that hour. See where it falls in the figure: if it falls at the beginning of the seventh house <note>the setting point</note>, subtract 258 days from the hour of birth. The result will be the hour of conception. 

If, however, it falls between the seventh house and the ascendant (that is, above the earth), see by how many degrees it is distant from the western degree; for every 12 degrees, take one day, and for every degree that remains beyond 12, take two hours. Add these days and hours to the minor duration (that is, to 258 days). Subtract the total from the hour of birth, and the time of conception will remain. 

If it falls in the ascendant, subtract 273 days from the hour of birth, and the time of conception will remain. If it falls between the ascendant and the seventh house (that is, below the earth), consider how far it is distant from the ascending degree; for every 12 degrees take one day, and for every remaining degree take two hours. Add the resulting number of days and hours to the middle duration, and subtract the total from the hour of birth. 

If the moon falls at the end of the sixth house, subtract the major duration from the hour of birth, and the hour of conception remains. Therefore, having noted the hour of conception, calculate the moon's position for that hour; the degree in which it is found was the ascendant at the hour of birth. 

Although this way is true according to the intention of almost all the wise men, it nevertheless seems to me—and I am certain—that it is not sufficient for investigating the degree in a case where I do not have the ascending degree except by estimation. For it presupposes that I know the distance...

<summary>This page explains the Trutine of Hermes, a method used to find an exact birth time by calculating the date of conception based on the Moon's position at birth. It defines three standard lengths of pregnancy (258, 273, and 288 days) and provides a mathematical formula for adjusting these based on the Moon's distance from the horizon.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the discussion on the Trutine of Hermes, which links the timing of birth to the timing of conception. It transitions into the method for identifying the "Hyleg," the planet or point in a birth chart that determines a person's life force and longevity.</meta>

...the moon's distance from the western degree <note>the descendant</note> or from the ascendant: and if I am ignorant of the ascending degree and consequently the descending degree, I do not know how I will be able to know the distance of the moon from either of them. For if it were supposed that my estimation fails by 12 degrees, the distance of the moon from the west or from the ascendant will also fail by 12 degrees, and for those degrees one day ought to be taken. Thus, the hour of conception will be off by one day, and the final error—that is, in the ascending degree—will be as much as the moon moves in one day.

# On the Hyleg <note>original: "Ex hoc hylech"</note>
<image-desc>Large decorative woodcut initial 'E' for the word 'Ex', depicting a scholar or astronomer seated at a desk with an open book, within a rectangular frame.</image-desc>
In this chapter, the author teaches how to choose the hyleg <term>hyleg: a technical term from Arabic astrology referring to the "Giver of Life," the point or planet in a chart used to calculate a person's lifespan</term>, that is, the significator of life in nativities <note>birth charts</note>. The meaning of the text is as follows: In choosing the significator of life, we must begin with the sun if the birth occurred during the day. If we find it before the ascending degree by 5 degrees or less—that is, if we find the sun itself in the 5 degrees that have ascended above the horizon at the end of the 12th house—or if we find it in the 10th house or the 1st house, we shall take the sun as the hyleg, whether it is in masculine or feminine signs. 

If, however, it is not in these signs <note>positions</note>, we must see if it is in the 7th, 8th, or 9th house; in masculine signs, we would still take it as the hyleg. But if it were in these three aforementioned places—the 7th, 8th, or 9th house—in feminine signs, it would not be fit to be the hyleg. 

If, therefore, the sun is not fit to be the hyleg, we shall look to the moon. If we find it in the ascendant, or in the 2nd or 3rd house, or in the 7th or 8th house, it will be the hyleg, whether it is in a masculine or feminine sign. If it is not in these places, we shall look to see if we find it before the degree of the ascending sign by 5 degrees or less, or in the 10th, 4th, or 5th house in a feminine sign; then it will still be the hyleg. If, however, it were in masculine signs in these places, it would not be fit as the hyleg.

But if the birth occurred at night, we must begin first with the moon. If we find it before the western degree <note>the descendant</note> by 5 degrees or less—that is, in those 5 degrees which are below the earth on the western side—or in any other of the aforementioned places according to the stated method, it will be the hyleg. And if it is not fit, we shall look to the sun. If the sun is before the degree of the 7th house by 5 degrees or less—that is, below the earth—or if it is in the 4th or 5th house, it will be fit as the hyleg, whether the signs are masculine or feminine. And if it is in the ascendant or in the 2nd house in a masculine sign, it will be fit as the hyleg; but in a feminine sign, it will not. 

If the luminaries <note>the sun and moon</note> are not fit to be the hyleg, we shall look at whether a conjunction or an opposition preceded the birth. If a conjunction preceded it, we shall look at the degree of conjunction—that is, that degree in which the luminaries were joined before the birth—and if we find that degree in any of the four angles <term>angles: the 1st, 4th, 7th, and 10th houses, the most potent points in a horoscope</term> or in any of the succedent houses <term>succedent: the 2nd, 5th, 8th, and 11th houses, which "follow" the angles</term>, we shall take it for the hyleg. 

And if we do not find it in any of the angles or succedents, we must look to the degree of the Part of Fortune <term>Part of Fortune: a calculated point in the chart representing luck and physical well-being</term>. If it is in any of the angles or succedents, we must take it for the hyleg. If it is not in any of these places, we must finally take the ascending degree of the birth. 

If, however, an opposition preceded the birth, we must look at the degree of opposition just as was said regarding the degree of conjunction, and afterward to the Part of Fortune, and lastly to the ascending degree of the birth. It is not necessary to check whether the signs are masculine or feminine for the degree of conjunction, the preceding opposition <note>original: "prouentionis"</note>, or the Part of Fortune. And thus it is clear that the sun can be the hyleg by day or night, above the earth or below the earth.

| Term | Definition |
| :--- | :--- |
| **hylech** | The Giver of Life; the primary significator of vitality. |
| **natiuitas** | Nativity; a birth or the astrological chart of a birth. |

| **luna** | The Moon. |
| **ascendens** | The Ascendant; the degree of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon. |
| **occidens** | The Descendant; the degree of the zodiac setting on the western horizon. |
| **domus** | House; one of the twelve divisions of the astrological sky. |
| **coniunctio** | Conjunction; when the sun and moon are at the same degree (New Moon). |
| **oppositio** | Opposition; when the sun and moon are 180 degrees apart (Full Moon). |
| **pars fortune** | Part of Fortune; an Arabic Part calculated from the Sun, Moon, and Ascendant. |
| **angulus** | Angle; the 1st, 4th, 7th, or 10th houses. |
| **succedentia** | Succedent houses; the 2nd, 5th, 8th, or 11th houses. |

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for determining the "Hyleg" or significator of life, transitioning from the sun’s placement to the moon’s potential roles and the specific requirements for planetary "dignity."</meta>

In these two places. For the hyleg <term>hyleg: the "Giver of Life," a point in the chart used to determine longevity</term> can be above the earth in six places and below the earth in five. And the moon, similarly, can be the hyleg in three places. For it can be hyleg below the earth in six places and above the earth in five. If, however, the moon in any of the aforementioned places should be combust <term>combust: a planet is "combust" when it is so close to the sun that its influence is "burned up" or hidden by the sun's light</term> under the rays of the sun, it will not be fit to be the hyleg. 

And when the houses have been equated according to the doctrine passed down in the rules of the astrolabe and the tables of the *primum mobile* <note>original: "primi mobilis," the "first mover" or the outermost celestial sphere in medieval cosmology</note>, every planet that is five degrees or less before the ascendant or before the beginning of any other house shall have its strength valid in the house that succeeds it. 

And none of the aforementioned places can be the hyleg unless some planet having "dignity" <note>astrological influence or "rank"</note> in that place beholds it—that is, whether it be the lord of the house, or the lord of the exaltation, or of the triplicity, or of the term, or of the face <note>these are the five essential dignities of traditional astrology</note>. 

The whole sense of the text can be reduced to a few words: There are five places from which the hyleg, that is, the significator of life, is received, which are these: the sun, the moon, the degree of the conjunction or prevention <note>the most recent New Moon or Full Moon before birth</note>, the part of fortune, and the degree of the ascendant of the nativity <note>birth chart</note>.

If the birth was during the day, one must begin with the sun. And if it is not in the fit places according to what was said before, the moon must be taken. And if the moon is not fit, the degree of the conjunction or prevention must be taken; if that is not fit, the part of fortune must be taken. And if that is not fit, the degree of the ascendant of the birth must be taken. 

Ptolemy <note>the famous 2nd-century Greco-Egyptian astronomer/astrologer</note> says that the hyleg should never 
<margin><image-desc>A manicula (pointing hand) is drawn in the right margin, pointing towards the line beginning "qʒ debeat accipi..."</image-desc></margin>be taken from any place existing below the earth. For he says—and it is appropriate—that we should not work on a matter as noble as this through anything that is below the earth, but always through that which ascends and appears above the earth. Nor is it suitable for us to work through a sign that the ascendant does not behold <note>aspect</note>, nor through the sign that ascends before the ascendant, which is called the House of Labor—that is, the 12th house—because it is "falling" from an angle, and even more so: because the vapors <note>original: "fumosities"</note> that rise from the moisture of the earth disturb its power as it comes from the sign to the earth with its thickness and gloom. It damages the power because the colors and sizes of the stars existing in this house appear to be of a different sort than they are by their own nature.

Haly Abenragel <note>a prominent 11th-century Arab astrologer</note> says the wise are much in disagreement on this; however, that which the greater part of them agrees upon is that for diurnal <note>daytime</note> births, they begin first with the sun. If it is in any of the angles or succeeding houses, in a masculine sign and in a masculine quarter, and it aspects any of its own dignities, it shall be the hyleg itself. If it is not so, and it does not aspect any of its dignities, it will not be fit for the hyleg. 

Then you shall seek the hyleg from the moon; and if you find it in an angle or a succeeding house, and in a feminine sign and a feminine quarter, and it aspects any of its dignities, take it for the hyleg; otherwise, it will not be fit. Then look at the birth: if it was a "conjunctional" birth <note>occurring after a New Moon</note>, take the hyleg from the ascending degree; and if any lord having some dignity there aspects that degree, you shall take it for the hyleg. And if the ascendant is not fit, seek the hyleg from the part of fortune. And if the part of fortune is not fit, seek the hyleg from the place of the conjunction or opposition preceding the birth. And if these are not fit, the birth shall not have a hyleg. 

And it will be necessary for you to return to directing <note>a timing technique</note> through the *atazir* <term>atazir: from the Arabic "al-tasyir," a method of planetary direction used to predict major life events</term> the places of the hyleg to the places of the Fortunes <note>benefic planets like Jupiter or Venus</note> and to their rays, and there you will find the place of the end <note>the end of life</note>. And if the birth was nocturnal <note>at night</note>, you shall first seek the hyleg from the moon, just as you began to seek it from the sun by day. And if you find it in an angle or succeeding house, in a feminine sign and in a feminine quarter, and it aspects any of its dignities, it shall be the hyleg. Otherwise, seek the hyleg...

It cites the authorities of Ptolemy and Haly Abenragel to explain why certain houses, like the 12th, are unsuitable due to atmospheric interference.</summary>

[the hy]lech from the sun, and if you find it in an angle <term>angle: the 1st, 4th, 7th, or 10th houses, which are the most powerful and active areas of a chart</term> or in a succedent house <term>succedent: the 2nd, 5th, 8th, or 11th houses, which "succeed" or follow the powerful angles</term> in a masculine sign and in a masculine quadrant, and if a planet having "dignity" <note>essential astrological influence or rank</note> there beholds it, you shall take it as the hyleg <term>hyleg: the "Giver of Life," a point in the chart used to determine longevity</term>; otherwise it will not be fit. And then, if the birth occurred during a full moon <note>original: "praeventionalis," referring to the "prevention" or full moon preceding birth</note>, you should seek the hyleg from the Part of Fortune just as you sought it from the Moon and Sun. And if the Part of Fortune is not fit, seek the hyleg from the degree of the ascendant <term>ascendant: the sign rising on the eastern horizon at the moment of birth</term>. And if any of these are not fit for the hyleg, seek the hyleg from the degree of the conjunction or prevention preceding the birth. And if it is in an angle or a succedent house, and is aspected by a planet having any of the five dignities there, accept it as the hyleg. 

And when the Sun is the hyleg and does not have an alcochoden <term>alcochoden: the "Giver of Years," a planet that aspects the Hyleg and determines the potential length of a person's life</term>, seek the hyleg from the degree of the Moon. And if that—that is, the degree of the Moon—is the hyleg and has no alcochoden, seek the hyleg from the degree of the ascendant if the birth was at a new moon <note>original: "coniunctionalis," referring to the conjunction of the Sun and Moon</note>; and if that degree is the hyleg and has no alcochoden, seek the hyleg from the Part of Fortune. However, if the birth was at a full moon, start first from the Part of Fortune; and if it is the hyleg and has no alcochoden, seek the hyleg from the degree of conjunction or opposition preceding the birth. And if none of these has an alcochoden, since the newborn has neither a hyleg nor an alcochoden, it is a sign that life will be of brief duration. 

And know that for the hyleg which is sought from the ascendant, the Part of Fortune, and the degree of conjunction or prevention, the masculinity or femininity of the signs and quadrants is not considered. And the greater part of the wise say that the Moon in the third house is fit to be the hyleg, because she "rejoices" in it <note>a planet "rejoices" in a specific house where its nature is particularly well-expressed</note>; and they say similarly that it is fitting for the Sun in the ninth house to be the hyleg because he rejoices there.

# Concerning this Alcochoden.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'E' showing a scholar or clerk seated at a desk, surrounded by an ornate frame.</image-desc>

To choose the alcochoden, which is the giver of the years of life. The meaning of the text is as follows: when you wish to know which of the planets is the giver of the years of life, we must look at which of the planets has the most dignities or strengths in the place of the hyleg. For example: if the Sun is the hyleg, we must look for the strongest planet in the place of the Sun. And if the Moon is the hyleg, we must look for the strongest in the place of the Moon; and the same must be understood for other places. And if such a planet "aspects" <note>forms a specific geometric relationship/angle</note> the place of the hyleg by any aspect, we must accept it as the alcochoden. 

If, however, it does not aspect it, we must look for the next one after it—namely, the planet that has some strengths in the place of the hyleg, but not as many as the first lord of the degree. For example: if one has six strengths in the place of the hyleg and another has 5 or 4 strengths there, if the first does not aspect the place of the hyleg, it cannot be the alcochoden. Then, if the second aspects the place of the hyleg, we must take it as the alcochoden; and if that one also does not aspect it, we must look again for another having fewer dignities, and so we must proceed until we find someone aspecting the place of the hyleg who has some dignity in the place of the hyleg. And if we find no planet aspecting the place of the hyleg that has a dignity or dignities there, that place cannot be the hyleg; therefore, in this case, it is necessary to seek another hyleg according to the method previously described. 

For example: if the birth was by day and the Sun was in a place fit to be the hyleg, but no one has dignity in the Sun's place while aspecting him, the Sun cannot be the hyleg because he has no alcochoden; therefore, it is then necessary to seek the hyleg from the Moon. And if she is in a place fit for the hyleg and no one having...

<summary>This page outlines the hierarchical procedure for identifying the Giver of Life (Hyleg) and the Giver of Years (Alcochoden) in a birth chart. It emphasizes that a potential Hyleg is invalid if it lacks an Alcochoden to "vouch" for its years, a condition that portends a short life for the newborn.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for determining the Alcochoden (Giver of Years) once the Hyleg (Giver of Life) has been identified. It discusses the hierarchy of planetary dignities and introduces the method of Haly Abenragel for calculating the specific number of years granted to the native based on planetary house positions.</meta>

...in that place, looking for one that holds dignity and beholds it <note>original: "sit aspiciens," meaning the planet must form an aspect or geometric relationship with the Hyleg</note> to seek the **alcochoden** <term>alcochoden: the "Giver of Years," a planet that determines the potential length of life based on its strength and position</term> from the Part of Fortune, or from the degree of conjunction or prevention <note>the New or Full Moon prior to birth</note>, or the ascending degree, according to the previously described order. And it seemed to some that one should begin with the Lord of the House; namely, that if the Lord of the House of the place of the **hyleg** aspects the **hyleg**, they took it as the **alcochoden** and did not look for any other. But if the Lord of the House did not aspect it, they looked to the Lord of the Exaltation; if that one aspected it, they established it as the **alcochoden**. And if that one did not aspect it, they looked to the Lord of the Triplicity; then to the Lord of the Term; then and finally to the Lord of the Face <term>Face: also known as a decan, the least powerful of the five "essential dignities" of a planet</term>. **Dorotheus** <note>Dorotheus of Sidon, a 1st-century astrological poet</note> first looked to the Lord of the Term and preferred it over the Lord of the House, but this does not seem consistent with the truth. 

And some have said that if two or three or more planets meet in dignities such that they have equal essential strengths in the place of the **hyleg**, and all aspect that place, the one that aspects from a closer position is to be taken. And if they are also equal in this—which rarely happens—the one who is in a "laudable state" <note>original: "esse laudabili," a favorable condition</note> from the **Sun** is to be taken, such as one joined with the **Sun** in the same degree and not separated from it by more than 16 minutes <term>cazimi: a planet "in the heart" of the Sun, considered exceptionally strong</term>. Or the one who is closer to an angle, or the one at the beginning of its morning rising—that is, which begins to appear in the morning before sunrise—or the one in its second station and moving toward direct motion, or one in another laudable state from the **Sun**; namely, that it be "oriental" if it is one of the three superior planets <note>Mars, Jupiter, or Saturn</note>, or "occidental" if it is one of the three inferior planets <note>Moon, Mercury, or Venus</note>. 

If it should happen that the **Sun** is the **hyleg** and is in **Leo** or **Aries**—that is, in its own house or exaltation—it shall be both **hyleg** and **alcochoden**. And if the **Moon** is the **hyleg** and is in **Cancer** or **Taurus**, she shall be both **hyleg** and **alcochoden**, and it is not necessary to consider any other. **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the influential 2nd-century astronomer and astrologer</note> says that the **alcochoden** is the one who has more strengths in the place of the **hyleg**, whether it aspects that place or does not aspect it. But none of the wise men agree with him; therefore, it seems safer to me to hold to the way of the wise.

<margin><image-desc>Hand-drawn decorative bracket spanning lines 26 to 30</image-desc></margin>### Concerning the years which the alcochoden gives
Regarding the years which the **alcochoden** gives, although the author says nothing, I nevertheless wish to recount a few things more consistent with the truth according to the opinion of **Haly Abenragel** <note>Abu l-Hasan 'Ali ibn Abi l-Rijal, a 11th-century Arab astrologer</note>. **Haly Abenragel** says the greater part of the experts in this science say that when the **alcochoden** is in the angles, it gives its "greater years"; when in the succedent houses, it gives its "middle years"; and when in the cadent houses, it gives its "lesser years." Nor do they give any improvement to one succedent house over another, and this is an error. 

**Haly Abenragel** says: know that among the covered secrets and hidden signs of this science is this: when the **alcochoden** is at the degree of the tenth house, it gives its greater years. And when it is at the degree of the eleventh house, it gives its middle years. And when it is removed from one of these two places and moves toward the other, see how many degrees there are from one to the other and divide by them the years that are between the greater years and the middle years. 

**For example:** I suppose the degree of the 10th house is the 3rd degree of **Pisces**, and I suppose **Venus** is the **alcochoden** and is in the 13th degree of **Pisces**; she is therefore distant from the point of the 10th house by 10 degrees. And I suppose that from the beginning of the 10th house to the beginning of the 11th, there are 30 degrees. I know that if she were at the beginning of the 10th house, she would give the greater years, which are 82. And if she were at the beginning of the 11th house, she would give her middle years, which are 45. Now, however, she is at neither of these two points. I will therefore subtract the 45 years—the middle ones—from the greater years—the 82—and 37 years remain. I will multiply these by the distance of **Venus** from the beginning of the tenth house, which results in 370; and I will divide by 30—that is, by the length in...

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for determining the Alcochoden (Giver of Years). ...between the tenth house and the eleventh, and 12 years will result, and 10 remain after the division, which I will multiply by 12 and divide by 30, and four months will result. Therefore, I will subtract 12 years and four months from the **greater years** of **Venus** <note>In the medieval system, Venus’s "greater years" are 82. Subtracting 12 years and 4 months correctly leaves 69 years and 8 months.</note>, and 69 years and 8 months will remain; these will be the years that **Venus** gives in such a case. You can apply this example to other angles and houses. 

**Haly Abenragel** <note>Abu l-Hasan 'Ali ibn Abi l-Rijal, an 11th-century Arab astrologer whose work was a primary source for European practitioners.</note> says that when the **alcochoden** <term>alcochoden: the "Giver of Years," a planet that determines the potential length of life based on its strength and position</term> is **combust** <term>combust: a planet is "combust" when it is so close to the Sun that its influence is "burned up" or hidden by the Sun's rays.</term> and is moving toward the Sun so that it cannot be seen, it loses all of its given years. For when it is in this state, it gives only days or hours, or things that have no name <note>meaning insignificant or negligible spans of time.</note>. 

And the limit of **combustion** is as follows: After **Saturn** is **occidental** <term>occidental: setting after the Sun, visible in the evening.</term> from the Sun, and the Sun moves toward it and there are 15 degrees between them, until it remains and becomes **oriental** <term>oriental: rising before the Sun, visible in the morning.</term> and there are ten full degrees between them. And **Jupiter** is the same. **Mars**, however, after there are 15 degrees between it and the Sun in the west, until it is oriental and there are nine degrees between them. 

**Mercury**, however, carries out its actions and gives its full gifts as long as it is **direct** <note>moving forward through the zodiac, rather than appearing to move backward in retrograde.</note>; provided that it is not closer to the Sun than seven degrees. From seven degrees down to 5, it gives, but not fully. But from the start of seven degrees until it passes the Sun by five degrees, it loses all of its given time. And from five degrees until the completion of seven degrees <note>after it has passed the Sun</note>, it gives a little; truly, after it has passed 7 degrees, it gives its full gift. And **Venus** does the same thing, except that when it appears visibly at the least of these five degrees, it then gives its full gift. 

The **Moon** <note>original: "Luna"</note>, however, when it applies to the Sun by moving toward it and there are 15 degrees between them, its action is weakened and it gives a small thing—namely <note>original: "s." for "scilicet"</note>, days or something similar to this. And when it has applied to the Sun at 12 degrees, it loses all of its gift until it has passed the degree of the Sun by 10 degrees; then it gives a small gift, namely days or something similar, until it has passed the Sun by 12 degrees, and then it will give its full and complete gift. 

The **fall** <term>fall: also called "casus," the sign where a planet is at its weakest, opposite its sign of "exaltation."</term> of a planet, which is the opposite of its exaltation, takes away half of the gift. And the limit of this fall is as follows: the **Sun**, when it is in the 5th degree of **Libra** until it passes the 25th degree of the same, is in its fall. The remainder of the sign shall be judged as its **detriment** <term>detriment: a sign where a planet is poorly placed and "out of its element," traditionally considered less severe than a "fall."</term>. 

**Saturn** is in its fall from the start of the 12th degree of **Aries** until it passes the 26th degree of the same; and the remainder of the sign shall be judged as detriment. **Jupiter** is in its fall from the 4th degree of **Capricorn** up to the completion of 20 degrees; and the remainder of the sign shall be judged as detriment. **Mars** is in its fall from the start of the 19th degree of **Cancer** until the end of Cancer itself; and the start of that sign shall be judged as detriment. 

And **Venus** is in its fall after it has passed the **term** <term>term: a specific set of degrees within a sign ruled by a planet, a minor form of planetary dignity.</term> of **Jupiter** up to the 21st degree of **Virgo**. This statement about **Venus** cannot stand unless you understand it according to the terms of **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century author of the *Tetrabiblos*.</note>. For according to those terms, the 14th degree of **Virgo** is the end of the term of **Jupiter**. But according to the terms set down in **Alchabitius** <note>Al-Qabisi, a 10th-century Arab astrologer whose introductory text was standard in medieval universities.</note>, it is not so; for there, the term of **Jupiter** ends at the 21st degree of **Virgo**. Therefore, either the text of **Haly** is corrupt, or his saying cannot be understood except according to the terms of **Ptolemy**. 

And **Mercury** is in its fall from the start of the 8th degree of **Pisces** until the completion of its 19th degree; and in the remainder of that same sign, it is judged as if it were in its detriment. The **retrogradation** <term>retrogradation: the apparent backward motion of a planet as seen from Earth.</term> of the three superior planets <note>Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars.</note> from the start of their retrogradation until the opposition of the Sun—that is, until the midpoint of the retrogradation—takes away half of the gift, and the other half remains. And when it has passed the opposition of the Sun, look at how many degrees and minutes it will go while retrograding from the opposition of the Sun, and...

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for calculating the lifespan based on the Alcochoden (Giver of Years). It specifically addresses how to adjust the years given by a planet when it is in retrograde motion or influenced by aspects from other planets.</meta>

Look at how many [degrees] there are, and what proportional part they have to all the degrees it must **retrograde** <term>retrograde: the apparent backward motion of a planet in the sky.</term> from the point of **opposition** <term>opposition: when a planet is 180 degrees away from the Sun.</term> until it returns to **direct** motion. Then, you should add that to the half of the given [years] and calculate those degrees correctly with their minutes in such a way that when the planet is at the beginning of its **direction** <note>the point where it stops moving backward and starts moving forward again.</note>, it will give its full allotted time.

I provide an example of this: suppose that **Mars** was the **alcochoden** <term>alcochoden: the planet that "gives" the years of life.</term> and its total gift was 60 years <note>original: "60 anni"</note>, according to the proportion previously calculated between the angle and the succeeding house. And suppose that the planet is itself **retrograde**, exactly at the midpoint of its **retrogradation**. In the middle of its **retrogradation** and before that midpoint, it has lost half of these years, and 30 years remain. 

Now, suppose that it has moved two degrees beyond the midpoint of its **retrogradation** (that is, past its **opposition** to the **Sun**), and suppose that from that **opposition** until it becomes **direct**, it must travel 10 degrees in **retrograde**. I will therefore multiply those two degrees by the half of the gift that was lost (namely, by 30 years), which results in 60. I will then divide that 60 by 10, and 6 years will be the result. I will add these 6 years to the half of the gift (the 30 years), and 36 years will result. This would be the gift of **Mars** in the case proposed.

Then **Venus** and **Mercury**, after each of them begins to **retrograde**, lose half of the gift they give when they are **direct**. Afterward, look as accurately as you can at how many degrees and minutes remain for them until their **occultation** <term>occultation: here referring to "combustion," when a planet is hidden by the Sun's light.</term>. For **Venus** may perhaps appear even when the distance between it and the **Sun** is less than 5 degrees. When it is visible, it will give half its gift, unless it appeared while being less than 5 degrees from the **Sun**, because then that appearance is of no benefit to it in this state. Afterward, you should calculate those degrees and minutes from the moment it began to **retrograde** until it hidden itself, subtly and correctly, and divide them by this half-gift that remained in the aforementioned manner. For when it is fully hidden, it loses all its gift, as we said before, because nothing remains to it. This can be modeled similarly to the example given for the **retrogradation** of the three superior planets <note>The "superior planets" are Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars.</note>.

**Haly** <note>Abu l-Hasan 'Ali ibn Abi l-Rijal.</note> says that **Jupiter** and **Venus**, when they are joined bodily <term>joined bodily: in a mathematical conjunction, occupying the same degree of the zodiac.</term> or when they **aspect** the **alcochoden** by a **trine** <term>trine: an aspect of 120 degrees, considered very harmonious.</term> or **sextile** <term>sextile: an aspect of 60 degrees, considered helpful.</term>, they add to the number of years the **alcochoden** gave, according to the number of their own years—adding years, months, days, or hours according to their strengths or weaknesses. 

However, both **infortunes** <term>infortunes: "malefic" planets, specifically Saturn and Mars, believed to bring challenge or hardship.</term>, **Saturn** and **Mars**, when they are joined bodily with the **alcochoden** or **aspect** it from an **opposition** or a **square** <term>square: an aspect of 90 degrees, considered tense or difficult.</term>, they subtract from its number [of years] according to their **lesser years**. But when **Mercury** is with the **fortunes** <term>fortunes: "benefic" planets, Jupiter and Venus, believed to bring growth and harmony.</term> that are adding to the **alcochoden**, it likewise adds the number of its **lesser years**; and when it is with the **infortunes** that are subtracting, it also subtracts its portion. 

When **Mars** and **Saturn** **aspect** by a **trine** from signs of **short ascension** or by a **sextile** from signs of **long ascension** <note>Signs of short ascension rise more quickly over the horizon; long ascension rise more slowly. This technical distinction changes how "friendly" an aspect is.</note>, they subtract. And I say that the **Sun**, in **square** and **opposition**, subtracts the number of its **lesser years**, and it adds [years] from a **trine** and **sextile**. Many of the ancient and modern astrologers say that a fortunate **Moon** adds [years] from a **trine** or **sextile**. 

And all the wise men of this science confess that when a native does not have a **hylech** <term>hylech: the "Life-Giver," the primary point in a chart used to determine the length of life.</term>, but has either of the two **fortunes** in the **Ascendant** or the **Midheaven**, it is possible that they will live according to the quantity of the **lesser years** of that **fortune** which signified life—unless the degree of the **Ascendant** and the **Moon** were afflicted by an **infortune**, or if that same **fortune** was the lord of the **house of death** <note>The eighth house of the zodiac.</note>; for then it signifies only a small amount of durability and life.
