# The Almuten who presides over the birth.

Here the author teaches how to choose the **almuten** <term>almuten: the planet with the most dignity and power across several points in the birth chart.</term>, that is, the ruling planet in the figure from which the native receives their being, after the **hylech** and the **alcochoden**. The meaning of the text is...

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for determining the most powerful planet in a birth chart (the Almuten) and begins a new chapter on "Profections," a method of symbolic timing used to predict events in a specific year of a person's life.</meta>

...these. The planet that has the greatest lordship or the most "strengths" <note>The author is referring to 'essential dignities,' a system where planets gain power by being in specific signs or degrees.</note> in the five aforementioned places from which the **hylech** <term>hylech: the "giver of life," a point in the chart used to determine a person's lifespan.</term> is taken—which are the position of the **Sun**, the position of the **Moon**, the position of the prenatal **conjunction** or **prevention** <note>The New Moon or Full Moon immediately preceding birth.</note>, the **Lot of Fortune** <term>Lot of Fortune: a mathematical point in the chart representing well-being and luck.</term>, and the degree of the **Ascendant** <term>Ascendant: the sign rising on the eastern horizon at the moment of birth.</term>—that planet is the **almuten**, which means "the victor." 

And if any planet possesses dignities in a second, third, or even more of these aforementioned places, that one is the **almuten**. For example: I propose that one planet is the lord of the **Ascendant**, and also the lord of the **term** <note>A subdivision of a zodiac sign.</note> of that same **Ascendant**, and additionally the lord of the **face** <note>Also known as a decan, a 10-degree subdivision of a sign.</note> of the same. Such a planet would have 8 strengths in the **Ascendant**. But I propose that another planet is the lord of the **exaltation** of the **Ascendant**, the lord of the **house** where the **Sun** is located, and the lord of the **triplicity** <note>A group of three signs belonging to the same element (Fire, Earth, Air, or Water).</note> of the **Lot of Fortune**: such a planet would be a worthier **almuten** than the first, because it has more strengths across the various aforementioned places. To keep the explanation brief, one must collect the strengths of every planet in these five places; whichever one has the highest total number of strengths shall be the **almuten**.

# From this follows the Profection in the years of births and of the world.

<margin>
In this chapter, he teaches the work of profection, that is, the changing of the years.
</margin>

...from one sign to another; and this is done in two ways. First, in the years of **nativities** <note>Individual birth charts.</note>. Second, in the years of **great conjunctions** <note>Rare alignments of Jupiter and Saturn used for mundane or world astrology.</note>. The work regarding the years of **nativities** is easy, and the meaning of the text is as follows: when you wish to know which sign the current year of someone’s birth applies to, see how many completed years have passed since the birth. Start from the degree of the **Ascendant** of the birth and assign one sign to each year. Where the number ends, that will be the sign of the **profection** <term>profection: a technique where the chart is "rotated" by one sign (30 degrees) for every year of life.</term> from the **Ascendant**. 

For example: the **Ascendant** of a certain birth was at 10 degrees of **Aries**. At the end of the first year, the **profection** reaches 10 degrees of **Taurus**. At the end of the second year, it reaches 10 degrees of **Gemini**; and so on according to this order, giving each year one sign until the completion of 12 years. At that point—namely, at the end of 12 years—it returns to the place of the **root** <term>root: also called the 'radix,' the original positions of the planets at the moment of birth.</term>. 

If, however, many years have passed since the birth, subtract 12 from the total number of completed years as many times as you can—that is, divide by 12—and take the remainder that is less than 12 and project it from the **Ascendant** of the birth in the manner described before. For example: the **Ascendant** of a certain birth was at 13 degrees of **Virgo**, and 32 completed solar years have passed since the birth. I will subtract 12 from 32 years twice, and 8 completed years remain. I will therefore give the first year to **Virgo**, the second to **Libra**, the third to **Scorpio**, the fourth to **Sagittarius**, the fifth to **Capricorn**, the sixth to **Aquarius**, the seventh to **Pisces**, and the eighth to **Aries**. The **profection** will therefore come to the sign that follows immediately, namely to **Taurus**, at a degree identical to the degree of the **root**, which is the 13th degree of that sign. 

Just as I have described for the degree of the **Ascendant**, so it must be done for the degree of the **Sun**, the degree of the **Moon**, and the degree of the **Lot of Fortune**. Wherever the year lands, the lord of that sign is the "disposer" of that year. From that planet, one identifies the deeds the native will involve themselves in during that year, as well as the good and bad fortune that will happen to them. The author provides examples in the text; therefore, it is not necessary to dwell much on more examples. 

Next, the author says that when the **profection** of the year has reached a certain degree of a sign, and there is a planet (or the **rays** <note>The 'aspects' or mathematical angles cast by a planet.</note> of a planet) located after that degree in the same sign or in the same house in the chart, and you wish to know exactly when the **profection** will reach that planet or its rays: see how many degrees and minutes there are between the degree the year has reached and the planet or its rays. Multiply those by 12 and one-sixth. This is to say: you will give 12 days and one-sixth of a day <note>This equates to 12 days and 4 hours per degree, which is how long it takes for a profection to travel one degree of the zodiac.</note> to every degree. This will give you the number of days from the beginning of that year until the time...

<summary>This page defines the "Almuten" as the planet with the most cumulative power across five key points in a birth chart. It then introduces the technique of "Profections," explaining how to calculate which zodiac sign rules a specific year of a person's life by rotating the chart one sign per year.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of astrological "profections," moving from the calculation of individual lifespans to "profections of the world," which were used to predict the rise and fall of religions and empires.</meta>

...[the ti]me in which the **profection** <note>A timing technique where a point in the chart is moved forward by one sign per year.</note> will reach the planet or its rays. Moreover, the reason why 12 days and one-sixth of a day are given to each degree is this: because in this way, at the end of the year, one sign is completed. For if you multiply 30 degrees by 12 and a sixth of one, there will result 365 days, which are the days of one solar year. **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century foundational authority on astronomy and astrology.</note> says in proposition 236 of the fourth part of his *Quadripartitum* <note>The Latin title for Ptolemy's *Tetrabiblos*.</note>: and we shall know the disposition of the years' event by taking the number of the years of birth; and we shall cast them from any of the **hyleg** <term>hyleg: the "giver of life," a point in the birth chart that signifies life.</term> places according to the order of the signs, giving one year to each sign, and we shall take the ruler from the sign where the number of degrees falls. **Haly** <note>Likely Ali ibn Ridwan, a famous Arabic commentator on Ptolemy.</note> says the same in his commentary: that in this saying Ptolemy wants to explain that each sign governs for one year; and thus the revolution of the sign in which the **significator** <note>A planet or point representing a specific area of life, such as health or career.</note> is located will go around once every 12 years until the end of life; and all astrologers know this.

# Moreover, the Profection from the Years of the World.

<margin>Picatrix?</margin> <note>The marginal note "Pic dos" likely refers to the *Picatrix*, an influential medieval handbook of astrological magic.</note>
The work of profection in the years of the world follows. The meaning of the text is such: When you wish to know the application of the years of some "sect" <note>original: "secte". In medieval astrology, this refers to a religion or political-religious movement.</note>, see how many complete solar years have passed from the beginning of that year in which the **Great Conjunction** <term>Great Conjunction: The meeting of Jupiter and Saturn, believed by medieval scholars to signal the birth of new religions or empires.</term> occurred which signified that sect; and divide that number of years by 12, and the number that remains below 12, cast from the **Ascendant** of that year in which the conjunction occurred, and where that number lands, there is the sign of the profection. The author provides an example in the text regarding the sect of the Saracens <note>original: "sarracenoz", referring to the Muslims</note>, that is, of Muhammad; and he says that between the year of the conjunction that signified that law <note>meaning the Islamic religion</note> and the year of Muhammad's appearance—namely, the year in which he began to preach—there were 61 years. Another manuscript has 52 years. I believe it ought to be 52 years and 57 days; otherwise, it will not agree with what he says later: that the **Ascendant** of the year in which that conjunction occurred was the sign of **Gemini**, and that year in which he began to preach arrived at the sign of **Virgo**, namely, at the sign of that planet who was the lord of the ascending sign of the year of the conjunction <note>The planet Mercury rules both Gemini and Virgo, creating a symbolic link between the religion's origin and its manifestation.</note>. For if 12 is subtracted from 51 as many times as possible, 3 remains. Therefore, give the first to the sign of **Gemini**, the second to **Cancer**, the third to **Leo**; and thus the incomplete year that was placed in the count arrives at the sign of **Virgo**. It is of no great concern to us how many years there were from the year of that conjunction until the first year of the Arabs <note>The start of the Hijri calendar.</note>. For it is enough for us to know that the year in which the years of the Arabs began reached **Virgo**; because the years of the Arabs are known to us from their extraction tables, and we will be able to begin from **Virgo** according to what the author says in the text. And because in the author's time they worked in those parts by the years of the Persians <note>original: "phaz", referring to the solar Yazdegerdi calendar used in Persia</note>, he therefore applies all his work to the years of the Persians. And he says: when you wish to have knowledge of this matter—that is, to know to which sign the year applies from the year of the conjunction that signified the sect of the Saracens—see how many years of the Persians have passed and turn them into days by the extraction table serving the years of the Persians; or else let the years be multiplied by 365, for a **profectional year** contains that many days, because profectional years do not have the additional quarter of a day. And to the number of days that results for you, add 3,624 days; for there were that many days from the beginning of the years of the Arabs until the beginning of the years of the Persians. And divide the total collected by 365 and one quarter; and solar years will result; if there is any remainder, they will be the days of an incomplete year. And you can also reduce the number of days to solar years by the extraction tables serving the solar years. The whole intention of the author lies in this: that we should know how many complete solar years have passed from the beginning of the years of the Arabs until the time of consideration, and we give to each...

--- Page 138 ---
<meta>The text continues the technical instructions for "profections of the world," which apply the movement of one zodiac sign per year to historical and religious eras. The commentator, writing in the year 1330 AD, provides practical examples for calculating the astrological status of Islam and major political shifts.</meta>

...[each] year has one sign according to the manner previously described; and we shall begin from **Virgo**; and I shall set forth an example, applying it to our calculations according to the years of our Lord Jesus Christ. When you wish to know to which sign the year of the **conjunction** <term>conjunction: an alignment where two planets appear in the same spot in the sky, often signaling major historical changes.</term> that signified the law of the **Saracens** <note>original: "sarracenorum"; here referring to the religion of Islam.</note> applies, subtract 621 years, 6 months, and 14 days from the years of Christ. For so many years passed from the beginning of the years of Christ until the beginning of the years of the Arabs; and the number of completed years which remains, divide by 12. And that which remains, cast from Virgo, giving to each year one sign according to the manner previously described; and the incomplete year following the completed years which you divided remains in Virgo.

For example: the years of Christ are now 1330 completed, and 22 days. From those I shall subtract 621 years, 6 months, and 14 days. There remain 708 completed solar years, 6 months, and 8 days. These are the years that have passed from the beginning of the years of the Arabs until now. I shall divide those completed years by 12, and the whole number arises so that no completed year remains. I say, therefore, that the **profection** <note>The technique of advancing a point in a chart by one sign per year to determine the "ruler" of that year.</note> of the year of the great conjunction which signified the sect of the Saracens is now in Virgo, according to the opinion of the author. Just as it has been exemplified concerning the sect of the Saracens, so it can be done in other sects (if they ought to be called sects), if the conjunction signifying that sect and the **ascendant** <term>ascendant: the zodiac sign rising on the eastern horizon at a specific moment.</term> of the year of conjunction are known. But regarding this matter, it is not expedient to speak much. For it is a thing that does not agree with our faith <note>The commentator expresses a common medieval caution: using astrology to predict the "end" or "fate" of religions was often considered borderline heretical.</note>; but if anyone delights in these things and wishes to trace the changes that occur in laws [religions] back to the movements of the superior bodies, let him read the books *On the Great Conjunctions* <note>Likely referring to the work by the Persian astrologer Abu Ma'shar (787–886 AD), which was the standard text for this theory.</note> and there he shall find it.

Next, he says: if you want the profection from the sign of the conjunction which signified the aforesaid sect, cast the aforesaid years from **Scorpio**; because that conjunction, according to him, was in Scorpio. If, however, you want the profection from the ascendant of a kingdom, see how many solar years have passed from the beginning of that kingdom. He gives an example of one kingdom, but I do not know of which kingdom he speaks, and I do not care, because the text can be understood without his example. Moreover, just as it is said concerning the ascendant of a kingdom, so it should be understood concerning the ascendant of the foundation of any city, or castle, or even any building whatsoever; so that the completed solar years are taken which have passed from the beginning of its foundation, and they are cast from the ascendant of the start of the foundation according to the manner said before.

Then he says: if you want the profection from the ascendant of the shift of the conjunction of **Jupiter** <note>original: "Iouis"</note> and **Saturn** from the **water triplicity** <term>triplicity: a group of three zodiac signs belonging to the same element (fire, earth, air, or water).</term> to the **fire triplicity**, subtract 176 completed years from the years of the Persians, and convert those which remain into solar years and cast from **Leo**. He means to say that the conjunction of Jupiter and Saturn at the beginning of the years of the Persians was in the water triplicity, and 176 years after the beginning of the Persian years (by which they calculated), the conjunction shifted to the fire triplicity. Therefore, he orders to subtract 176 years from the years of the Persians, and there remain the years which have passed from the year of the shift of the conjunction to the fire triplicity; and he orders to turn those years that remain into solar years, because [the Persian years] do not correctly have the leap year, and solar years do; for profection is done according to solar years. And he orders to cast from Leo, because according to him, Leo was the ascendant in the year of the shift of the conjunction to the fire triplicity.

From which it appears that this author lived and composed this book in the time when the conjunction was in the fire triplicity. Now, however, this conjunction is in the **air triplicity**. Therefore, I shall set forth an example concerning the triplicity in which we now are. When you wish to know the profection from the year of the shift of the conjunction of Jupiter and Saturn to the air triplicity, subtract 1324 years, 7 days, and 20 hours from the years of Christ; for so much time passed from the beginning of the years of Christ until the beginning of the years [of the air triplicity shift].

<meta>This page continues the specific calculation for the "profection of the world," transitioning from the historical calculation of Islamic influence into the general rules for "Direction," a primary technique used by medieval astrologers to predict when events will occur.</meta>

...of the change of the conjunction to the triplicity in which we now are: and the remainder cast from the 13th degree of **Taurus**. For that was the ascendant of the aforementioned year: and wherever the number ends, there will be the **profection** <note>The technique of advancing a point in a chart by one sign per year to determine the "ruler" of that year.</note> of the year. For example: the years of Christ are 1330 completed and 22 days. From those I shall subtract 1324 years and 71 days. And there remained 5 completed years and 316 days of the incomplete year. I shall give the first year to **Taurus**, beginning from its 13th degree; the second to the sign of **Gemini**, beginning from its 13th degree; the third to **Cancer**; the fourth to **Leo**, beginning from its 13th degree; the fifth to **Virgo**, beginning from its 13th degree. Therefore, the profection of the 6th year of the world completed comes to **Libra** at the 13th degree. 

And if you wish [to find] the degree in which the profection of the last day is—from the days which passed in the incomplete year—multiply the number of days of the completed year <note>In this context, this refers to the days elapsed so far in the current, unfinished year.</note> by 30 and divide the product by 365. The quotient will be the degrees. If anything remains after the division, multiply it by 60 and divide by the same as before, and the quotient will be the minutes. For example: in the proposed example, there remained 316 days of the incomplete year, which I shall multiply by 30, and 9480 will result. I shall divide this number by 365, and it will give 25 degrees, and 355 remains after division. I shall multiply these by 60, and 21,300 results. I shall divide by 365, and 58 minutes will result. Therefore, I shall add 25 degrees and 58 minutes to the 13th degree of Libra, and the number will reach the 8th degree and 58 minutes of **Scorpio**. There, then, is the profection from the **ascendant** <term>ascendant: the degree of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon at a specific moment.</term> of the year of the change of the conjunction to the air triplicity.

In this chapter the author teaches how to **direct** <term>direct: to calculate the timing of events by moving a planet or point through the sky until it meets another.</term> whichever significator
</margin>

# From here, Direction.
<image-desc>Decorative drop cap initial "E" featuring floral and vine motifs within a square frame with a black background.</image-desc>
It must be understood first that **direction** (or "to direct") a **significator** <term>significator: a planet or point in a chart that signifies a specific topic, such as the person's life, wealth, or health.</term> to any place in the circle is nothing other than finding the degrees of the **equinoctial** <note>original: "equinoctialis"; the celestial equator.</note> which revolve from the place of the significator to the place to which it should be directed. If the place of the significator is in the **midheaven** <note>original: "medio celi"; the highest point in the sky at the time of a chart.</note> or in the "angle of the earth" <note>original: "angulo terre"; the lowest point in the chart, the northern angle.</note>, then to direct is to find the degrees of the equinoctial which revolve from the place of the significator to the place where it must be directed on the **right horizon** <note>The horizon at the Earth's equator; used here as a mathematical reference point for calculating "right ascensions."</note>. But if it is in the ascendant or in the descendant, then it is finding those degrees on the **oblique horizon** <note>The horizon relative to the observer’s specific latitude.</note>. If, however, it is not in any of these places, then the **ascensions** <term>ascensions: the rising times of zodiac signs, which vary based on geographic location and the angle of the ecliptic.</term> are taken mixedly—namely, by mixing the ascensions of the right circle with the ascensions of the region, proportionally according to the distance of the significator's place from the angles. 

And the degrees of direction sometimes signify years, sometimes days, and sometimes other times. In **nativities** <note>Personal birth charts.</note>, each degree signifies a year. In the revolutions of the years of the world and of nativities, each degree signifies a day. And some wish that in revolutions of years, 59 minutes and 8 seconds (namely, the movement of the sun in one day) should signify one day. In **conjunctions** of the sun and moon and in **oppositions**, according to some, each 13 degrees and 10 minutes (namely, as much as the moon moves in a day by its mean motion) signifies a day. 

Therefore, when you wish to direct some significator to any place in the circle: if that significator is in the degree of the midheaven or in the angle of the earth, subtract the ascensions of the right circle of the significator's degree from the ascensions of the right circle of the place to which you wish to direct it, and the degrees of direction remain. If indeed the significator is in the ascendant or descendant, subtract the ascensions of the oblique circle of the significator's degree from the ascensions of the oblique circle...

toward which you wish to direct it, and the **degrees of direction** <note>The total distance a planet must "travel" in a symbolic calculation to reach a specific point, representing a span of time in the person's life.</note> remain.

An example when it is in the **midheaven** <note>The highest point on the ecliptic at a specific moment; the 10th house.</note>. I suppose that the **Moon** is in the midheaven in the 5th degree of **Aquarius**, and I suppose that the **opposite rays of Saturn** <note>The point exactly 180 degrees away from Saturn, known as an opposition.</note> are in the 6th degree of **Pisces**. I suppose that I wish to direct the **Moon** to the opposite rays of **Saturn**. I sought, therefore, the ascensions of the degree of the **Moon** in the **right circle** <note>original: "circulo directo." This refers to Right Ascension, the measurement along the celestial equator.</note> and found 37 degrees and 23 minutes. I also sought the ascensions of the 6th degree of **Pisces**, where the opposite rays of **Saturn** are located, in the same right circle, and found 67 degrees and 48 minutes. Then I subtracted the ascensions of the **Moon** from the ascensions of the rays of **Saturn**, and there remained 30 degrees and 25 minutes. Those would be the **degrees of direction** in this case.

An example when it is in the **ascendant** <note>The degree of the zodiac rising over the eastern horizon.</note>. I suppose that the **Moon** is in that same 5th degree of **Aquarius** and the rays of **Saturn** in the 6th of **Pisces**. I sought the ascensions of the degree of the **Moon** in the **oblique circle** <note>original: "circulo obliquo." This refers to Oblique Ascension, which accounts for the tilt of the horizon at a specific geographic latitude.</note> in a region where the latitude is 48 degrees, and I found 330 degrees and 22 minutes. I also sought the ascensions of the 6th degree of **Pisces** and found 348 degrees and 31 minutes. I subtracted the ascensions of the **Moon** from the ascensions of the degree of the rays of **Saturn**, and there remained 18 degrees and 9 minutes. Those would be the **degrees of direction** in this case. In this same way, the degree of the ascendant is directed toward any place in the circle.

If, however, the **significator** <note>The planet or point that represents the subject being studied.</note> to be directed should be outside these locations—that is, if it should be between the **midheaven** and the **ascendant**—see its distance from the midheaven by subtracting the ascensions of the degree of the mid-circle <note>The Midheaven's Right Ascension.</note> in the right circle from the ascensions of the degree of the **significator**, and save that distance. Then, find half of the **diurnal arc** <note>term: "arcus diurni" — the amount of time/degrees a planet stays above the horizon from rising to setting.</note> of the degree of the **significator** by the method described in the chapter on radiations, and save it. 

Then, subtract the right circle ascensions of the degree of the **significator** from the right circle ascensions of the place toward which you wish to direct it, and what remains will be the **significator of the right circle**; save it. Next, subtract the ascensions of the degree of the **significator** in the **oblique circle** from the ascensions of the place toward which you wish to direct it in that same oblique circle, and what remains is the **significator of the region**. 

Then, subtract the smaller significator from the larger, and multiply the **difference** <note>original: "dñiam" (differentiam).</note> by the distance from the angle of the **midheaven**, and divide the product by the half of the **diurnal arc**. The **proportional part** will result; add this to the significator of the right circle if it was smaller than the significator of the region, or subtract it if it was larger, and the result will be the **degree of direction**. 

If you should wish to direct against the succession of the signs <note>Against the natural order of Aries, Taurus, Gemini, etc.</note>, as parts and retrograde planets are directed—that is, by proceeding from the **ascendant** toward the **midheaven**—then you must take the distance of the significator from the ascendant by the ascensions of the region, and you must add the proportional part of the difference of the two significators to the significator of the region if that is smaller than the significator of the right circle, or subtract it from it if it is larger. In other respects, the work does not differ. 

If, however, the **significator** to be directed should be between the **ascendant** and the **angle of the earth** <note>term: "anguluz terre" — the Imum Coeli or the lowest point of the chart, the 4th house cusp.</note>, see the distance of the degree of the significator from the ascendant by subtracting the ascensions of the degree of the ascendant of the region from the ascensions of the degree of the significator of the region, and save the distance. Then, find half of the **nocturnal arc** <note>term: "arcus nocturni" — the path of a planet below the horizon, from setting to rising.</note> of the degree of the significator by the method described in the work on radiations, which also you should save. Then subtract the ascensions of the degree of the significator in the **oblique circle** from the ascensions of the place toward which you wish to direct it in that same oblique circle, and what remains is the **significator of the region**, which you should save. Then subtract the ascensions of the degree of the significator of the right circle from the ascensions of the right circle of the place toward which you wish to direct it, and what remains will be the **significator of the right circle**. Then subtract the smaller from the larger, and the **difference** of the two significators will remain, which multiply...

by the distance of the degree of the **significator** <note>A planet or point in a birth chart that represents a specific person or topic, such as the Moon for the soul or the Sun for vitality.</note> from the **ascendant** <note>The degree of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon at the moment of birth.</note> and divide the product by half of the **nocturnal arc** <note>The total time/distance a planet spends below the horizon during the night.</note> of the degree of the significator, and the **proportional part** will result. Add this to the significator of the region if it is less than the significator of the **right circle** <note>original: "circuli directi." This refers to measurements on the celestial equator, or Right Ascension.</note>; or subtract it from the significator of the region if it is greater than the significator of the right circle. Whatever remains after addition or subtraction are the **degrees of direction**. 

However, if you wish to direct by the **succession of the signs** <note>Moving through the zodiac in the standard order (Aries, Taurus, Gemini, etc.).</note>—namely, by proceeding from the **angle of the earth** <note>The *Imum Coeli* or the lowest point of the chart, representing the 4th house and one's roots or the end of life.</note> toward the **ascendant**—then you must take the distance of the significator from the **angle of the earth** by the ascensions of the **right circle**, multiply that by the difference of the two significators, and divide by half of the **diurnal arc** <note>The total time/distance a planet spends above the horizon during the day.</note> as was said before. You must then add the **proportional part** to the significator of the **right circle** if it is less than the significator of the region, or subtract it if it is greater; in other respects, the work does not differ. 

If, however, the significator to be directed is between the **angle of the earth** and the western degree <note>The *Descendant*, or the 7th house cusp where planets set.</note>, you must work with the **opposite degree** of the significator and with the opposite degree of the place to which you wish to direct it, and by the distance of the opposite degree of the significator from the **midheaven**. Use half the **diurnal arc** of the opposite degree of the significator, for the opposite degree of the significator is as far from the **meridian line** <note>The vertical line connecting the Midheaven and the Angle of the Earth.</note> as the degree of the significator is from the **angle of the earth**; and the half of the **diurnal arc** of the opposite degree is as great as the half of the **nocturnal arc** of the significator. The operation is then the same as it was between the **midheaven** and the **ascendant**.

If, indeed, the significator to be directed is between the western angle and the **midheaven**, you must work similarly by the opposite degree of the significator, and by its distance from the **ascendant**, and by half the **nocturnal arc** of that same opposite degree, and by the opposite degree of the place of the significator to which it is to be directed; and its operation is just as it is done between the **ascendant** and the **angle of the earth**.

But if it happens that the significator to be directed is in one **quadrant** <note>original: "quadra." One of the four sections of the chart divided by the Ascendant, Midheaven, Descendant, and Imum Coeli.</note> and the place to which it must be directed is in another **quadrant**—for example: if the significator is between the **midheaven** and the **ascendant**, and the place to which it must be directed is between the **ascendant** and the **angle of the earth**—then you must first direct it up to the **angle**, and afterwards direct the **angle** up to the place to which the significator must be directed, and join these directions together. 

You shall direct the aforementioned significator in this way: take the distance of the significator from the angle that is behind it in the order of the signs. For instance, if it is between the angle of the **midheaven** and the **ascendant**, you shall take its distance from the angle of the **midheaven**. And if it is between the **ascendant** and the **angle of the earth**, you shall take its distance from the **ascendant**. You shall also take half of the **diurnal** or **nocturnal arc** of the degree of the significator or its opposite in the manner previously described; and you shall take the angle to which you wish to direct it as the place to which it must be directed, and follow the order previously described. 

Once that direction is made, you shall direct the **angle** in the manner previously described when the significator is in one of the angles, continuing to the place to which the significator must be directed. Then add one direction to the other, and the **degrees of direction** will result.

The **author** <note>Likely referring to a source text the commentator is explaining, possibly Ptolemy or Alcabitius.</note> says in the text that the distance of the significator from the angle should be taken and divided by the **parts of the hours** <note>A division of the day into 12 equal parts (planetary hours), which vary in length depending on the season.</note> of the degree of the significator, which results in the "hours of distance" of the significator from the angle. He says one should take a **sixth part** of the difference of the two significators and multiply that sixth part by the hours of the significator’s distance from the **meridian**; the result should be added to or subtracted from the significator of the **right circle** in the manner described. But the method I have set down is easier and clearer. For in the author’s method, one must take the distance from the angle by the same method I have used, and beyond this, one must seek the **parts of the hours** of the degree of the significator, and...

...then divide the distance from the angle by the hour-parts <note>original: "partes horarum"; a measurement of time converted into degrees of the equator</note> of the degree of the **significator** <note>The planet or point in a birth chart representing the person or a specific topic of their life.</note> and keep what results. Beyond this, it is necessary to divide that difference by six so that one may have a sixth part of it. Since error easily occurs in a multitude of operations, the method I have set down seems more certain to me.

**Example**

When the significator is between the **midheaven** <note>original: "medium celi"; the highest point of the sky in the chart</note> and the **ascendant** <note>the degree rising on the eastern horizon</note>, and the place to which it ought to be directed is in the same quadrant—namely, between the midheaven and the ascendant. I suppose that the **Moon** is the **hyleg** <note>original: "hylech"; the "giver of life," a point used to calculate longevity</note> and that it is in the 5th degree of Aquarius. I also suppose that the opposite rays of **Saturn**—and it would be the same regarding its physical body—are in the 6th degree of Pisces; and I suppose that the angle of the midheaven is the 20th degree of Capricorn. 

To direct the Moon to the rays of Saturn, I first sought the ascensions of the degree of the midheaven in the right circle and found 291 degrees and 39 minutes. <note>The OCR reads "2i," a common early printing abbreviation or error for 291.</note> I subtracted the ascensions of the degree of the midheaven from the ascensions of the degree of the Moon, and there remained the distance of the Moon from the midheaven: 15 degrees and 44 minutes. Then I sought the ascensions of the degree of the Moon in the **oblique circle** <note>the horizon for that specific latitude</note> and found 330 degrees and 22 minutes. I also sought the ascensions of the degree opposite the place of the Moon, which were 194 degrees and 30 minutes, <note>OCR reads "104," but the calculation requires 194.</note> from which I subtracted the ascensions of the degree of the Moon. But because I could not subtract since the ascensions of the degree of the Moon were greater than the ascensions of the opposite degree, I added 360 degrees to the ascensions of the opposite degree of the Moon, resulting in 554 degrees and 30 minutes. Then I subtracted, and there remained 134 degrees and 8 minutes. This was the total **diurnal arc** <note>the distance a planet travels above the horizon from rising to setting</note> of the degree of the Moon, which I halved; the half was 67 degrees and 4 minutes. 

I thus kept the distance of the Moon from the midheaven and half of the diurnal arc of the Moon. Then I subtracted the ascensions of the degree of the Moon in the right circle (which were 337 degrees and 23 minutes) from the ascensions of the degree of the rays of Saturn in the same circle (which were 8 degrees and 48 minutes) <note>Adjusting for the 360-degree circle</note> and there remained 31 degrees and 25 minutes; this was the significator of the right circle, and I kept it. Then I subtracted the ascensions of the degree of the Moon in the oblique circle (which were 330 degrees and 22 minutes) from the ascensions of the rays of Saturn in the same oblique circle (which were 348 degrees and 31 minutes), and there remained the significator of the region, which was 18 degrees and 9 minutes. Then I subtracted the significator of the region from the significator of the right circle, and there remained the difference of the two significators, which was 12 degrees and 16 minutes. 

Then I multiplied that difference by the distance of the Moon from the midheaven and divided by half of the diurnal arc of the degree of the Moon, and the proportional part resulted, which was 2 degrees, 52 minutes, and 38 seconds. I also subtracted this proportional part from the significator of the right circle—because it was greater than the significator of the region—and there remained the **degrees of direction**, which were 27 degrees, 32 minutes, and 21 seconds. 

I took one year for every degree, and one month for every 5 minutes remaining, and 6 days for every minute, and 6 minutes of a day for every second. When this time is completed from the time of the **nativity** <note>the moment of birth</note> whose chart was arranged in this way, the person born will die. 

When, however, the significator is between the ascendant and the **angle of the earth** <note>the Imum Coeli, the lowest point of the chart</note>, you will work with the distance from the ascendant and with half of the **nocturnal arc** <note>the distance a planet travels below the horizon</note>; it does not differ in any other way. When you wish to direct **against the succession of the signs** <note>moving backward through the zodiac, e.g., from Aquarius to Capricorn</note>, if the significator is between the midheaven and the ascendant, you will work with the distance from the ascendant and with half of the diurnal arc. <meta>This page continues the technical instructions for "Primary Directions," specifically focusing on how to handle the "angle of the earth" (the Bottom of the Heaven) and introducing the complex problem of celestial latitude—when a planet is slightly above or below the sun's path.</meta>

...the **angle of the earth** <note>original: "angulum terre"; the lowest point of the chart (Imum Coeli), representing the northern midnight point</note> and the degree of the west <note>the Descendant</note>, you will work with the opposite degree of the **significator** and the opposite degree of the place to which you wish to direct; and then your work is between the **midheaven** <note>original: "medium celi"; the highest point of the sky</note> and the **ascendant**. And if the significator is between the degree of the west and the midheaven, you will work similarly with the opposite degrees; and then your work is between the ascendant and the angle of the earth.

¶ Note, however, that according to the method described, significators are directed when they have no **latitude** <note>original: "latitudo"; the distance of a planet north or south of the ecliptic (the sun's path)</note>. However, when significators possess latitude, the method is more difficult; for this reason, an instrument was made for directing planets that have latitude, and that instrument is called a **directorium** <note>a "director"; a specialized physical tool or variant of an astrolabe used for these calculations</note>. It seemed to the composer or inventor of that instrument that a significator having latitude could not be directed through **tables of ascensions** <note>mathematical tables showing how many degrees of the equator rise with each degree of the zodiac</note>, or at least not without great difficulty. 

But I say that his instrument is not sufficient, unless it is of the greatest size so that it can receive minutes <note>fractions of a degree</note>; however, such a thing can hardly be made. For in directions, one year is usually taken for every one degree. Now, if in directions the instrument is not divided except by degrees, accuracy will hardly be found in it to within a year—and this is only if the instrument is well-made so that there is no error in the dividing; however, even this is not enough. 

And I tell you that a significator having latitude can be directed to a place having latitude by the tables of ascensions; and I have not seen the opposite method in any book. The method, however, is this: when you wish to direct a significator having latitude from the **ecliptic** <note>the apparent path of the sun</note> to the body of any planet that also has latitude, or to the rays of any point having latitude: 

First, seek the degree with which the significator **culminates at the meridian** <note>original: "mediat celum"; literally "mediates the heaven," the point where the planet reaches its highest daily point</note> according to the teaching of the rules of the **Primum Mobile** <note>the "First Mover," the outermost celestial sphere used here to refer to the primary rotation of the heavens</note>. Next, seek the degree with which the place to which you wish to direct culminates at the meridian according to the same teaching. You shall take the degree with which the significator culminates for the position of the significator; by this, you will know the distance of the significator from the midheaven or from the ascendant by the method previously stated, neither more nor less. 

And you shall take the place with which the target location culminates as the position to which it ought to be directed; and with these two, you will seek the significator of the **right circle** <note>original: "circulus directus"; referring to Right Ascension, or the measurement along the celestial equator</note> by the method previously stated. Then, seek the degree with which the significator rises in its region according to the teaching of the rules of the Primum Mobile; seek also the degree with which the place to which it ought to be directed rises according to those same rules. Then, with the degree with which the significator rises, you will seek the half of the **diurnal** <note>daytime</note> or **nocturnal** <note>nighttime</note> **arc** <note>the path the planet takes above or below the horizon</note> by the method previously stated; and you will seek the significator of the region with these two degrees: namely, with the degree with which the significator rises and with the degree with which the target place rises. In nothing else does the work differ. 

I tell you also, and briefly, that it is necessary to seek the degree in which the significator culminates and the degree with which the target place culminates. With the first two, it is necessary to seek the significator of the right circle. With the other two, it is necessary to seek the significator of the region. With the degree with which the significator culminates, it is necessary to seek the distance from the midheaven if it is between the midheaven and the ascendant; with the degree with which the significator rises, it is necessary to seek the half of the diurnal or nocturnal arc. If, however, the significator is between the ascendant and the angle of the earth, it is necessary to seek the distance of the significator from the ascendant with the degree with which the significator rises, and with the same, the half of the nocturnal arc; in all other things, the work is the same as the work previously described. There is also one other case that can happen in directions when the significator and the place to...

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of primary directions, focusing on the complex scenarios where planets have celestial latitude—meaning they are not positioned exactly on the sun's path (the ecliptic). It explains how to calculate the timing of life events when points reach the meridian or the horizon simultaneously.</meta>

...which must be directed have **latitude** <note>original: "latitudine"; the distance a planet sits north or south of the sun's path</note>: or one of them possesses latitude. For it can happen that the **significator** <note>the planet or point representing a specific life topic</note> to be directed and the place to which it must be directed reach the **meridian line** <note>the imaginary north-south line through the highest point of the sky</note> at the same time: so that both **culminate** <note>original: "mediant celum"; reach the midheaven</note> with the same degree, even though they are not in the same degree on the **ecliptic** <note>the sun's path through the zodiac</note>. If any of these cases occur: then you shall work in the same way I will describe. 

If the significator and the place to which it must be directed reach the **midheaven** <note>the highest point of the sky</note> with the same degree, because there are no **ascensions** <note>mathematical units of time/arc</note> in the **right circle** <note>the celestial equator</note> between them, you shall set the "right circle significator" as zero. Afterward, you will seek the "regional significator" by taking the ascensions in the **oblique circle** <note>the local horizon</note> from the place with which the significator rises until the place with which the target point rises: and all of that (namely, the regional significator) you will multiply by the distance of the significator from the **angle** <note>a cardinal point like the Ascendant or Midheaven</note> and divide the product by half of the **diurnal or nocturnal arc** <note>the total time the point is above or below the horizon</note>: and the **proportional part** will result, which you will keep as the degrees of the direction. 

If, however, the significator to be directed and the target point **rise** with one and the same degree, you shall set the "regional significator" as zero, and multiply the "right circle significator" by the distance of the significator from the angle: and divide by half of the diurnal or nocturnal arc: and the proportional part will result, which you will keep as the degrees of the direction. 

To provide examples for all these cases would be too long: but I intend, the Lord granting, at the end of this exposition to provide the **calculation of one nativity** <note>original: "equatiōez vnius natiuitatis"; a full birth chart analysis</note> in which the method of investigating the **ascendant** and choosing the **Hylech** <note>the "giver of life" point in a chart</note> and the **Alchochoden** <note>the planet that determines the number of years a person might live</note> will be clear: and I will place there some **projections of rays** <note>aspects or planetary influences</note> and some directions: let these words suffice concerning the work of directions. 

The author says in the text: having known the years of the direction, if you wish to know the location of this direction in the **zodiac**, add the years of the direction to the ascensions in the right circle of the degree of the significator (if the significator was in the midheaven or in the **angle of the earth** <note>the bottom of the sky</note>) and reduce it to equal degrees in the right circle: and where that number leads you in the circle of the signs: there is the **place of direction**: and it is called the **place of division** <note>original: "locus diuisionis"</note> and the **lord of the term** <note>the planet ruling that specific 5–7 degree subdivision of a zodiac sign</note> of that place is called the **divisor** <note>original: "diuiſoꝛ"; the planet "dividing" or governing that period of life</note>. 

If the significator was in the ascendant, add the years of the direction to the **regional ascensions** of the degree of the significator: and reduce to equal degrees in the oblique circle: and where the number leads you in the circle of the signs, there is the place of division: and the lord of the term of that place is the divisor. And this is the same work as the work concerning the projections of rays: and when you have performed the direction and performed this work, you will find in the end the same place to which you directed: unless the significator had latitude.

## ¶ From this, the distribution of life in nativities.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'E' featuring a seated scholar figure within a structured frame.</image-desc>From this chapter, the author teaches how to direct the degree of the **ascendant** in **nativities** <note>birth charts</note>. The meaning of the text is as follows: Consider in which planet’s **term** <note>original: "termino"; a specific subdivision of a zodiac sign</note> the ascending degree sits and consider the end of that term. 

Then seek the ascensions of the ascending degree in the **oblique circle** <note>the horizon</note> through the table of your region and save them: seek also the ascensions of the end of that term through the same table, from which you should subtract the ascensions of the ascending degree; and the degrees that remain are the **degrees of direction**. Take one year for every degree, and one month for every five minutes: and for every single minute, 6 days, and for every second, two hours and 24 minutes of an hour: and the planet which is the lord of the term is the **dispositor** of the years, or the **divisor**. 

Afterward, consider the end of the term immediately following, and subtract the ascensions of the end of the preceding term from the ascensions of the end of that term: and the degrees of direction will remain; take them thus, a year for every degree...

...take number one and stop. And the lord of that **term** <term>term: an unequal subdivision of a zodiac sign ruled by a specific planet; different systems of terms existed, such as the "Egyptian" or "Ptolemaic" terms</term> is the **dispositor** <term>dispositor: the planet that "rules" or manages a specific degree or section of the zodiac</term> or the divider; and in this way, you shall proceed to the end of any sign following the order of the signs until the end of life. 

And if there is any planet, or the rays <note>original: "radij"; referring to astrological aspects or light sent from other planets</note> of any planet, in any term, that planet will be a partner in the division. I set an example of this here: suppose the **ascendant** <note>the degree of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon at the moment of birth</note> of a certain birth was the 4th completed degree of **Virgo**. To **direct** <note>original: "dirigendum"; a predictive technique that moves the chart forward in time, often equating one degree to one year of life</note> this degree, consider first in which planet's term this degree is located. And it is first clear that it is in the term of **Mercury**, and the term lasts until the end of the 7th degree of Virgo. I sought the **ascensions** <note>mathematical units used to convert zodiacal degrees into time</note> of the ascending degree on the **oblique horizon** <note>the horizon as viewed from a specific latitude</note> in a region where the latitude is 48 degrees, and I found 144 degrees and 27 minutes. I also sought the ascensions of the end of the term—namely, the end of the 7th degree of Virgo—and I found 148 degrees and 33 minutes. I subtracted the ascensions of the ascending degree from the ascensions of the end of the term, and 4 degrees and 6 minutes remained. I gave one year to each degree, and one month to every 5 minutes, and 6 days to each minute; the result was four years, one month, and 26 days. And Mercury, the lord of the term, was the dispositor of these years.

Then I considered the term immediately following, which is that of **Venus**, and it lasts until the end of the 17th degree of Virgo. I sought the ascensions of the end of that term—namely, of Venus—and I found 162 degrees and 15 minutes. From these, I subtracted the ascensions of the end of the preceding term of Mercury, and 13 degrees and 44 minutes remained, which made 13 years, 8 months, and 12 days. And Venus disposed these years. At the end of this term, the age of the native was 17 years, 9 months, and 18 days. Then follows the term of **Jupiter**, which lasts until the 21st completed degree of Virgo. I sought its ascensions and found 167 degrees and 43 minutes <note>original: "93.minuta"; likely a scribal or OCR error for 43, as 93 minutes exceeds the standard 60-minute degree, and 43 fits the subsequent calculation</note>. From these, I subtracted the ascensions of the end of the preceding term, and there remained five degrees and 28 minutes, which made five years, five months, and 18 days; and Jupiter was the dispositor of these years. The age of the native at the end of these years was 23 years, three months, and six days. Then follows the term of **Mars**, and it must be done as before. Following this order, the direction is to be continued until the end of life. And as often as the direction reaches the body or the rays of a **malefic** <note>original: "infortune"; specifically the planets Mars or Saturn, associated with hardship</note>, sickness or an impediment in the body will come to the native according to the nature of that malefic, unless **benefics** <note>original: "fortune"; specifically Jupiter or Venus, associated with luck and protection</note> look upon <note>original: "aspexerint"; form an astrological aspect</note> that same place; for then they will diminish the evil.

# Regarding the twelfth-parts of the planets and
# the houses.

In this chapter, the author teaches how to find the lord of the **twelfth-part** <term>twelfth-part: also known as a dodecatemoria; a subdivision of a single 30-degree zodiac sign into twelve equal parts of 2.5 degrees each</term>. It is to be understood for the clarity of this chapter that every sign is understood to be divided into twelve parts, and each part consists of two degrees and 30 minutes. **John of Seville** <note>original: "Hyspalensis"; a prolific 12th-century translator of Arabic astrological texts into Latin</note> places this chapter and the two following chapters among the "dignities" <note>the various ways a planet gains strength or influence based on its position</note> of the planets. The lord of the first twelfth-part is the lord of that sign. The lord of the second twelfth-part is the planet following it in descending order, and so on according to the order. 

For example: the lord of the first twelfth-part of **Aries** is **Mars**. The second is the **Sun**. The third is **Venus**. The fourth is **Mercury**. The fifth is the **Moon**. The sixth is **Saturn**. The seventh is **Jupiter**. The eighth is **Mars** again; and so on according to the order <note>This follows the "Chaldean order" of the planets: Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, Sun, Venus, Mercury, Moon</note>. The author’s opinion is as follows: when you wish to know the twelfth-parts of the planets or the houses, see how much the planet has traveled through the sign or house in which it is, and multiply that number of degrees by twelve; to the resulting number, add the number of degrees you multiplied. And discard what is collected starting from the beginning of the sign...

# And from this, the ninth part [anaubarach].

<margin>Here he teaches how to find the lord of the **ninth part** <term>ninth part: known in Latin as 'novenaria' and in Arabic-influenced texts as 'anaubarach'; it is a 3-degree and 20-minute division of a sign</term>. For the meaning is such:</margin>
When you wish to know the ninth part, or the lord of the ninth part of any planet: see how many degrees the planet has traveled through the sign in which it is, or even the **house** <note>In astrology, 'house' can refer to a division of the local sky or a sign's role in a chart</note> in which it is. And divide that whole sign into 9 divisions; and each division will consist of 3 degrees and 20 minutes <note>The Latin text says "7.20 minutes" in several places, which appears to be a scribal error or a specific notation for 20 minutes (1/3 of a degree), as the subsequent math only works if each part is exactly 3 degrees and 20 minutes</note>. Then see in which part of that division the planet is located. You shall give the first division to the lord of the **movable sign** <term>movable sign: also called 'cardinal' signs (Aries, Cancer, Libra, or Capricorn), these signs mark the start of the seasons</term> of that same **triplicity** <term>triplicity: a group of three zodiac signs belonging to the same element (Fire, Earth, Air, or Water)</term>. And you give the second division to the lord of the following sign, and so on, following the order of the signs until you come to that ninth part in which the planet is located, whose lord you are seeking. 

For example: I suppose that the **Sun** is at 24 degrees of **Aquarius**. To find the lord of the ninth part of the Sun's degree, I first gave 3 degrees and 20 minutes of Aquarius to the lord of the movable sign of the same triplicity. Now, Aquarius belongs to the **Air triplicity**. The movable sign of that triplicity is **Libra**; its lord is **Venus**. Therefore, Venus is the lady of the first ninth part of Aquarius—that is, the first 3 degrees and 20 minutes. Then I gave the second ninth part to **Mars**, the lord of the next sign, which is **Scorpio**; and at that point, 6 degrees and 40 minutes from the start of Aquarius were completed. Then I gave the third ninth part to **Jupiter**, the lord of **Sagittarius**, which follows Scorpio; and 10 degrees from the start of Aquarius were completed. I gave the fourth ninth part to **Saturn**, the lord of **Capricorn**, which follows Sagittarius; and 13 degrees and 20 minutes from the start of the sign were completed. I gave the fifth ninth part to Saturn, the lord of Aquarius; and 16 degrees and 40 minutes were completed. I gave the sixth ninth part to Jupiter, the lord of **Pisces**, and 20 degrees from the start of the sign of Aquarius were completed. I gave the seventh ninth part to Mars, the lord of **Aries**; and 23 degrees and 20 minutes from the start of the sign were completed. I gave the eighth ninth part to Venus, the lord of **Taurus**. That, therefore, is the division of the ninth part of the Sun in the proposed case; and at that point, 26 degrees and 40 minutes of Aquarius were completed. Therefore, when you find in the books of judgments mention of the "lord of the **anaubarach**" <note>original: "anaubarach"; a transliteration of the Arabic 'an-nawbahr'</note> of the **luminaries** <note>the Sun and the Moon</note>, if it is by day, it refers to the lord of the ninth part of the Sun; if by night, to the lord of the ninth part of the Moon's degree. Other things the author says will be clear by reading the text.

# And from this, the decan [adoringen].

<margin>Here he teaches how to find the **decan** <term>decan: also called a 'face,' this is a 10-degree division of a zodiac sign (30 ÷ 3 = 10)</term>. His meaning is such: Divide the</margin>
**ascending sign** into three parts; and each division will consist of ten degrees. You shall give the first division to the lord of the **ascendant** <note>the sign rising on the eastern horizon</note>, the second to the lord of the second sign [of that triplicity], and the third to the lord of the third sign. He means to say that the first division belongs to the lord of that same sign which is ascending. And the second division belongs to the lord of the following sign of the same triplicity. The third division belongs to the lord of the third sign of that triplicity, so that the three decans are the lords of the three signs of one triplicity. The author provides such an example in the text: Supposing that the first **face** <note>another term for decan</note> of Aries is ascending, its decan-lord is Mars. If the second face is ascending, its decan-lord is the Sun, the lord of **Leo**, which is the second sign of the same triplicity; and if the third face is ascending, its decan-lord is Jupiter, the lord of Sagittarius, which is the third sign of that triplicity. And if the first face of Leo is ascending, its decan-lord is the Sun, the lord of that same sign. If the second face is ascending, its decan-lord is Jupiter, the lord of the sign which follo<unclear>ws</unclear>...

## And from this, the Lord of the Hour.

<margin>In this chapter, he sets out the order of the "lord of the hours" in **nativities** <term>nativity: a birth chart or the moment of a person's birth</term>. The meaning of the text is as follows:</margin>
Look at which hour the newborn leaves the womb and find the lord of that hour. Give that planet the first year of the child's life; through it, the condition of the person in that year is signified, specifically regarding the health or sickness of the body, just as one does with the lord of the **ascendant** <note>The planet ruling the sign rising on the eastern horizon at birth</note>. Then, you shall give the lord of the following hour to the second year. Through that planet, the state of the person's substance <note>wealth and possessions</note> in that year is signified, just as it is by the lord of the **second house** <note>The "house of substance" in astrology</note>. You shall give the lord of the third hour to the third year from the hour of birth, and through it, the state of brothers in that year is signified, as by the lord of the third house. In this way, you shall proceed with the other lords of the hours, following the order of the hours and the houses until the end of life. The text makes this clear with an example: suppose someone was born on Sunday at the fourth hour. Since the fourth hour <note>original: "hora quarta"</note> of Sunday is the hour of the Moon, the Moon will be the lady of the first year, and from her, the state of the body is derived. The following hour is Saturn's; therefore, Saturn will be the lord of the second year, and from him, the state of substance in that year is derived, and so on in order.

## And from this, the arrangement of the Firdaria.

<margin>In this chapter, the author teaches how to arrange the years of the **firdaria** <term>firdaria: a Persian system of planetary periods that dictates which planet governs a specific span of years in a person's life</term> and the governance of the planets in nativities. The meaning of the text is as follows:</margin>
If the birth occurs during the day, the *firdaria* begins with the Sun. The Sun governs the person according to the quantity of the years of his *firdaria*, which are ten. After the Sun, Venus governs according to the quantity of the years of her *firdaria*, which are eight. After Venus, Mercury governs for 13 years; then the Moon for nine years; then Saturn for 11; then Jupiter for 12 years; then Mars for 7 years. Then the **Head of the Dragon** <note>The North Node of the Moon</note> governs for three years; then the **Tail of the Dragon** <note>The South Node of the Moon</note> for two years. After this, the arrangement returns to the Sun and proceeds through the order of the planets as before. The total number of all these years is 75. For whoever survives past this number of years, the cycle returns to the first arrangement as far as is possible. 

However, if the birth is at night, the *firdaria* begins with the Moon, and the Moon arranges her years. Then Saturn, then Jupiter, then Mars, then the Sun, then Venus, and afterwards Mercury. Then it returns to the Moon and follows the order of the planets as before. 

When any planet governs the years of its *firdaria*, it governs the first seventh part of those years alone. In the second seventh part, the planet that follows it in order participates with it. In the third part, the third planet participates, in the fourth the fourth, and in the fifth the fifth, and so on through the seven parts and the seven planets. 

For example: suppose a person was born during the day. The *firdaria* begins with the Sun, and the Sun governs him for the duration of his *firdaria* years, which are 10 years. The Sun alone governs the first seventh part of these ten years—specifically for one solar year, 156 days, and 12 hours <note>This is the result of 10 divided by 7</note>. This is indeed the seventh part of ten years. In the second seventh part of these ten years, Venus had participation with the Sun in the *firdaria* arrangement for one year, 156 days, and 12 hours. At that point, two years and 313 full days have passed for the newborn. Then Mercury had participation with the Sun in the arrangement of the *firdaria* for one year, 156 days, and 12 hours; and at the end of this...

<summary>This page explains two systems for timing life events: assigning planets to individual years based on the birth hour, and the "Firdaria" system of longer planetary periods (e.g., the Sun ruling for 10 years).</summary>
<keywords>firdaria, planetary periods, lord of the hour, nativity, astrology, lunar nodes, decans, triplicity, time-lords</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for calculating astrological time-periods.
