...of the vision, four solar years, 104 days, and 6 hours have passed for the **native** <term>native: the person for whom a birth chart is cast</term>. Then the Moon had participation with the Sun through that same seventh part of 10 years; and at the end of this division, 5 years, 260 days, and 18 hours have passed for the native. Then Saturn had participation with the Sun through a seventh part of 10 years; and at the end of this division, seven full years and 52 days have passed for the native. Then Jupiter had participation with the Sun in the arrangement of the **firdaria** <term>firdaria: a system of planetary periods used to predict different stages of a person's life</term> through the same seventh part; and at the end of this division, 8 solar years, 208 days, and 12 hours have passed for the native. Then Mars had participation with the Sun in the arrangement of the firdaria in the last seventh part of the ten years; and at the end of this division, 10 full years have passed for the native.

According to this method, it is to be done in the firdaria of all the planets. However, the **Head of the Dragon** <note>The North Node of the Moon</note> and the **Tail** <note>The South Node of the Moon</note> each arrange the years of their firdaria by themselves, without the participation of the [other] planets. To narrate the events happening to the native in each of these divisions would be too long. But **Haly Abenragel** <note>A famous 11th-century Arab astrologer, known for his "Great Book" on judgments of the stars</note>, in his great book, has fully set down all the divisions and the judgments of every division. Therefore, let those who want these judgments read that book.

# And From This, the Elevation

<margin>In this chapter, he teaches how one planet is elevated above another.</margin>
His intention is that the planet which was higher in its small circle—that is, in the **epicycle** <term>epicycle: in ancient astronomy, a small circle that a planet moves along, which itself moves along a larger circle around the Earth</term>—is said to be "elevated" over another by the elevation spoken of here. And this is what **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century scholar whose work defined medieval astronomy</note> says in the 63rd proposition of the *Centiloquium*. For he says that in a **conjunction** <term>conjunction: when two planets meet at the same point in the zodiac</term> of Saturn and Jupiter at the same minute, one must look to the elevation of one over the other and judge according to the strength of its nature in this world. And do similarly in the 21 remaining conjunctions.

**Haly** <note>Haly Abenragel</note> says there in his commentary: the elevation of a planet over another planet is its distance from the **apogee** <term>apogee: the point in a planet's orbit where it is farthest from the Earth</term> of its small circle, and that planet is said to be elevated over the other. The author, however, in the text, commands us to look at the **mean position** <term>mean position: where a planet would be if it moved at a constant speed</term> and the **true position** <term>true position: the planet's actual observed location</term> of the planet. If the true position of the planet is less than the mean motion, the planet is "descending" from the upper part of the epicycle toward the lower part. If, however, the true position of the planet is greater than the mean motion, the planet is "ascending" from the lower part of the epicycle toward the upper part.

To speak briefly, if the **equated argument** <term>equated argument: a mathematical value used to find a planet's specific position on its epicycle</term> of the planet is less than six common signs, it is descending; if it is more, it is ascending. Then the author commands to subtract the mean motion of the planet from its true motion, if the mean motion is less than the true—or vice versa if it is greater—namely, so that the smaller is subtracted from the larger; and he commands to multiply the remainder by seven. Then he commands to divide the resulting number by 22, and to take the quotient as the "elevation" when it ascends, or for the "depression" when it descends. 

Regarding Venus and Mercury, he commands us to look at the difference between the location of each of them and the location of the Sun, and to do just as with the others. The method previously mentioned according to the opinion of Ptolemy seems better and more certain to me. Then the author says that the strongest significance of the planets occurs when one of them is elevated above the other in a conjunction. However, in an **opposition** <term>opposition: when two planets are directly across from each other, 180 degrees apart</term> and in the **fourth aspect** <note>A square aspect, or 90 degrees apart</note>, the significance will be less apparent. And if one planet is ascending and the other is descending, the one that is ascending goes above the descending one. And if both are ascending, the one that has the greater ascent goes above the other. And if both are descending, the one that has the lesser [descent goes above the other].

<summary>This page concludes the specific timing calculations for the "firdaria" system and introduces the astronomical concept of planetary elevation. It explains how a planet's position on its epicycle relative to its mean motion determines its strength and hierarchical "height" during planetary alignments.</summary>
<keywords>firdaria, planetary periods, sub-periods, Haly Abenragel, Ptolemy, Centiloquium, elevation, epicycle, mean motion, true motion, conjunction, apogee, equated argument</keywords>

--- Page 149 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of planetary movement, shifting from the "firdaria" (planetary periods) to the physical and astrological positioning of planets in relation to one another, specifically focusing on "elevation" and the weather-related "opening of the gates."</meta>

...of descent. He also says, in another way, that a planet is "elevated" over another planet in terms of its **latitude** <term>latitude: the distance of a planet north or south of the ecliptic (the Sun's apparent path)</term>, because the northern planet moves above the **meridian** <term>meridian: the great circle passing through the celestial poles and the zenith of a particular location</term>. And if both planets are northern, the one that is further north moves above the one that is less northern. If, however, both are southern, the one with the lesser latitude <note>original: "minoris latitudinis"</note> moves above the one with the greater latitude. If any of the planets is on the **ecliptic** <term>ecliptic: the Sun's path, where a planet has zero latitude</term> and thus lacks latitude, then the northern planet moves above it, and the one lacking latitude moves above the meridian.

# And from this, the "Opening of the Gates."

<margin>Gaspar says</margin>
<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'A' featuring a scholar seated at a desk with an open book.</image-desc>
An **Opening of the Gates** <term>apertio portarum: an astrological configuration believed to signal significant changes in weather, such as rain or wind</term> is said to occur when a lower planet joins a higher one, provided that their **houses** <term>houses: the zodiac signs which a specific planet "rules" or naturally inhabits</term> are opposite one another. For example: when the Moon joins with Saturn or aspects <note>looks at</note> him, that is an "opening of the gates." Similarly, it occurs when the Sun joins Saturn; for the houses of the Sun and Moon <note>Leo and Cancer</note> are opposite the houses of Saturn <note>Aquarius and Capricorn</note>. Likewise, an opening of the gates happens when Mercury joins Jupiter, because the houses of both are opposite each other <note>Gemini/Virgo vs. Sagittarius/Pisces</note>. Similarly, when Venus joins Mars, it is an opening of the gates because their houses are opposite <note>Taurus/Libra vs. Aries/Scorpio</note>. **John of Seville** <note>original: "hyspalensis"; a prolific 12th-century translator of Arabic astrological texts into Latin</note> says that Venus signifies humidity; Mercury signifies winds; Saturn signifies mists and cold; Mars signifies winds from the right (that is, from the south) and heat; Jupiter signifies temperate weather and winds from the left (that is, from the north). The same John of Seville says that when the Moon separates from a conjunction or aspect with one planet and joins another, and the houses of those planets are opposite, this is an "opening of the great floodgates" <note>original: "apertio magnarun ualuarum"</note> in which winds or rains must necessarily come, according to the nature of those planets. For example: when the Moon, in separating from Venus, joins Mars; or separating from Mercury, joins Jupiter; or separating from the Sun, joins Saturn: this is an opening of the great floodgates.

# And from this, the *Albutez*.

<margin>Here he speaks of fortunate and unfortunate hours. He says that the Indians <note>ancient Indian astrologers</note> found 12 hours after the conjunction <note>New Moon</note> of the Sun and Moon and gave them to the Sun. They divide these into three parts and judge the first four hours according to the judgment of the first **lord of the triplicity** <term>triplicity: a group of three zodiac signs belonging to the same element (Fire, Earth, Air, or Water)</term> of the Sun's position. They judge the second four hours according to the second lord of the triplicity, and the final four according to the third lord of the triplicity at the hour of conjunction. Afterward, they give the next 12 hours to Venus and divide them similarly into three parts, judging each four-hour block by the lords of Venus's triplicity at the hour of conjunction. They do the same with Mercury and the Moon and the other planets according to the **Chaldean order** <note>the traditional sequence of planets: Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, Sun, Venus, Mercury, Moon</note> until the cycle returns to the Sun after 84 hours; then they receive from the Sun again in the same order. They continue this until the next conjunction. Some say that after the conjunction there are 12 hours called **combust** <term>combust: literally "burnt," referring to a planet or time period weakened by the Sun's power; here meaning "unlucky"</term> in which it is not good to begin any work. After these 12 "burnt" hours, there are 72 hours "un-burnt" in which starting work is useful. After these 72, there are again 12 "burnt" hours, then 72 "un-burnt," then 12 "burnt," and so on until the next conjunction. To put it briefly, all 12-hour blocks given to the Sun in the planetary order are "burnt," and those of other planets are "un-burnt." Then the author says they divide these 12 "burnt" hours into three divisions, and each division consists of four hours. Some say that if someone begins a war in the first four "burnt" hours, it is to be feared...</margin>

<summary>This page explains "planetary elevation" based on latitude and the "Opening of the Gates," a system for predicting weather based on planets entering opposite houses. It also introduces the "Albutez," an 84-hour cycle of planetary hours used to determine lucky and unlucky times for starting new tasks.</summary>
<keywords>astrological latitude, Opening of the Gates, John of Seville, weather prediction, Albutez, planetary hours, combust hours, Indian astrology, triplicity lords</keywords>

--- Page 150 ---
<meta>This page concludes the fourth "distinction" of the manual, which discusses the results of wars based on planetary hours, and begins the fifth "distinction," which shifts focus to the calculation and meaning of the "Arabic Parts" or "Lots" in astrology.</meta>

...the loss of his soul. The author does not mean the loss of the soul after this life—in the sense that after the soul is separated from the body, devils might seize it and lead it to hell. Rather, he means the loss of the soul as the **loss of the present life** <note>original: "amissionem vite presentis"</note>; and this is how it is understood in all sections concerning astrological judgments. For it belongs to the astrologers to speak of this kind of loss, whereas the first kind of loss <note>eternal damnation</note> belongs to the theologians. 

And he who begins a war in the first four hours must fear harm to his body—that is, without the loss of his life—but he who begins a war in the last four hours must fear the loss of all he possesses, and the loss of those who belong to him is also feared. And thus is completed the exposition of the fourth distinction of **Alchabitius's** <note>Al-Qabisi, a 10th-century Arab astrologer whose work was a standard textbook in medieval Europe</note> *Introduction to the Mastery of the Judgments of the Stars*.

---

# Fifth Distinction on the commemoration 
# of all the "Parts."

<image-desc>A large decorative initial 'D' containing floral motifs within a square frame.</image-desc>
This distinction is the fifth distinction of this book, in which the author teaches the **projection of parts** <term>parts: also known as Arabic Parts or Lots, these are mathematical points in a horoscope calculated by measuring the distance between two planets and adding it to the Ascendant</term>. It can be divided into two sections: in the first section, he lists the parts that occur in **nativities** <term>nativities: birth charts for individuals</term>. In the second section, he lists the parts that occur in the **revolutions of the years of the world** <term>revolutions of the years of the world: a branch of astrology that predicts global events, like war or harvest, based on the Sun's movement each year</term>. The second section begins where it says: "and because with God’s help." 

The first section is further divided into two parts: in the first, he gives the method for the projection of parts in general and provides an example using the **Part of Fortune** <term>Part of Fortune: the most important "Lot," representing well-being, luck, and worldly success</term>. In the second part, he specifically lists the parts associated with the twelve **houses** <term>houses: twelve divisions of the sky that represent different areas of life, like family, health, or career</term>. The second part begins where it says: "Let us begin therefore after the Part of Fortune." 

the meaning of the first part is as follows: The method for calculating the parts is to take the equal degrees that exist between one place and another, and that number is projected from the **Ascendant** <term>Ascendant: the sign and degree rising on the eastern horizon at the moment of birth</term> or another specified place. Alternatively, the degrees between the two places are added to the degrees that were at the beginning of the sign of the Ascendant, and the total is calculated from the beginning of that sign, giving each sign 30 degrees. Wherever that number ends, there lies the Part of Fortune. 

For example: suppose the Sun is at the end of the 6th degree of Pisces, and the Moon is at the end of the 14th degree of Gemini, and the degree of the Ascendant is the 10th degree of Aries. I will subtract the Sun’s true position from the Moon’s true position, and 98 degrees remain. To those, I will add the 10 degrees from the beginning of Aries to the degree of the Ascendant, resulting in 108 degrees. I will then begin to project this from the beginning of Aries, giving each sign 30 degrees; this number ends at the 18th degree of Cancer. Therefore, the Part of Fortune is at that point in this example.

# Let us begin therefore.
In this section, he lists the projection of the parts according to the order of the twelve houses, placing in each house the parts pertaining to it. This can be divided into as many sections as there are houses. First, he lists the parts of the first house, of which there are six. 

1. The **Part of Life**. 
2. The **Part of Hylech** <term>Hylech: the planet or point that acts as the "giver of life" or "releaser," used to determine a person's lifespan</term>. 
3. The **Part of Future Things** (or the **Part of Divination**). 
4. The **Part of Love and Concord**. 
5. The **Part of Stability and Duration**. 
6. The **Part of Boldness and Audacity**. 

He says in the text: when you wish to find the Part of Life, take the degrees from Jupiter to Saturn by day; by night, take the degrees from Saturn to Jupiter, and project them from the Ascendant. **Albumazar** <note>Abu Ma'shar, a highly influential Persian astrologer and scholar (787–886 AD)</note> says in his *Greater Introduction* that this part signifies natural life, the state of the body, and one's livelihood; if it is in a good state, it signifies a long life, bodily health, and joy...

<image-desc>A hand-drawn manicule (pointing hand) in the bottom-left margin pointing towards the start of the final paragraph.</image-desc>

<summary>This page concludes the discussion on planetary hours and war, transitioning into a detailed explanation of "Arabic Parts." --- Page 151 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Arabic Parts" or "Lots" within the fifth distinction. The previous page introduced the concept of projecting these mathematical points from the Ascendant to determine specific life outcomes. We are currently finishing the description of the "Part of Hylech" and "Part of the Future" before moving through the parts associated with the Second and Third Houses.</meta>

...the health of the soul. If it [the Part] is hindered, it signifies a shortness of life and a multitude of illnesses, as well as grief and sadness of the soul. Next, the author says the **Part of Hylech** <term>Hylech: from the Persian word for "giver of life," this is the point in a birth chart used to determine a person's lifespan</term> is taken from the degree of the **conjunction** <note>original: "coniunctionis"; referring to a New Moon</note> that preceded the birth if the birth occurred during a conjunctional phase, or from the degree of the **prevention** <note>original: "puentiōis" / "preventionis"; referring to the opposition of the sun and moon, or a Full Moon</note> if the birth occurred during a preventional phase, up to the degree of the moon; and it is projected from the **ascendant** <term>ascendant: the sign of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon at the moment of birth</term> of the birth, and where the count ends, there is the Part of Hylech. **Albumazar** <note>Abu Ma'shar (787–886 AD), a highly influential Persian astrologer in the medieval West</note> says that this part is **directed** <term>directed: "direction" is a predictive technique where points in the chart are moved forward in time to determine when major life events will occur</term> just as the hylech itself is directed by degrees, and its **perfection** <term>perfection: a technique where the chart is "advanced" by one sign per year</term> is led through the signs just as is done with the hylech. Whenever its direction or perfection reaches the malevolent [planets or points], it signifies hindrance and danger. 

And there were many wise astrologers who, when they found a man suffering the greatest dangers at certain times and did not find that his hylech had reached the places of the malevolents at that same time, nor found that same hindrance clearly signified in the **revolution** <term>revolution: the "Solar Return" chart cast for a person's birthday each year</term> of that same year, they did not know the cause of this thing. And therefore it was hidden from them: because they did not direct this part; for if they had directed it, they would have found this evil happening at the same time when this part reached certain places signifying that danger. The **Part of the Future** <note>original: "Pars futuroꝝ" / "Pars futurorum"</note> is taken by day from the Moon to the Sun, and by night the opposite, and is projected from the ascendant. According to Albumazar, the property of this part is to signify the state of the soul and the body. It signifies faith and prophecy, one’s region and the worship of God, secrets, thoughts, and intentions, as well as hidden and concealed things. And everything that is absent from things you shall apply in this way; the indications in others, and from what place to what place each is taken, and from what place it is projected, is clear in the text.

## Second house.

<margin>Here he places the parts of the second house and there are three. The first is the **part of substance**.</margin>
<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'S' featuring floral and scrolling vine motifs within a square border.</image-desc>
The second is the **part of poverty** and the smallness of wit. The third is the **part of happiness, triumph, and victory**. Albumazar says that the **part of substance** <note>original: "pars substātie"</note> signifies gain, livelihood, and the food by which bodies are sustained; if it is in a good place, it signifies a good state in substance, food, and livelihood. And if it is hindered, it signifies an evil state in these things we have mentioned. Other species of fortune appearing from substance—namely that which is treasured, kept, and acquired—are signified by the other indicators of substance and the **Part of Fortune** <term>Part of Fortune: one of the most important Arabic Parts, calculated from the Sun, Moon, and Ascendant, signifying material success and well-being</term>.

## Third house.

<margin>Here he places the parts of the third house and there are two. The first is the **part of brothers**. The second is...</margin>
<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'T' featuring floral and foliage patterns within a square border.</image-desc>
...the **part of the benevolence of brothers**. Albumazar says that the part of brothers and its **lord** <term>lord: the planet that "rules" the zodiac sign in which a point or house falls</term>—that is, the lord of the sign in which it falls—signifies the state of brothers, their concord, love, travels, and absence. After this, look to see if this part and its lord fall in signs of many children; then brothers will be multiplied. And if they fall in signs of few children, they will be few. And if you wish to know their number, take the number of signs that were between the part and the lord of the house in which it fell, or how many were between the lord himself and the part, and assign one to each sign. And if there is a **common sign** <term>common sign: also known as a "mutable" sign (Gemini, Virgo, Sagittarius, Pisces), which were thought to represent duality or doubling</term> between them, double the count of that sign. And if there is any planet between the part itself and the lord of the house in which it fell, take one for it also. Albumazar also places one part in this house which is called the **part of the death of brothers**: and he says that it is taken by day from the Sun to the degree of the **Midheaven** <note>original: "medij cęli"</note> and by night the opposite. And the degrees of the ascendant are added to it, and it is projected from the ascendant. And whenever this part comes...

...while by **directing** <term>directing: a predictive technique where points in the chart are moved forward in time to determine when major life events will occur</term> toward the **significators** <term>significator: a planet or point in a chart that represents a specific person or area of life</term> of brothers and sisters, giving one year to each degree, or through **perfection** <term>perfection: also known as profection; a technique where the chart is "advanced" by one sign per year</term> giving one year to each sign, something horrible will happen to the brothers and sisters, or one of them will die.

# The Fourth House
<margin>Here he places the parts of the fourth house, and there are six. The first is the part of fathers.</margin>
<image-desc>Decorative initial Q with floral motifs inside a square border.</image-desc>
The second is the part of the death of the father. The third is the part of years. The fourth is the part of inheritances and possessions. The fifth is the part of the nobility of the person born—specifically, whether he is truly the son of the one to whom he is attributed or not. The sixth is the part of the "end of things." **Albumazar** <note>Abu Ma'shar (787–886 AD), a preeminent Persian astrologer whose work was central to medieval European science</note> says that the part of the father signifies the being of the father and his nobility and lineage; if this part is in a good state, the father will be noble. And if the **lord** <term>lord: the planet that rules the zodiac sign where a specific Part or "house" is located</term> of the house of this part is in a good state, the father will be fortunate. But if it is in a bad state, he will be hard-pressed. From the second part of this house, which is the part of the father's death, the cause of death is determined: and whenever the perfection of the years reaches this part or its lord, it signifies danger to the father, and similarly when either of them reaches the significators of the father.

# The Fifth House
<margin>Here he places the parts of the fifth house, and there are seven. The first is the part of children.</margin>
<image-desc>Decorative initial Q with floral motifs inside a square border.</image-desc>
The second is the part of the time in which children ought to be had. The third is the part through which masculinity or femininity is known. The fourth is the part through which it is known regarding the person born <note>referring to a child for whom a specific inquiry is being made</note> whether it is male or female. The fifth is the part at which, when **Jupiter** <note>traditionally the "Great Benefic" planet associated with growth and children</note> arrives, there is a sign of a son. The sixth is the part of the love of children. The seventh is the part of the knowledge of children. Albumazar says: if the part of children falls in a **sign of many children** <note>certain zodiac signs, like Water signs, were traditionally considered more "fertile" than others</note> he will have many children. But if it falls in a sterile sign, he will not have any child. But if it falls in a sign of few children, he will have few children. And if this part signifies children and is **fortunate** <note>meaning well-placed or supported by helpful planets</note>, the children will live; and if unfortunate, they will die.

# The Sixth House
<margin>Here he places the parts of the sixth house, and there are two. The first is the part of azemena, that is, of debility</margin>
<image-desc>Decorative initial S with foliate motifs inside a square border.</image-desc>
that is inseparable from some limb. The second is the part of servants. If the **azemena** <term>azemena: from the Arabic "al-zamana," referring to permanent physical disabilities or chronic infirmities</term> part is conjoined with the **fortunes** <note>the beneficial planets, Jupiter and Venus</note>, the person born will be healthy in his limbs. But if it is joined with **infortunes** <note>the challenging planets, Mars and Saturn</note>, he will have inseparable diseases in his limbs; and the locations where these sicknesses will occur are known from the locations of the infortunes that signify them.

# The Seventh House
<margin>Here he places the parts of the seventh house, and there are four. The first is</margin>
<image-desc>Decorative initial S with foliate motifs inside a square border.</image-desc>
the part of the betrothal of men. The second is the part of the betrothal of women, and this is determined in two ways. The third is of delight and pleasure. The fourth is the part of weddings. When the part of betrothal arrives through direction at Jupiter or **Venus** <note>the planet most associated with love and marriage</note>, it is a sign of betrothal. And I found in one **nativity** <term>nativity: a birth chart or the moment of birth itself</term> the time of betrothal occurred when the lord of the part of betrothal reached the part itself through direction, giving one year to each degree.

# The Ninth House

<meta>This page continues the discussion of "Arabic Parts" or "Lots," shifting focus from individual life events to mundane astrology—the study of world cycles, kingdoms, and the rise and fall of kings. It specifically addresses how to calculate the timing and duration of a ruler's reign.</meta>

...where the conjunction is, up to the place in which the planets are joined. And unless it is understood in this way, it is impossible. For it is supposed that a man knows the degree of the **Ascendant** <term>Ascendant: the zodiac sign rising on the eastern horizon at the moment of birth or an event</term> at the hour of the conjunction of the **superior planets** <note>The "superior planets" in medieval astrology are the three furthest from Earth in the geocentric model: Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note>. This, however, is impossible according to what Abraham Ibn Ezra says; for he says, "if I could know the day on which they ought to be joined, this would be a great thing." <note>Abraham Ibn Ezra (c. 1089–1167) was a renowned Jewish scholar; his remark highlights how difficult it was to calculate precise planetary positions with the astronomical tables of the time.</note> And there is a Part calculated in another way: namely, it is taken from the degree of the **Midheaven** <term>Midheaven: the highest point in the sky at a given moment, representing career and status</term> of the **revolution** <term>revolution: a chart cast for the moment the Sun returns to a specific point, often the start of a year, to predict events for that period</term> of that year in which the conjunction occurred, to the Sun, and it is **projected** <term>projected: in calculating "Parts," the distance between two points is measured and then added to the position of a third point to find the result</term> from the degree of Jupiter. 

The Second Part, by which it is known how long a king ought to last in his kingdom: here, the hour of the king's **election** <term>election: the practice of choosing a specific moment to begin an action, like a coronation, to ensure success</term> is taken from the Sun at the 15th degree of the sign of Leo and projected from the Moon. It is also taken from the Moon at the 15th degree of Cancer and projected from the Sun. 

The Third Part concerns the time of the king’s election: here, the hour of the king’s election is taken during the day from Jupiter to Saturn, and during the night the opposite <note>original: "e contra"</note>, and projected from the Ascendant of the revolution of the year in which the king rose to power. And if Jupiter is in a **common sign** <term>common sign: also called "mutable" signs (Gemini, Virgo, Sagittarius, or Pisces), which represent transition</term> and the revolution is by day, and with this Jupiter is **falling** <term>cadent: also called "falling," this refers to a planet in a position where its influence is considered weak or unstable</term> from an angle, then it is taken from Saturn to Jupiter and 30 degrees are added on top, and it is projected from the Ascendant. If indeed Jupiter and Saturn are **opposite** each other and both are falling from the Ascendant, half of the degrees between them are taken and projected from the Ascendant. And if Jupiter is in his **exaltation** <term>exaltation: a specific zodiac sign where a planet’s influence is thought to be at its strongest</term> and the revolution is at night, count from him to Saturn and project from the Ascendant.

Then the author says that there are two primary Parts from which the time of the king's election and his duration are extracted. The first of these is that you look at the hour of the king's election and look where the **profection** <term>profection: a predictive technique where the chart is "advanced" by one sign per year to see which themes will be active</term> of the year reaches from the **conjunction of the triplicity** <note>A major astrological alignment occurring roughly every 240 years when Jupiter and Saturn meet in a new element (Fire, Earth, Air, Water), signaling a change in eras or dynasties.</note> which signified that "sect" in which that kingdom exists, according to the rule that gives one year for every 30 degrees and one month for every two and a half degrees; and so by this proportion up to the hour of the election. And when you know in which degree of the sign it is, keep that degree; for from it you will **equate** <term>equate: to calculate the precise mathematical position of a point in a horoscope</term> the first Part. And when you wish to equate it, you will adjust the Ascendant of the revolution of the year in which the chosen one himself rose. Afterward, look at Saturn and Jupiter, and whichever of them is **oriental** <term>oriental: rising in the east before the Sun in the morning</term> to the Sun, take from that planet to the degree of the equation of the first Part which you kept, and project from the Ascendant of the revolution; and where the number reaches, that is the location of the first Part.

¶ Note that the author says "take from the planet oriental to the Sun, whether Saturn or Jupiter," and he does not say what should be done when both are oriental. I believe that then it should be taken from the one who is closer to the Sun. Likewise, he does not say what should be done when neither of them is oriental; wherefore I will say briefly that I do not know what should be done, because I have not seen these two Parts placed in this manner in any other book. 

The Second Part is known in this way: Look at the conjunction of Jupiter and Saturn in which the king rose or began his kingdom, and to what sign and degree the profection of the year reaches—giving 30 degrees to each year and 2 and a half degrees to each month—up to the day on which the king rose; and that itself is the location of the equation of the second Part. After this, take from Saturn or Jupiter, whichever of them is oriental, to the location of the second Part, and project from the Ascendant of the year's revolution; and where the number reaches, that itself is the location of the second Part. These are the Parts that signify the strength of the king and his duration. 

Haly Abenragel <note>Ali ibn Abi al-Rijal (died after 1037), a famous Arab astrologer whose work was widely translated into Latin.</note> says: You shall look into the revolution of the years of the world for the **enthronement** <term>enthronement: the coronation or formal start of a ruler's reign</term> of the one enthroned. First from Saturn, which is the first Part, and from Jupiter, which is the second. And how many degrees and how many minutes there were between them; note this. 

¶ Afterward, consider in which sign the **lord of the Midheaven** <term>lord: the planet that rules the zodiac sign located at the Midheaven</term> is, and reduce those degrees to the **ascensions** <term>ascension: the time it takes for a zodiac sign to rise over the horizon, used by astrologers to convert degrees of space into lengths of time</term> of that sign in which it was; and what comes out from there will be the duration, whether years or months or days. And if the planet is in its own **houses** <term>house: the zodiac sign(s) traditionally ruled by a planet, where it feels "at home"</term> or in its exaltations, they will be years; and if they are **pilgrims** <term>pilgrim: a planet in a sign where it has no special dignity or strength, like a traveler in a foreign land</term> in **succeedent** houses <term>succeedent: the four houses (2nd, 5th, 8th, 11th) that follow the strongest corners of the chart, considered moderately powerful</term>, they will be months; otherwise, they will be days. Similarly, when the Sun **applies** <term>application: a planet moving toward a geometric aspect with another planet</term> to Mars, and Mars is **void of course** <term>void of course: a planet that makes no further major aspects before leaving its current sign, often signifying a lack of results</term>, not applying to Saturn, it signifies that the soldiers will move against the enthroned one himself and will come upon him with weapons raised. And for this reason...

<summary>This page details the complex mathematical calculations for the "Arabic Parts" used to predict the stability and length of a king's reign. It cites authorities like Abraham Ibn Ezra and Haly Abenragel to explain how planetary movements at the time of a coronation or a "Great Conjunction" dictate political outcomes.</summary>

<keywords>mundane astrology, Arabic Parts, Great Conjunction, Abraham Ibn Ezra, Haly Abenragel, kingship, coronation, profection, planetary revolution, superior planets, duration of reign</keywords>

--- Page 155 ---
<meta>This page continues a technical discussion on the "Arabic Parts" (or Lots), moving from the personal fate of kings to "mundane" astrology, which predicts economic conditions like the price of commodities and the abundance of harvests.</meta>

...give him sorrows and anxieties, and perhaps [these] will seize him. However, if Mars applies to Saturn with this, their natures are joined, and no one will move against him.

# Likewise, there are other Parts.

<margin>Here the author presents the Parts that are used in the "revolutions of years" <term>revolutions of years: charts cast for the moment the Sun enters Aries, used to predict the year's events</term>.</margin>

...through which you will know which things will be expensive and which will be cheap. The meaning of the text is this: When you wish to know whether some thing will be very expensive or cheap, or only slightly so, consider the Part of that thing and see into which planet's **house**, **exaltation**, **term**, or **triplicity** <note>These are the five "essential dignities," or layers of influence a planet has over a specific degree of the zodiac.</note> that Part falls. If the planet [ruling that spot] is **retrograde** <term>retrograde: when a planet appears to move backward in the sky, usually signaling weakness or delays</term> or in some malicious place, that thing becomes cheap and will be of little value. But if it is in a place of strength or in an **angle** <term>angle: the four most powerful houses in an astrological chart (1st, 4th, 7th, and 10th)</term>—and especially in the **Midheaven**—the price of that thing will be "heavy" and it will be of greater value. 

And if the lord of that house reaches the place of its "descent" <note>original: "descensionis"; this refers to a planet's "fall," the sign opposite its exaltation where it is weakest.</note>, that thing becomes cheap. He means to say: if a planet having several "strengths" (fortitudes) in the location of the Part is in another of its dignities—and especially in its exaltation, or in one of the angles, especially the Midheaven—that thing will be expensive. And according to the amount of the planet's strength in its dignities, or in the angles or **succedent** <term>succedent: the houses (2nd, 5th, 8th, 11th) that follow the powerful "angles"</term> houses, such will be the amount of the scarcity <note>original: "caristie"; in medieval Latin, this refers to high prices or dearness caused by scarcity.</note>. 

But if the planet itself is in its **fall** from its house or exaltation, or is in a **cadent** house <term>cadent: houses (3rd, 6th, 9th, 12th) that "fall away" from the angles, considered the weakest positions</term>, that thing becomes cheap and will be of little value. Look similarly at the **aspect** <term>aspect: the geometric angle between two points in the sky, like a square or a trine</term> of the **fortunes** and **misfortunes** <note>In medieval astrology, "fortunes" (benefics) are Jupiter and Venus, while "misfortunes" (malefics) are Saturn and Mars.</note> toward that Part, and toward the Moon and its lord—how it is situated and how it looks upon that Part. If a fortune and the Moon look upon the Part, the price of that same thing will be multiplied. But if the misfortunes look upon it, that thing will suffer detriment and will be cheap and of little value. 

I tell you that if a planet having several strengths in the place of the Part is retrograde in the revolution of the year, and is also in its other dignities or in an angle, it signifies that the thing will be expensive—but not at the beginning of the year as long as the lord of the Part is retrograde. When that planet begins to move **direct** <term>direct: moving forward through the zodiac again after a retrograde period</term>, then that thing will begin to be expensive. In the same way, if the planet that is lord of the Part is in its fall and is direct, it signifies the cheapness of the thing, but that cheapness will be greatest when the planet begins to be retrograde. 

Next, he presents the **profections** <note>A timing technique where a point moves symbolically through the signs of the zodiac at a rate of one sign per year or month.</note> and begins with the **Part of Wheat**. He says that the Part of Wheat is calculated from the Sun to Mars and added to the Ascendant. From which places they are taken and to which they are joined is clear in the text. And so that the method may be clearer, I provide an example of the Part of Wheat in the chart of the conjunction preceding the Sun's entry into Aries, and also in the chart of the revolution for this year—namely, 1331. 

I say that for Paris, according to the truth of the **Alfonsine Tables** <note>Famous astronomical tables compiled in Toledo in the 13th century.</note>, at the hour of the conjunction of the Sun and Moon preceding the Sun's entry into Aries, the Ascendant will be the first degree of Cancer. The place of the Sun at that hour will be 5 signs, 56 degrees, 42 minutes, 36 seconds. The true place of Mars was 1 sign, 44 degrees, 17 minutes, 28 seconds. I will see how much distance is between the Sun and Mars by subtracting the Sun's position from the place of Mars, and I find 1 sign, 47 degrees, 34 minutes, 52 seconds. I will count this number from the Ascendant, and the number ends in Libra at 18 degrees, 10 minutes, 6 seconds. 

Then I will see who is strongest there, and I find **Saturn** is strongest in that place, because it is his exaltation, on account of which he has four strengths there. It is also his triplicity, for which reason he has three strengths. It is also his **face** <term>face: a 10-degree subdivision of a sign, providing a minor amount of dignity</term>, by reason of which he has one strength. Therefore, Saturn has 8 strengths in the place of the Part of Wheat. After Saturn is Venus, because it is her house, and she has 5 strengths there; and Jupiter has a **term** there. I will look at the state of each of these in the chart, and according to their state, I will look regarding the Lot of Wheat. First, I will look at Saturn—what state he has in the chart—and I find him in the **angle of the earth** <note>The "angle of the earth" (Imum Coeli) is the lowest point in the chart, the cusp of the 4th house.</note>, not distant from the angle by more than 4 degrees. This signifies that wheat ought to be expensive, though not excessively, because...

<summary>This page explains how to use planetary "Parts" or "Lots" to predict the market price and scarcity of goods, using wheat prices in 1331 Paris as a specific case study. It details how the dignity and movement (retrograde or direct) of a Part's ruling planet determines whether a commodity will be expensive or cheap.</summary>

<keywords>Arabic Parts, Mundane Astrology, Part of Wheat, Alfonsine Tables, Saturn, market prices, astrology calculations, 1331, Paris, essential dignities, retrograde</keywords>

--- Page 156 ---
<meta>This page concludes the astrological analysis of economic trends—specifically the price of wheat—and provides the formal colophon (publication details) of the volume, followed by a table of contents or index of catchwords for the entire work.</meta>

...the angle is not [equally] strong; for the strongest of the angles in this regard is the **Midheaven** <term>Midheaven: the 10th house, representing the highest point in the sky and the most powerful of the four angles</term>; next is the **Ascendant** <term>Ascendant: the 1st house, representing the eastern horizon</term>; after that the **Angle of the Earth** <term>Angle of the Earth: the 4th house, the lowest point in the sky</term>; and finally the **Western Angle** <term>Western Angle: the 7th house, representing the setting sun</term>. However, because **Saturn** is **retrograde** <term>retrograde: when a planet appears to move backward in the sky, usually signaling weakness or delays</term> and is a **peregrine** <term>peregrine: a planet in a position where it has no essential dignity, like a traveler in a foreign land with no influence</term> in its location, having no dignity there, it signifies that wheat will be of low price at the beginning of the year. But when Saturn begins to move **direct** <term>direct: when a planet resumes its forward motion after being retrograde</term>, its price will rise and it will become more expensive. This is the significance of Saturn. Next, I look to the state of **Venus**, and I find her in her own house (that is, her primary domicile). This similarly signifies that wheat will be somewhat expensive. I will also examine the same Part in the chart for the **revolution of the year** <term>revolution of the year: a chart cast for the moment the Sun enters Aries, used to predict the year's events</term> for that same year. I say that the **Ascendant** of the revolution, according to the aforementioned tables, will be the 4th degree, 15 minutes, and 26 seconds of **Libra**. The distance between the **Sun** and **Mars** will be—that is—1 sign, 45 degrees, 3 minutes, and 59 seconds <note>The text likely refers to the calculation of the "Part of Wheat," which involves measuring the distance between planets and projecting it from the Ascendant.</note>. I will calculate this number starting from the Ascendant, and the number ends in **Capricorn**, at 19 degrees, 19 minutes, and 25 seconds. **Mars** possesses **exaltation** and **triplicity** in the location of this Part; he therefore has seven "strengths" (fortitudes) there <note>In medieval astrology, "fortitudes" were points awarded for a planet's zodiacal dignity: Exaltation is worth 4 points and Triplicity is worth 3.</note>. **Saturn** has **house** and **face** <term>face: the weakest of the five essential dignities, also known as a decan</term> there; he therefore has six strengths there. Mars is stronger in that location, and since he is in the angle of the **Midheaven**, this signifies that wheat ought to be expensive; but because Mars is in his **fall** <term>fall: the sign opposite a planet's exaltation, representing a state of weakness or "descension"</term> from his exaltation, it will not be expensive until he has exited his fall.

¶ Here ends the writing on **Alchabitius** <note>original: "Alchabiciū"; Al-Qabisi was a 10th-century Arab astrologer whose work was a cornerstone of European astrological education.</note>, compiled by **John of Saxony** in the city of Paris in the year 1331. Corrected by the Doctor of Arts and Medicine, Master **Bartholomew of Alten** and Nusia. Printed in **Venice** by the brothers **Johannes and Gregorius de Gregoriis**, in the year of our Lord's birth 1492, on the 18th day of February.
